Mean Field Behavior during the Big Bang Regime for Coalescing Random Walks
Jonathan Hermon, Shuangping Li, Dong Yao, Lingfu Zhang

Abstract
In this paper, we consider coalescing random walks on a general connected graph 𝒢 = ( V , E ) \mathcal{G}=(V,E) . We set up a unified framework to study the leading order of the decay rate of P t P_{t} , the expectation of the fraction of occupied sites at time t t , particularly for the ‘Big Bang’ regime where t ≪t_coal:= E (inf{s: There is only one particle at time s }). Our results show that P t P_{t} satisfies certain ‘mean-field behaviors’ if the graphs satisfy certain ‘transience-like’ conditions. We apply this framework to two families of graphs: (1) graphs given by the configuration model whose degree distribution is supported in the interval [ 3 , d ¯ ] [3,\bar{d}] for some d ¯ ≥ 3 \bar{d}\geq 3 , and (2) finite and infinite transitive graphs. In the first case, we show that for 1 ≪ t ≪ | V | 1\ll t\ll\left|V\right| , P t P_{t} decays in the order of t − 1 t^{-1} , and ( t ​ P t ) − 1 (tP_{t})^{-1} is approximately the probability that two particles starting from the root of the corresponding unimodular Galton-Watson tree never collide after one of them leaves the root. The number ( t ​ P t ) − 1 (tP_{t})^{-1} is also roughly | V | / ( 2 ​ t meet ) |V|/(2t_{\text{meet}}) , where t meet t_{\text{meet}} is the mean meeting time of two independent walkers. By taking the local weak limit, we prove convergence of t ​ P t tP_{t} as t → ∞ t\to\infty for the corresponding unimodular Galton-Watson tree. For the second family of graphs, we consider a growing sequence of finite transitive graphs 𝒢 n = ( V n , E n ) \mathcal{G}_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n}) , satisfying that t meet = O ⁡ ( | V n | ) t_{\text{meet}}=O(\left|V_{n}\right|) and the inverse of the spectral gap t rel t_{\text{rel}} is o ⁡ ( | V n | ) o(\left|V_{n}\right|) . We show that t rel ≪ t ≪ t coal t_{\text{rel}}\ll t\ll t_{\text{coal}} , ( t ​ P t ) − 1 (tP_{t})^{-1} is approximately the probability that two random walks never meet before time t t , and it is also roughly | V | / ( 2 ​ t meet ) |V|/(2t_{\text{meet}}) . In addition, we define a natural ‘uniform transience’ condition, and show that in the transitive setup it implies the above estimates of t ​ P t tP_{t} for all 1 ≪ t ≪ t coal 1\ll t\ll t_{\text{coal}} . Estimates of t ​ P t tP_{t} are also obtained for all infinite transient transitive unimodular graphs, in particular, all transient transitive amenable graphs.

1
Introduction

We study
coalescing random walks
(
CRW
) on a connected graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, defined as follows.
Initially each vertex
v\in V
is occupied by one particle.
Each particle performs an independent continuous-time edge simple random walk (
SRW
); i.e., for each edge incidental to the current location of the particle, with rate
1
it jumps across that edge. Whenever two particles meet (or ‘collide’, i.e., one particle jumps to the current location of the other one), they merge into one particle which continues to perform a SRW. Whenever such a collision happens, the total number of particles in the system decreases by 1 if the graph is finite.

One can consider the more general case in which the jump rate depends on an edge. A
graphical representation
of the model is obtained by letting each directed edge
(x,y)
ring at times given by a Poisson process of rate
r_{x,y}
, independently for different directed edges. Whenever a particle is at
x
at time
t_{-}
and the directed edge
(x,y)
rings at time
t
, the particle moves to
y
at time
t
. It is easy to see that each particle follows a continuous-time Markov chain denoted by
(V,\mathbf{r})
, where
\mathbf{r}=\{r_{x,y}\}_{x,y\in V}
. Any two particles evolve independently until they collide; and once they collide, they will be at the same location at all later times (and thus they have effectively coalesced).

Unless otherwise stated, throughout this paper we assume that
V
is either finite or countable; and the Markov chain has symmetric jump rates, i.e.,
r_{x,y}=r_{y,x}
for all
x,y
.
This implies that the Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
is reversible w.r.t. the counting measure, and when
V
is finite, its stationary distribution
\pi
is the uniform distribution on
V
. We also assume
(V,\mathbf{r})
is irreducible, i.e., for any
x,y\in V
and any
t>0
, we have
p_{t}(x,y)>0
where
p_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the time-
t
transition probability corresponding to
\mathbf{r}
. In particular, when
(V,\mathbf{r})
is a SRW on a graph
\mathcal{G}
, we assume
\mathcal{G}
is connected.

This model has received a lot of attention in the literature.
For graphs such as
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{d}
, the asymptotic behaviors of the particle densities in CRW have been studied in many works, including
[
7
,
10
,
29
,
30
]
. Bounds on the particle densities and on the coalescence time (to be defined in (
1.1
) below) for general graphs or some other families of graphs have also been proven, for instance in
[
23
,
24
,
9
,
4
,
15
,
19
]
.
See also the references therein. Part of the motivation of studying the CRW model stems from its connection with the voter model via duality.
We refer interested reader to the survey
[
5
, Chapter 2 and Chapter 5]
for a detailed introduction of the CRW model and its mean field version Kingman’s coalescent, including many known results for CRW on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the duality between CRW and the voter model, and the decay rate
of
1/t
for the particle densities in Kingman’s coalescent. We will explain some of these results in the introduction.

We denote by
\xi_{t}
the set of vertices in
V
that are occupied at time
t
.
When
V
is finite, a quantity of interest is the
coalescence time

\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}:=\inf\{t:|\xi_{t}|=1\},

(1.1)

which is the first time when there is only one particle left.
By duality, the law of
\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}
is the same as that of the consensus time for the voter model (see Section
2.1
for details).

We are interested in the rate of decay (as
t\to\infty
) of the probability
P_{t}(x)
of some specific site
x
being occupied at time
t

P_{t}(x):=\mathbb{P}(x\in\xi_{t}).

In the finite setup, we can define the expected density
P_{t}
of particles at time
t

P_{t}:=\frac{\mathbb{E}|\xi_{t}|}{|V|}=\mathbb{E}P_{t}(\mathfrak{u}),\quad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{u}\sim\Unif(V)\text{ and is independent of the CRW}.

Under transitivity
1
1

1

We say that
(V,\mathbf{r})
is
transitive
, if for all
u,v\in V
there exists a permutation
\phi=\phi_{u,v}:V\to V
such that
\phi(u)=v
and
r_{\phi(x),\phi(y)}=r_{x,y}
for all
x,y\in V
.
When
(V,\mathbf{r})
is transitive,

P_{t}(x)
does not depend on
x
.
, we simply write
P_{t}
for
P_{t}(x)
, even when
|V|=\infty
. We also use the notation
P_{t}(\mathcal{G})
sometimes, when we consider a graph
\mathcal{G}
where each particle takes a SRW.
Clearly,

P_{t}
is a decreasing function of
t
, which vanishes in the infinite setup and converges to
1/|V|
in the finite setup as
t\to\infty
, whenever
(V,\mathbf{r})
is irreducible.

In the finite setup, loosely speaking, the evolution of CRW can be divided into two time periods. The
coalescing regime

t\asymp t_{\mathrm{coal}}:=\mathbb{E}\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}
during which there are typically
O(1)
particles; and the regime
t\ll t_{\mathrm{coal}}
, known as the
Big Bang Regime
, a term coined (in this context) by Rick Durrett, during which (typically) there are many particles and the model evolves rapidly (see, e.g.,
[
13
]
).

The aim of this work is to improve the understanding of the CRW model beyond the setup of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and in the finite setup to improve the understanding of the model during the big bang regime. The initial motivation of this work was to answer questions posed by Rick Durrett in an AMS workshop on Stochastic Spatial Models
(RI 2019), which we paraphrase here:

(i)

Prove results for the decay of
P_{t}
as
t\to\infty
, for CRW on random graphs generated by the configuration model or on Galton-Watson trees. In particular, since random walks on these graphs are transient, do we expect the density to decay in the order of
t^{-1}
, or even
\lim_{t\to\infty}tP_{t}=c
for some constant
c
?

(ii)

For the constant in the first question, can we identify it abstractly? Moreover, can we show that it is given by the probability that two independent walks starting at a random vertex and a random neighbor of that vertex (respectively) do not collide?

In this paper we go beyond random graphs and set up a unified framework to determine the decay rate of
P_{t}
for general
(V,\mathbf{r})
, up to smaller order terms, under certain ‘transience-like’ conditions (Proposition
5.2
).
For this we consider sequences of finite graphs
\mathcal{G}_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n})
and times
t_{n}
, restricted to
1\ll t_{n}\ll|V_{n}|
, as well as infinite graphs.
We apply our framework to the configuration model with minimal degree
3
and bounded maximal degree in Theorem
1.6
, or the corresponding unimodular Galton-Watson tree in Theorem
1.8
. In addition, in Theorems
1.9
-
1.12
we apply our framework to transitive Markov chains
(V,\mathbf{r})
which either are transient and unimodular (in the infinite setup), or satisfy certain finitary notions of transience (in the finite setup), as well as a certain spectral condition proposed by Aldous and Fill in this context
[
2
]
.

For finite graphs and Markov chains, the novelty of our results is that they are applicable to small values of
t
. For instance, for the configuration model and finite transitive graphs satisfying a ‘uniform transience’ condition (Definition
1.10
below), our results apply to the entirety of the big bang period (more precisely, for any
t\gg 1
).
Such exact asymptotic results for the rate of decay of
P_{t}
were previously known only for the
d
-dimensional integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
In a seminal work of Bramson and Griffeath
[
7
]
they showed that for the SRW on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have

P_{t}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi t}}(1\pm o(1)),&\hbox{ for $d=1$;}\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\log t}{t}(1\pm o(1)),&\hbox{ for $d=2$;}\\
\frac{1}{\psi_{d}t}(1\pm o(1)),&\hbox{ for $d\geq 3$.}\end{cases}

(1.2)

Here
\psi_{d}
is the probability that a simple random walk starting from the origin never returns to it and
o(1)
is some error term that vanishes as
t\to\infty
.

The starting point of our arguments are rough upper and lower bounds on the decay of
P_{t}(x)
(Theorem
3.1
), which also holds for general
(V,\mathbf{r})
(which need not satisfy any ‘transience-like’ assumptions).
When
(V,\mathbf{r})
is transitive, these bounds give the first characterization of
P_{t}
sharp up to a constant factor, as previously only non-matching upper and lower bounds were available (see Section
1.2.5
).

We then bootstrap such general bounds into bounds sharp up to smaller order terms. Our arguments are inspired by Bramson and Griffeath
[
7
]
. We determine the asymptotics of certain moments of a quantity
\hat{N}_{t}
, which has a natural interpretation in terms of both the voter model and CRW:
for the voter model it is the size of the set of vertices which have the same opinion as the origin at time
t
, while for CRW it is the number of particles which have the same location as the particle whose initial position is the origin at time
t
(in the sense of the graphical representation).
For
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such moments calculation is due to Sawyer; see

[
26
, Theorem 2.2]
. Alas Sawyer’s proof involves a ‘magical’ combinatorial calculation which relies heavily on the specific structure of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and hence does not seem to generalize to other graphs. While Bramson and Griffeath were able to use Sawyer’s theorem, which they described as ‘coming tantalizingly close to determining the asymptotics
of
P_{t}
’ as a ‘black box’, we on the other hand have to study these moments directly in terms of CRW. This is the most difficult part of our arguments. As a corollary, we generalize Sawyer’s theorem to many other graphs (see Theorems
1.14
and
1.15
), thus deducing that in those cases
\hat{N}_{t}/\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{t}
converges in distribution to a Gamma distribution.

We comment that determining the asymptotics of
P_{t}
(or
P_{t}(x)
in the non-transitive setup) actually gives one information about the distribution of the number of vertices occupied by particles in an arbitrary large finite set
A
, i.e.,
|\xi_{t}\cap A|
. Determining the expectation of a random variable is generally more interesting when the random variable is concentrated around the expectation, and this is the case for
|\xi_{t}\cap A|
when
\sum_{a\in A}P_{t}(a)\gg 1
. Indeed, Arratia
[
3
, Lemma 1]
showed that for all
x\neq y
,

\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbbm{1}[x\in\xi_{t}],\mathbbm{1}[y\in\xi_{t}])\leq 0.

Actually Arratia proved this when the underlying graph is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, but the proof works for general Markov chains.
It follows that
\mathrm{Var}(|\xi_{t}\cap A|)\leq\mathbb{E}|\xi_{t}\cap A|
. Thus if
A\subset V
satisfies that
\mathbb{E}|\xi_{t}\cap A|=\sum_{a\in A}P_{t}(a)\gg 1
, by Chebyshev’s inequality
|\xi_{t}\cap A|
has
O((\mathbb{E}|\xi_{t}\cap A|)^{1/2})
fluctuations around its expectation.

1.1
Statements of our results

To formally state our results we need some further notations and terminologies. We start by introducing the key notion of the
meeting time
.

Definition 1.1
.

Consider a Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
. For any two measures
\mu,\nu
on
V
, we write
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
for the law of a walk where its starting location is sampled from
\mu
, and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu}
for the joint law of two independent walks
X,Y:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow V
, where their starting locations are sampled from
X(0)\sim\mu
and
Y(0)\sim\nu
independently. When
\mu
is the Dirac measure on some
x\in V
, we also write
\mathbb{P}_{x}
for
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
and
\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu}
for
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu}
. Similarly, when
\mu
and
\nu
are the Dirac measures on some
x,y\in V
respectively we write
\mathbb{P}_{x,y}
for
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu}
.
We define the
meeting time
as the first collision time of the two independent walks
X
and
Y
:

\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}:=\inf\{t:X(t)=Y(t)\},

which is a random variable whose law depends on
\mu,\nu
. An important quantity in the study of CRW is
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r})=t_{\mathrm{meet}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\pi}\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}
, where
\pi
is the uniform distribution on
V
. We also write
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G})
when we consider a graph
\mathcal{G}
where each particle performs a SRW. By an abuse of terminology, we also refer to
t_{\mathrm{meet}}
as the meeting time.

Definition 1.2
.

For a Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
and any
x,y\in V
, we let
\nu_{x}(y):=\frac{r_{x,y}}{r(x)}
, where
r(x):=\sum_{y}r_{x,y}
is the total jump rate from
x
(by convention
r_{x,x}=0
).
For any
t\geq 0
we define

\alpha_{t}(x):=r(x)\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}>t).

(1.3)

Roughly speaking,
\alpha_{t}(x)
is the weighted probability that the two walkers, starting from
x
and a random neighbor of
x
respectively, don’t meet by time
t
.
We also define

\alpha_{\infty}(x):=r(x)\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}=\infty),

(1.4)

which is the weighted probability that the two walkers, starting from
x
and a random neighbor of
x
respectively, don’t meet forever.
When
|V|
is finite, we let
\alpha_{t}:=\sum_{x\in V}\alpha_{t}(x)/|V|
be its average over all vertices.
Note that for a transitive Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
,
\alpha_{t}(x)
is independent of
x
, so it should equal
\alpha_{t}
when
|V|
is finite.
For infinite transitive chains we also use
\alpha_{t}
(resp.
\alpha_{\infty}
) to denote
\alpha_{t}(x)
(resp.
\alpha_{\infty}(x)
), for any
x\in V
.

Our main results are of the following forms:

\displaystyle P_{t}\approx\frac{1}{t\alpha_{t}},

(A1)

\displaystyle P_{t}\approx\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{t|V|}.

(A2)

The first form is predicted in a heuristic argument of van der Berg and Kesten
[
29
,
30
]
, and the second form comes from a mean field picture conjectured by Aldous and Fill
[
2
]
and recently verified by Oliveria
[
24
]
.
See Section
1.2.2
and
1.2.3
respectively for more discussions.

In many of the results below, we are taking a sequence of graphs or chains, and study their asymptotic behaviors.
For simplicity we set up some asymptotic notations.
Consider sequences of quantities
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
We write
A_{n}=o(B_{n})
or
A_{n}\ll B_{n}
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{A_{n}}{B_{n}}=0
. We write
A_{n}=O(B_{n})
or
A_{n}\lesssim B_{n}
(and also
B_{n}=\Omega(A_{n})
and
B_{n}\gtrsim A_{n}
) if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|A_{n}|\leq C|B_{n}|
for all
n
. We write

A_{n}\asymp B_{n}
or
A_{n}=\Theta(B_{n})
if we have both
A_{n}=O(B_{n})
and
A_{n}=\Omega(B_{n})
.

1.1.1
The configuration model

We first study random graphs given by the
configuration model
. As a convention in this paper and unless otherwise noted, whenever we consider a ‘graph’, we always mean that the jump rate across each edges equals
1
(i.e.,
r_{x,y}=\mathbbm{1}[x\textrm{ is a neighbor of }y]
).

Definition 1.3
.

Let
D
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
We define
\CM_{n}(D)
as the probability measure on the set of all
n
-vertices graphs, given by the configuration model with degree distribution
D
. In other words, we construct
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
as follows.
We take
n
vertices labelled
1,\ldots,n
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
i.i.d. sampled from
D
.
For each vertex
i
we attach
d_{i}
half edges to it.
Then we get
\mathcal{G}_{n}
by uniformly matching all half edges, conditioned on
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}
being even.

In this paper we always assume that the support of
D
is contained in
[3,\bar{d}]
for some
\bar{d}\geq 3
.
Note that under this condition, as
n\to\infty
, the probability for
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
being connected converges to
1
(see, e.g. Theorem 4.15 in
[
33
]
).
We refer interested readers to Chapter 7 of

[
31
]

for a detailed introduction of the configuration model.

Remark 1.4
.

The assumption that the degree is at least
3
is used to guarantee that the vertex expansion constant of
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
is bounded from below by some constant with high probability, i.e., with probability tending to
1
(as
n\to\infty
). Together with the assumption of
the degree being bounded from above by
\bar{d}
, the relaxation time of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is bounded from above by some constant with high probability, which is used throughout the proof of Theorem
1.6
. See Lemma
6.1
.
The assumption of bounded maximal degree also ensures that with high probability
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is
\delta_{D}
-homogeneous (defined in Definition
6.3
), i.e., the number of vertices with each possible degree is
\Theta(n)
. See also Remark
1.7
for the necessity of the boundedness of maximal degree in our proof.
It is of great interest to weaken the assumptions on
D

to include other types of degree distribution, e.g., the Poisson distribution and the power-law distributions. We leave this for future investigations.

Definition 1.5
.

We consider the
local weak limit
of
\CM_{n}(D)
, which is a unimodular Galton-Watson tree whose root has offspring distribution
D
, and later generations have offspring distribution
D^{*}
, which is a size biased version of
D
, i.e.,
D^{*}(k)=\frac{(k+1)D(k+1)}{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}iD(i)}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We use
\UGT(D)
to denote this law on the space of all rooted trees.

For more details on the local weak convergence of
\CM_{n}(D)
to
\UGT(D)
, see Appendix
B
.
For
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
, the density

P_{t}
is a random variable in the space of all graphs with
n
vertices. Likewise, for
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
, if we let
o
be its root,
P_{t}(o)
(which is the probability of
o
being occupied at time
t
in the CRW) is also a random variable in the space of all infinite trees.
To verify (
A1
) and (
A2
) for these random variables, we define

\alpha(D):=\mathbb{E}(\alpha_{\infty}(o)),

(1.5)

where
\alpha_{\infty}(o)
is defined in equation (
1.4
).

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
be sampled from
\CM_{n}(D)
, conditioned on that
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is connected.
For any sequence of times
t_{n}
such that
1\ll t_{n}\ll n
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}P_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})=\frac{1}{\alpha(D)},

(1.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{nt_{n}}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}P_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})=1,

(1.7)

in probability.

We will show in Section
6.4
that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})/n

converges to
1/(2\alpha(D))
in probability. This was recently proven in the particular case of random
d
-regular graphs in
[
8
]
using different techniques. This implies that (
1.6
) is equivalent to (
1.7
).

Remark 1.7
.

We remark that our proof of Theorem
1.6
works for more general graphs beyond the configuration model. Essentially we only need the following conditions:

•

There exists
\delta>0
, such that for any degree
j
present in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, the number of vertices with degree
j
is at least
\delta n
.

•

The relaxation time of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
(defined as the inverse of its spectral gap) is upper bounded by a constant independent of
n
.

•

As
n\to\infty
,
\mathcal{G}_{n}
converges to a transient (random) graph in the local weak sense.

Note that the first condition and the third condition imply that the maximal degree of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
should be bounded uniformly in
n
. The second condition implies that for the configuration model, the degree needs to be at least
3
, since otherwise there would be a path of length in the order of
\log n
, and the relaxation time would be unbounded as
n\to\infty
.

A similar result holds for the unimodular Galton-Watson tree.

Theorem 1.8
.

For
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
with root
o
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}t\mathbb{E}P_{t}(o)=\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}.

1.1.2
Transitive Markov chains

Our next series of results are about transitive Markov chains
(V,\mathbf{r})
. As a convention in this paper, whenever we consider a transitive Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, we always assume that the total jump rate
r(x)=1
for each vertex
x
.

We denote by
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})=t_{\mathrm{rel}}
the
relaxation time
of a Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, defined as the inverse of its spectral gap (i.e., minus of the second largest eigenvalue of the infinitesimal generator of
(V,\mathbf{r})
). We also write
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G})
when we study the SRW on a graph
\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 1.9
.

Suppose we are given a sequence of finite transitive Markov chains
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
such that
|V_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\lesssim|V_{n}|
,
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
. Then for any sequence of times
t_{n}
satisfying

t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|V_{n}|t_{n}}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}P_{t_{n}}=1,

\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}\alpha_{t_{n}}P_{t_{n}}=1.

We next prove a stronger result under the following stronger ‘transience condition’. A related notion in the context of cover times of random walks was proposed in
[
17
, Section 2]
.

Definition 1.10
.

A sequence of finite Markov chains
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
with
\max_{x\in V_{n}}\mathbf{r}_{n}(x)\asymp 1\asymp\min_{x\in V_{n}}\mathbf{r}_{n}(x)
is said to be
uniformly transient
if

\lim_{s\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\max_{x,y\in V_{n}}\int_{s\wedge t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,y)\mathrm{d}t=0,

(1.8)

where
p_{t}^{(n)}(x,y)
is the time
t
transition probability from
x
to
y
corresponding to
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
.

As will be discussed in Section
1.2.4
, this condition is surprisingly only slightly stronger than the assumptions in Theorem
1.9
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
be a sequence of finite transitive Markov chains which is uniformly transient, and
|V_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Then for every sequence of times
t_{n}
satisfying
1\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|V_{n}|t_{n}}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}P_{t_{n}}=1,

(1.9)

\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}\alpha_{t_{n}}P_{t_{n}}=1.

(1.10)

Our methods of proving Theorems

1.9
to
1.11
can be adapted to certain infinite transitive graphs.
As a convention of this paper, all infinite graphs are assumed to be locally finite.
We will consider unimodular graphs, which are graphs that satisfy the mass-transport principle. See
[
22
, Chapter 8]
for details of unimodular graphs and mass-transport principle. Unimodular transitive graphs are a large sub-class of transitive graphs. All Cayley graphs and
all
amenable
2
2

2

An infinite transitive graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
is called ‘amenable’, if
for any
x\in V
,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log p_{t}(x,x)=0,

where
p_{t}
denotes the transition probability of a SRW on this graph.
In other words, the probability of a SRW returning to the starting point does not decay exponentially in
t
. In particular, the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is amenable for all
d
.
For this and other equivalent spectral and geometric definitions of amenability, see
[
22
, Chapter 6]
.
transitive graphs are unimodular.

Theorem 1.12
.

For any infinite transient unimodular transitive graph
\mathcal{G}
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}tP_{t}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\infty}}.

1.1.3
Applications to the voter model: generalizing Sawyer’s theorem

In this subsection, we present results about the voter model obtained by duality (see Section
2.1
for more details).

Generally, the voter model is defined as follows. Given a Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, we think of each vertex as a ‘voter’. Initially each vertex
x\in V
is assigned a mutually different number, which we think of as its ‘opinion’. Then for any voters
x,y
, with rate
r_{x,y}
the voter
y
adopts the opinion of
x
.

Now we state Sawyer’s Theorem
[
26
, Theorem 2.2]
.

Theorem 1.13
(Sawyer’s Theorem).

Consider the voter model on a Markov chain with state space
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with
d\geq 2
. Let
\hat{N}_{t}
be the number of voters that have the same opinion as the voter at the origin at time
t
. Assume there exists a function
g(x)\geq 0
so that
r_{x,y}=g(x-y)
and
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}g(x)=1
. Also assume that

\begin{split}&\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2}g(x)<\infty,\quad\mbox{ if }d=2;\\
&\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|^{2+\varepsilon}g(x)<\infty\;\mbox{ for some }\epsilon>0,\quad\mbox{ if }d>2.\end{split}

Here
\left\|x\right\|=(\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
for
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{N}_{t}}{\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{t}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{Gamma}\left(2,2\right),

as
n\to\infty
, where
\mathrm{Gamma}\left(2,2\right)
is the Gamma distribution with probability density function
f_{\mathrm{Gamma}\left(2,2\right)}(x)=4x\exp(-2x)\mathbbm{1}[x\geq 0]
.

We note that in this theorem
g
is not required to be an even function so that
\mathbf{r}
may not be symmetric.
One merit of Sawyer’s Theorem is that it gives the limiting distribution of

\hat{N}_{t}/\mathbb{E}\hat{N_{t}}
in the explicit form. This is very helpful in determining the asymptotics of
P_{t}
.

We now describe our extensions of Sawyer’s Theorem to more general Markov chains. We consider either (1) a sequence of finite transitive Markov chains
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
with unit jump rate at each state, or (2) random graphs sampled from the configuration model
\CM_{n}(D)
(from Definition
1.3
) conditioned to be connected. Let
t_{n}
be a sequence of times.
These times and Markov chains or graphs satisfy the conditions in either Theorem
1.6
or
1.9
or
1.11
.
Let
\hat{N}_{n,t}
be the number of voters that have the same opinion as the voter at
\mathcal{U}
at time
t
, where
\mathcal{U}\sim\mbox{Unif}(V_{n}
).

Theorem 1.14
.

Under either setting, we have

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\frac{\hat{N}_{n,t_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{n,t_{n}}}\right)^{k}\right)=\frac{(k+1)!}{2^{k}}.

Here, if we are under the setting of Theorem
1.6
, the expectation is conditional on the random graph, and the convergence is in the sense of convergence in probability (i.e., it is a quenched result).
Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{N}_{n,t_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{n,t_{n}}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{Gamma}\left(2,2\right),

as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem
1.14
follows from our proof of the corresponding theorems: under the setting of Theorem
1.6
or
1.9
or
1.11
, the corresponding results can be obtained from (
5.28
) and Proposition
6.10
, or from (
5.28
), or from (
7.24
), respectively.

We have a similar result for infinite graphs.
For a unimodular Galton-Watson tree
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
, or an infinite transient unimodular transitive graph, let
\hat{N}_{t}
be the number of voters that have the same opinion as the voter at the root (in the Galton-Watson tree setting) or a certain vertex (in the infinite transitive graph setting) at time
t
.

Theorem 1.15
.

Under either setting, we have

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\frac{\hat{N}_{t}}{\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{t}}\right)^{k}\right)=\frac{(k+1)!}{2^{k}}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{N}_{t}}{\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{t}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{Gamma}\left(2,2\right),

as
t\to\infty
.

In the setting of
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
this result follows from Theorem
1.14
in the configuration model setting, using the same arguments in the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
In the setting of an infinite transient unimodular transitive graph, Theorem
1.15
follows from equations (
7.28
) and (
7.29
) in the proof of Theorem
1.12
.

1.2
Discussions of our results and related previous works

1.2.1
The big bang regime

Recall that in the finite setup, the big bang regime refers to the setting where
t\ll t_{\mathrm{coal}}
, while our results are stated for
t\ll n
for the configuration model, and
t\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
for transitive Markov chains. There is in fact no loss at all because in our setups, we always have
t_{\mathrm{coal}}\asymp t_{\mathrm{meet}}
. We explain this now.

First, we note that
t_{\mathrm{coal}}\geq t_{\mathrm{meet}}
holds for any Markov chain. This follows from the monotonicity of the CRW model w.r.t. initial conditions, as can be seen from the graphical representation which allows one to couple CRW with different starting conditions.

For the other direction,
let

t_{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathbf{r})=t_{\mathrm{hit}}:=\max_{x,y}\mathbb{E}_{x}T_{y}
be the maximal expected hitting time of a Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, where
T_{y}
is the hitting time of the state
y
.
In
[
23
]
, using the powerful random target lemma, Oliveira proved that for a finite reversible chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
,
t_{\mathrm{coal}}
is upper bounded by a constant multiplying
t_{\mathrm{hit}}
.

For transitive chains we have

t_{\mathrm{hit}}\leq 2\max_{x}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(T_{x})\leq 4t_{\mathrm{meet}},

(1.11)

where
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}
means that the Markov chain starts from the stationary measure
\pi
.
Here the first inequality is due to
[
21
, Lemma 10.2]
and the second inequality follows from
[
2
, Proposition 14.5]
.
Thus we conclude that
t_{\mathrm{coal}}\asymp t_{\mathrm{meet}}
.

For the configuration model, the assumption that
D
is at least three and upper bounded implies that the relaxation time
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
is bounded by some constant with high probability (see Lemma
6.1
). Hence the proof of Proposition
2.14
below shows that for
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
,

t_{\mathrm{hit}}\leq Cn
for some constant
C
with high probability. Actually
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
grows linearly in
n
(see Lemma
6.12
). Thus for the configuration model we also have
t_{\mathrm{coal}}\asymp t_{\mathrm{meet}}
.

There is a recent work
[
19
]
of Kanade, Mallmann-Trenn and Sauerwald, where they provide essentially optimal conditions for
t_{\mathrm{coal}}\asymp t_{\mathrm{meet}}
. We note that they work in discrete-time settings and that the jumps rates are not assumed to be symmetric.

We also mention that while our results (in the finite setup) concentrate on the big bang regime, results on the regime
t\asymp|V|
in various setups are already proven in
[
24
]
(see discussions in Section
1.2.3
).

1.2.2
A heuristic for the rate of decay

The exact decay rate of
P_{t}
is predicted by a heuristic of van der Berg and Kesten. They masterfully turned it into a proof for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 3

[
29
,
30
]
(as well as for a model generalizing CRW when
d\geq 5
). This heuristic involves an ‘independence approximation’ in which the events that far away vertices are occupied are assumed to be approximately independent. In justifying this heuristic van der Berg and Kesten relied on the transience of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 3
as well as on some other properties of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
3
3

3

It seems plausible that their arguments can be extended to transitive graphs satisfying a uniform polynomial growth estimate, but we do not believe that their arguments extend to all amenable transitive graphs without some substantial modifications, and likely also some new ideas. In the finite setup, it is not clear if their approach can be pushed to graphs which are just ‘barely transient’ like a
n\times n\times m
torus with
m\asymp\log n
. Our Theorems
1.9
and
1.11
can treat such graphs.
The starting point is the equation
4
4

4

For a finite chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
,
-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P_{t}=\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{\text{all unordered }(x,y):\,x\neq y}r_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(x\text{ and }y\text{ are occupied at time }t)
. In the infinite setup (
1.12
)
is valid for unimodular transitive graphs, since applying the mass transport principle to the transport functions
f(x,y)=r_{x,y}\mathbbm{1}[xy\in E,x\in\xi_{t},y\notin\xi_{t}]
yields
\sum_{y:\,y\sim x}r_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(x\in\xi_{t},y\notin\xi_{t})=\sum_{y:\,y\sim x}r_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(x\notin\xi_{t},y\in\xi_{t})
, from which (
1.12
) easily follows.

-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P_{t}(x)=\sum_{y:\,y\sim x}r_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(x,y\in\xi_{t}).

(1.12)

Here recall that
\xi_{t}
is the set of vertices that are occupied at time
t
.
Using the graphical representation of the CRW model we can write for any
s\in[0,t]
,

\mathbb{P}(x,y\in\xi_{t})\approx\sum_{w,z}\mathbb{P}(w,z\in\xi_{s})\mathbb{P}(B_{t,s}(w,z,x,y)),

where
B_{t,s}(w,z,x,y)
is the event that the particles which are at
w
and
z
reach locations
x
and
y
respectively at time
t
. Note that here we write ‘
\approx
’ instead of ‘=’ because there might be particles at locations other than
w
and
z
that also reach
x
or
y
at time
t
. However, such events make a negligible contribution to
P_{t}
provided we let
1\ll t-s\ll t
.
Again by the graphical representation, the probability of
B_{t,s}(w,z,x,y)
is the same as the probability that two independent random walks started at
w
and
z
reach locations
x
and
y
respectively at time
t-s
, and do so without colliding. By reversibility, this is the same as
\alpha_{t-s}(x,y,w,z)
, which is defined to be the probability of two independent particles starting from
x
and
y
reaching locations
w
and
z
at time
t-s
and doing so without colliding.
Then we have

\sum_{w,z}\mathbb{P}(B_{t,s}(w,z,x,y))\approx\sum_{w,z}\alpha_{t-s}(x,y,w,z)=:\alpha_{t-s}(x,y).

We also have the ‘independence approximation’ in the transitive setup: for all
w,z
that are far way from each other,

\mathbb{P}(w,z\in\xi_{s})\approx\mathbb{P}(w\in\xi_{s})\mathbb{P}(z\in\xi_{s})=P_{s}^{2}.

We note that actually, for any vertices
w,z
one has that
\mathbb{P}(w,z\in\xi_{s})\leq P_{s}^{2}

[
3
]
.
When
t-s
is large, for the sum
\sum_{w,z}\alpha_{t-s}(x,y,w,z)
, the main contribution is from those
w,z
that are far away from each other.
Thus we have that

\mathbb{P}(x,y\in\xi_{t})\approx\alpha_{t-s}(x,y)P_{s}^{2}.

Note that
\alpha_{t-s}=\sum_{y:\,y\sim x}r_{x,y}\alpha_{t-s}(x,y)
(where
\alpha_{t-s}=\alpha_{t-s}(x)
is defined in (
1.3
) and is independent of
x
by transitivity). In many cases it is possible to verify that for some
s
close
t
we have that
\alpha_{t-s}\approx\alpha_{t}
and
P_{s}\approx P_{t}
. This gives that
-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P_{t}\approx\alpha_{t}P_{t}^{2}
which leads to the prediction:

P_{t}\approx\frac{1}{t\alpha_{t}}.

(A1)

We note equation (
1.2
) (in the case
d\geq 2
) due to Bramson and Griffeath can also be re-written as (
A1
).
While we do not directly try to justify this independence approximation, our results confirm the conclusion (
A1
) in many cases. We take a different approach which has the advantage of also generalizing Saywer’s Theorem to other instances.

1.2.3
Mean field behavior

Another motivation for our work comes from a conjecture of Aldous and Fill
[
2
, Open Problem 14.12]
who suggested to study the distribution of
\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}
in the (finite) transitive setup under the spectral condition
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{hit}}
. They conjectured that under this condition the coalescence time should exhibit certain mean-field behaviors. Our results show that in some sense, under appropriate transience assumptions, such mean-field behaviors are valid also during a large part of the big bang regime (for transitive graphs and the configuration model).

Consider a sequence of graphs with the number of vertices growing to infinity. Aldous and Fill conjectured that in the transitive setup, under the spectral condition
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{hit}}
, the law of
\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}
is close in total variation to the law of
t_{\mathrm{meet}}\sum_{k=2}^{n}\binom{k}{2}^{-1}\tau_{k}
where
\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{n}
are i.i.d. Exp
(1)
and
n
is the number of vertices in the graph.
It is instructive to note that for the complete graph this random variable equals
\tau_{\mathrm{coal}}
in law, and the evolution of the number of particles is the same as in Kingman’s coalescent from the moment there are
n
equivalence classes.
This conjecture was nearly solved by Oliveira
[
24
]
(for the stronger
W_{1}
Wasserstein metric), but under the seemingly marginally stronger assumption that the mixing time
t_{\mathrm{mix}}
satisfies
t_{\mathrm{mix}}\ll t_{\mathrm{hit}}
.
This condition was recently shown to be equivalent to
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{hit}}
in
[
16
]
.

Let
\rho_{k}
be the first time when there are
k
particles.
Using his earlier work
[
23
]
, Oliveira argues that for
k\gg 1
, the time
\rho_{k}=O(t_{\mathrm{meet}}/k)=o(t_{\mathrm{meet}})
with high probability. Hence the period up to
\rho_{k}
can be seen as a short burn-in period. He then argues (in some precise quantitative sense) that if
k
diverges slowly enough in terms of some function of
t_{\mathrm{meet}}/t_{\mathrm{rel}}
then from that point on, the evolution of the process is close to the evolution on the complete graph of the same size
n

with
k
initial particles, up to a scaling of the time by a factor of
t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n
. More precisely, he shows that with high probability, simultaneously for all
2\leq i\leq k
, once there are
i
particles left, the time until the next collision is much larger than the
k^{-2}
mixing time of
i
independent random walks.
Hence the mixing time can be viewed as a burn-in period (compared to the time until the next collision), so the coalescence time is close in distribution to the sum
\sum_{i=2}^{k}\hat{\tau}_{i}
where
\hat{\tau}_{2},\ldots,\hat{\tau}_{k}
are independent, and
\hat{\tau}_{i}
is the first collision time of
i
independent walks starting from i.i.d. stationary initial distributions.

By a classic result of Aldous and Brown
[
1
]
, the assumption
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
implies that the collision time between each pair of particles is roughly exponentially distributed with mean
t_{\mathrm{meet}}
. Oliveira showed that in fact the law of the first collision time among the remaining
i
particles is close to the law of the minimum of
\binom{i}{2}
independent Exp
(1/t_{\mathrm{meet}})
random variables, which is the law of
\binom{i}{2}^{-1}\tau_{i}
. He also showed that this holds simultaneously for all
2\leq i\leq k
, in the sense that the sum of the error terms over
2\leq i\leq k
is
o(1)
.

Observe that for the complete graph,
\rho_{k}
has the same distribution as
t_{\mathrm{meet}}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\binom{i}{2}^{-1}\tau_{i}
. This leads to the following mean field approximation for the density of particles: for
t\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
we have

P_{t}\approx\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{tn}.

(A2)

Here
t\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
corresponds to
k\gg 1
, which implies that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\binom{i}{2}^{-1}\tau_{i}
is concentrated, from which (
A2
) follows by a simple calculation.

The number
k
from Oliveira’s arguments is allowed to diverge only as some function of
t_{\mathrm{hit}}/t_{\mathrm{mix}}
. Hence it could be the case that it is only allowed to diverge arbitrarily slowly, and the fastest it may be allowed to diverge is like
o(\sqrt{n})
(for expanders).

The results in this work verify the validity of (
A2
) under certain finitary notions of transience assumptions as well as the spectral condition
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{hit}}
proposed by Aldous and Fill for times
t\gg 1
or
t\gg t_{\mathrm{rel}}
(depending on the assumptions being made). For instance, for transitive expanders and for the configuration model we allow any
1\ll t\ll n
. This corresponds to taking any
1\ll k\ll n
.

Interestingly, like Oliveira, a key quantity of interest for us is
\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=2}^{k}\hat{\tau}_{i}\leq t)
, as it happens to be the
(k-1)
-th moment from Sawyer’s Theorem at time
t
. We verify that, up to a scaling of the time by
t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n
, this probability is well approximated by the corresponding probability for the complete graph, i.e., the mean field counterpart. As in
[
24
]
, this essentially shows that
\hat{\tau}_{2},\ldots,\hat{\tau}_{k}
are asymptotically independent, and that each
\hat{\tau}_{i}
can be thought of as the minimum of
\binom{i}{2}
independent collision times. The main difference is that, here we have to do some more careful analysis of this probability for
t
much smaller than
t_{\mathrm{meet}}
. While Oliveira estimates this probability up to some
o(1)
additive error term, for the small
t
that we consider this error term may be of a much higher order than the probability being estimated. One strategy we take is to reverse time and study a certain type of random walks with branching. See Section
1.3
for details.

1.2.4
Assumptions for transitive chains

In this section, we discuss the assumptions that appeared in Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.11
.

We note that under transitivity the condition
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\lesssim|V_{n}|
is equivalent to
t_{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\lesssim|V_{n}|
, by the fact that
2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\leq t_{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
and (
1.11
).
These conditions are also equivalent to several other conditions. For example, if the transitive Markov chain
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
is replaced by a
d_{n}
-regular transitive graph
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, it is equivalent to
d_{n}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\lesssim 1
, where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is the maximal effective resistance between a pair of vertices. It can also be shown to be equivalent to
\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1
(here we let
r_{x,y}=\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]/d_{n}
instead of
\mathbbm{1}{[x\sim y]}
, and recall that
p_{t}^{(n)}
is the transition probability of
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
).

We now briefly explain the equivalence between
\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1
and
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\lesssim|V_{n}|
.
First one can show that

\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1
if and only if
\int_{0}^{|V_{n}|}(p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)-1/|V_{n}|)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1
, which is also equivalent to

\int_{0}^{\infty}(p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)-1/|V_{n}|)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 1
.
Then the equivalence follows from the facts that
\int_{0}^{\infty}(p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)-1/|V_{n}|)\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{x}/|V_{n}|
and
\max_{x}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{x}\geq 2\max_{x,y}\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}
(this holds even without transitivity).
See the proof of Proposition
2.14
for details.

We next compare the assumptions in
Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.11
.
In the case where the transitive Markov chain
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
is replaced by a
d_{n}
-regular transitive graph
\mathcal{G}_{n}
with
d_{n}=O(1)
, following recent works
[
28
,
27
]
, it is shown in
[
6
, Proposition 2.10]
that the condition (
1.8
) is implied by

\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\ll\left|V_{n}\right|/\log\left|V_{n}\right|.

(1.13)

Condition (
1.13
) provides a simple and easily verifiable sufficient condition for uniform transience.
For example, the
d
-dimensional torus of side length
n
with
d\geq 3
satisfies (
1.13
).
We note that it follows from
[
6
, Proposition 2.10]
that the condition
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\lesssim\left|V_{n}\right|/\log\left|V_{n}\right|
implies that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\asymp|V_{n}|
, which implies part of the assumptions in Theorem
1.9
.
On the other hand, if
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\gg\left|V_{n}\right|
and
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is transitive of (uniformly in
n
) bounded degree, we have
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\gg|V_{n}|
, so the assertion of Theorem
1.9
fails.
5
5

5

It follows from
[
28
]
that
\mathcal{G}_{n}
satisfies a certain technical condition due to Diaconis and Saloff-Coste known as moderate growth, for which it is known that the mixing time is proportional to the diameter square. In our case, by the assumption
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\gg\left|V_{n}\right|
, this means that the mixing time and the meeting time are much larger than the number of vertices.
It follows that there are relatively few instances satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
1.9
but not those in Theorem
1.11
(i.e., uniform transience). Two such examples are an
n\times n\times\lceil\log n\rceil
(discrete) torus and a Cartesian product of the
n
-cycle with a transitive expander of size
n
.
Finally, we remark that in
[
27
, Theorem 1.8]
, using results from
[
28
]
a different analog of transience is proven for finite transitive graphs satisfying a condition similar to (
1.13
).

1.2.5
Some other existing bounds on
P_{t}

For a graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, Benjamini, Foxall, Gurel-Gurevich, Junge and Kesten
[
4
]
proved the lower bound
P_{t}(x)\geq 1/(1+d_{\max}t)
for all
x\in V
, where
d_{\max}
is the maximal degree of
\mathcal{G}
. They also pointed out in
[
4
, Proposition 1.4]
that, for CRW on any infinite graph with unit jump rate along each edge,
P_{t}(x)\leq(1+\varepsilon)/(2\sqrt{\pi t})
for all
x\in V
,
\varepsilon>0
and
t
large enough (depending only on
\varepsilon
).
Later Foxall, Hutchcroft, and Junge
[
15
]
showed, using an elegant application of the mass transport principle, that for any unimodular random rooted graph (with root
o
) we still have the bound
\mathbb{E}(P_{t}(o))\geq 1/(C_{1}+C_{2}t)
for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}
. Here the expectation is taken over the law of the rooted graph.

As we mentioned before,
in
[
23
]
Oliveira proved that for any finite reversible chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
one has that

t_{\mathrm{coal}}\leq Ct_{\mathrm{hit}}
for some universal constant
C
. Oliveira’s proof actually shows that for some absolute constant
C>0
, we have
\min\{P_{t},1/\left|V\right|\}\leq\delta
if
t\geq Ct_{\mathrm{mix}}+C\frac{t_{\mathrm{hit}}}{|V|\delta}
.

1.3
Ingredients of our proofs

As mentioned before in Section
1.1
, we extend the analysis from
[
7
]
beyond
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
In the finite setup, we uniformly pick a particle at the beginning, and let
N_{t}
be the number of particles that coalesce with it by time
t
, including itself. It has the same distribution as
\hat{N}_{t}
, the size of the set of vertices which have the same opinion as the origin at time
t
in the voter model, via duality; see Section
2.1
for a formal definition and discussions.
In the finite setup we also have that
P_{t}=\mathbb{E}[N_{t}^{-1}]
(see Lemma
2.1
below). Hence it suffices to study the distribution of
N_{t}
via computing the moments of
N_{t}
, and controlling the behavior of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}
near
0

(specifically, showing that
\mathbb{E}((N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}\leq\varepsilon])
goes to 0 as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
t
), since then one can go from the distributional convergence of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}
to the convergence of
\mathbb{E}((N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1})
.

In our paper we give an upper bound of
P_{t}
for general Markov chains (Theorem
3.1
below), which enables us to close the gap between the moments of
N_{t}
and the asymptotics of
P_{t}
in various setups, whereas the proof of such bounds in
[
7
]
relies heavily on the geometric structure of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(e.g., they used a partition of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
into boxes).

As in
[
7
]
, we also use moment calculations in our proofs. Such calculations in
[
26
]
are done using the voter model, and the arguments also rely on the lattice structure and are difficult to generalize.
Instead, we directly analyze the CRW model, using a quite different approach. We mostly work in the finite setup, where some care is required since the limiting arguments have to be replaced by quantitative ones, and in particular when considering non-transitive graphs as in Theorem
1.6
.
The calculation of the
k
-th moments of
N_{t}
can be reduced to the probability that
k+1
particles starting from independent stationary positions coalesce by time
t
.

By classic results of Aldous and Brown (see Section
2.2.3
for details), under the assumption
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
the meeting time
\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}
of two independent stationary walks is roughly distributed as
\Exp(1/t_{\mathrm{meet}})
, and the probability density is roughly
\frac{\exp(-t/t_{\mathrm{meet}})}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}
for
t\gg t_{\mathrm{rel}}
.
For
k+1
particles, we consider the
k
collision times.
We will show that its probability density function at
(t_{1},\ldots t_{k})
is roughly
\frac{1+o(1)}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}^{k}}
. Here the tuple
(t_{1},\ldots t_{k})
satisfies that
0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t
, and that each
t_{i+1}-t_{i}
is not ‘too small’.
Specifically, in establishing this we wish to have that for all
1\leq i\leq k
, the joint distribution of the locations of the remaining particles at the
i
-th collision time is close to
\pi^{\otimes(k+1-i)}
.
However, given information on past collisions,
the joint distribution of the locations of particles can be complicated.
We get around of this by showing that this information will have negligible effect after a short amount of time since the previous collision. Here ‘a short amount of time’ sometimes means a time interval large compared to
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
, and sometimes means any large time interval.
For transitive graphs, such analysis relies on the transitivity and transience conditions.
For the configuration model, the relevant analysis is more involved and subtle due to the lack of transitivity. In this case we rely on several facts: (i) the configuration model graphs are expanders with high probability (this is the reason we require that the minimal degree is at least
3
) (ii) the law of the local structure of the transitive graph is the same around each vertex. See Remark
1.7
for more general graphs that our approach can be applied to.

Another ingredient in these analysis is using the reversibility of Markov chains.
Thus we transform the probability density function of the
k
collision times into the probability of having no collisions in a certain branching random walk process, in which
k
new particles are created at time
t-t_{k},t-t_{k-1},\ldots,t-t_{1}
(see Section
4.2
). By a careful analysis of this non-collision probability we can single out all the factors that contribute to the error, and such analysis is summarized as Proposition

5.2
, which is our general framework.
For transitive chains we can make some refinements and obtain Proposition
7.4
.
We then apply these frameworks to different families of graphs and obtain the above mentioned results.

1.4
Further problems

Starting from this work there are several directions that could be further developed. The first is on whether the assumptions on the degree distribution in Theorem
1.6
can be relaxed. In particular, since the configuration model with Poisson degree distribution is contiguous to Erdös-Rényi graphs (see, e.g.,
[
20
, Theorem 2.1]
), it would be very interesting to investigate the following problem.

Problem 1.16
.

Fix any
\mu>1
and let
D
be
\mathrm{Poisson}(\mu)
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
be sampled from
\CM_{n}(D)
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
be the giant component of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
conditioned to survive forever and define
\alpha(D)
as in (
1.5
). Do (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) hold for
P_{t}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{n})
?

Then one can ask whether the results for (finite or infinite transitive graphs) still hold under some other conditions.

Problem 1.17
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be an infinite transitive graph. Assume that the size of the balls of radius
r
is at least
cr^{2}
for some constant
c
. Does (
A1
) hold? Here the
r^{2}
comes from the fact that (
A1
) holds for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
.

Problem 1.18
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n})
be a sequence of finite transitive graphs such that
|V_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and all graphs have the same degree.
As mentioned in Section
1.2.4
, under the condition
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\ll|V_{n}|/\log|V_{n}|
, (
A1
) holds for a sequence of times
t_{n}
with
1\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
.
Can we weaken this condition to
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{2}\lesssim|V_{n}|
?

One could also ask if our results can extend to some other random graphs.

Problem 1.19
.

Can we extend our results on unimodular Galton-Watson trees to general almost surely transient reversible random graphs, where the root has finite expected degree? (See Chapter 3 of
[
11
]
for the notion of reversible random graphs)

The next direction is to get more refined information on the fluctuation of the number of particles (in a finite subset
A
of the states).

Problem 1.20
.

As commented before Section
1.1
, for a large finite subset
A
of the state space,
|\xi_{t}\cap A|
has
O((\mathbb{E}|\xi_{t}\cap A|)^{1/2})
fluctuations around its expectation. Can we upgrade this to show that in fact (for appropriately large
t
) the fluctuations are asymptotically Gaussian? If so, can we determine the variance?

Organization of the remaining text

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2.1
we set up CRW and the voter model, and their relations, and provide basic tools for Markov chains.
Then we first prove non-sharp general bounds for the density
P_{t}
, in Section
3
.
In Section
4
we lay out the framework for the more careful analysis.
In Section
5
we carry out the estimates for general Markov chains, and all the arguments are packed into Proposition
5.2
.
In Section
6
we provide estimates that are specific for the configuration model, and prove Theorems
1.6
and
1.8
.
In Section
7
we prove Theorems

1.9
,
1.11
, and Theorem
1.12
for finite and infinite transitive Markov chains.

2
Preliminaries: the model and Markov chains

2.1
CRW and the voter model

In the voter model, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and any vertex
x\in V
, we denote
\zeta_{t}(x)
as the opinion of
x
at time
t
.
Let
\zeta^{x}_{t}
be the set of voters that at time
t
have the same opinion as voter
x
at time
0
,
i.e.,

\zeta^{x}_{t}:=\{y\in V:\zeta_{t}(y)=\zeta_{0}(x)\}.

For CRW starting with one particle at each vertex, recall that we use
\xi_{t}\subset V
to denote the locations of the remaining particles at time
t
.
The duality between the voter model and CRW implies

P_{t}(x)=\mathbb{P}(x\in\xi_{t})=\mathbb{P}(\zeta^{x}_{t}\neq\emptyset),

(2.1)

see, e.g.,
[
12
, Section 3]
or
[
2
, Chapter 14]
for an introduction to the duality.

Consider paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow V
satisfying the following property: for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
s\geq 0
, if
P_{i}(s)=P_{j}(s)
then
P_{i}(t)=P_{j}(t)
for all
t\geq s
. We denote the coalescence time as

\mathcal{C}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}):=\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\colon P_{1}(t)=\cdots=P_{k}(t)\right\}\cup\{\infty\}.

Throughout this paper, we use the random functions
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow V
to denote the locations of
k
particles performing CRW via the graphical representation of the model.
The starting locations are
X_{i}(0),1\leq i\leq k
, which can be random (while independent of the walks) or deterministic.

We denote by
N_{t}
the following random variable.
Suppose that
n=|V|
and
X_{1}(0),\ldots,X_{n}(0)
is an enumeration of all vertices in
V
.
Let
\iota
be uniformly chosen from
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We define

N_{t}:=|\{i:\mathcal{C}(X_{i},X_{\iota})\leq t\}|.

Note that we always have
N_{t}\geq 1
since
\mathcal{C}(X_{\iota},X_{\iota})=0
.

Our analysis of the decay of
P_{t}
relies on its following relation with
N_{t}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For finite graphs we have
P_{t}=\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1})
.

Take
\mathcal{U}\sim\Unif(V)
.
Define
\hat{N}_{t}:=\left|\{x\in V:\zeta_{t}(x)=\zeta_{t}(\mathcal{U})\}\right|
.
Then
\hat{N}_{t}
has the same distribution as
N_{t}
by duality (see, e.g.,
[
12
, Section 3]
), which implies that it suffices to prove
P_{t}=\mathbb{E}(\hat{N}_{t}^{-1})
.
Now we claim

\forall k\geq 1,\qquad\mathbb{P}(\hat{N}_{t}=k)=k\mathbb{P}(\left|\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\right|=k).

(2.2)

Indeed, this is by an interchange of summations:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\hat{N}_{t}=k)&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{y}\mathbb{P}(\left|\{x:\zeta_{t}(x)=\zeta_{t}(y)\}\right|=k)\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{y}\sum_{z}\mathbb{E}(\mathbbm{1}[y\in\zeta^{z}_{t},\left|\zeta^{z}_{t}\right|=k])\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{z}\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{y}\mathbbm{1}[y\in\zeta^{z}_{t},\left|\zeta^{z}_{t}\right|=k]\right)\\
&=\frac{k}{n}\sum_{z}\mathbb{E}(\mathbbm{1}[\left|\zeta^{z}_{t}\right|=k])\\
&=k\mathbb{P}(\left|\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\right|=k).\end{split}

Now the assertion of this lemma follows from (
2.1
) and (
2.2
):

P_{t}=\mathbb{P}(\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\neq\emptyset)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\left|\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\right|=k)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{P}(\hat{N}_{t}=k)=\mathbb{E}\hat{N}_{t}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{-1}.

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎Throughout we write
n^{x}_{t}:=\left|\zeta^{x}_{t}\right|
and
n_{t}:=n^{\mathcal{U}}_{t}
where
\mathcal{U}\sim\Unif(V)
is independent of
\{n^{x}_{t},x\in V\}
.
For future reference we also record the following corollary.

Corollary 2.2
.

For any
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[N_{t}\geq M])=\mathbb{P}(n_{t}\geq M)
and
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[N_{t}\leq M])=\mathbb{P}(0<n_{t}\leq M)
in finite graphs.

This is a direct consequence of (
2.2
).

2.2
Classical results on Markov chains

In this subsection, we recall some important classical concepts and results on Markov chains. Some other detailed results and proofs are deferred to Section
A
.

2.2.1
The Poincaré inequality

Definition 2.3
.

For any Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with a stationary distribution
\pi
, let
\ell_{2}(V,\pi)
be the space of all functions
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\|f\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{x\in V}f(x)^{2}\pi(x)<\infty
.
We define the inner-product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\pi}
induced by
\pi
on
\ell_{2}(V,\pi)
as

\langle f,g\rangle_{\pi}:=\sum_{x\in V}\pi(x)f(x)g(x).

And we define

\Var_{\pi}(f):=\|f-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(f)\|_{2}^{2},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(f):=\sum_{x\in V}\pi(x)f(x).

Let
p_{t}=e^{tQ}
be the time
t
transition kernel of the Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, where
Q(x,y)=r_{x,y}
for
x\neq y
and
Q(x,x)=-r(x)
. For a function
f\in\ell_{2}(V,\pi)
, the function
p_{t}f\in\ell_{2}(V,\pi)
is defined as

p_{t}f(x):=\sum_{y\in V}p_{t}(x,y)f(y).

Recall that
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
is the inverse of the spectral gap (the second smallest eigenvalue of
-Q
).
By the Poincaré inequality (see, e.g.,
[
21
, Lemma 20.5]
), we have the following result.

Lemma 2.4
.

Given any continuous-time Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
(not necessarily symmetric),
for
t\geq 0
and
f\in\ell_{2}(V,\pi)
,

\mathrm{Var}_{\pi}(p_{t}f)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2t}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\mathrm{Var}_{\pi}(f).

(2.3)

This lemma implies the following result.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(V,\mathbf{r})
be a finite-state continuous-time Markov chain with symmetric transition rates
\mathbf{r}
.
For
t>0
and a probability distribution
\mu
on
V
, we denote by
\mu_{t}
the distribution on
V
given by

\mu_{t}(x):=\sum_{y}\mu(y)p_{t}(y,x)=\sum_{y}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(y)=p_{t}\mu(x),\quad\forall x\in V

which is the law of
X(t)
for a walker
X
with initial location
X(0)\sim\mu
.
Then for any
t,s>0
, we have

\sum_{x}\left(\mu_{t}(x)-\frac{1}{|V|}\right)^{2}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2t}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\sum_{x}\left(\mu(x)-\frac{1}{|V|}\right)^{2},

(2.4)

and

\max_{x,y}p_{t+s}(x,y)-\frac{1}{|V|}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\left(\max_{x}p_{s}(x,x)-\frac{1}{|V|}\right).

(2.5)

We define the
\ell_{\infty,\pi}
distance between two probability measures
\mu
and
\lambda
w.r.t.
\pi
as

\left\|\mu-\lambda\right\|_{\infty,\pi}:=\sup_{x}\frac{\left|\mu(x)-\lambda(x)\right|}{\pi(x)}.

Using the Poincaré inequality, we have the following corollary of Lemma
2.5
.

Corollary 2.6
.

For any finite-state continuous-time symmetric Markov chain,
starting from any initial distribution
\mu
, we have

\left\|\mu_{t}-\pi\right\|_{\infty,\pi}\leq\frac{1}{|V|}
for
t\geq 2t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log|V|
.

2.2.2
Mixing time and relaxation time

Now we define the mixing time of a Markov chain.

Definition 2.7
.

The
total variation distance
between two distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on
(V,\mathbf{r})
is defined as

\left\|\mu-\nu\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x}\left|\mu(x)-\nu(x)\right|.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the
\varepsilon
-
total variation mixing time
is defined as

t^{\mathrm{TV}}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon):=\inf\{t\geq 0:\max_{x\in V}\left\|p_{t}(x,\cdot)-\pi\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\varepsilon\},

while the
\varepsilon
-
\ell_{\infty}
mixing time is defined as

t^{\infty}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon):=\inf\{t\geq 0:\max_{x\in V}\left\|p_{t}(x,\cdot)-\pi\right\|_{\infty,\pi}\leq\varepsilon\}.

Under Definition
2.7
, Corollary
2.6
can be restated as
t^{\infty}_{\mathrm{mix}}(1/|V|)\leq 2t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log|V|
. We also have the following inequality

\max_{x\in V}\left\|p_{Lt^{\infty}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\varepsilon)}(x,\cdot)-\pi\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq(2\varepsilon)^{L}

(2.6)

for any integer
L\geq 1
.
See
[
21
, Equation (4.30)]
.

In the case of a SRW on a graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
,
one way to control
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
is through the
vertex expansion constant

\kappa(\mathcal{G})
, defined as

\kappa(\mathcal{G}):=\min_{0<|S|\leq\frac{|V|}{2}}\frac{|\partial S|}{|S|},

(2.7)

where
\partial S:=\{v\in V\setminus S:\exists u\in S,u\sim v\}
is the out-boundary of
S
. We have the continuous-time analogue of Cheeger’s inequality.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any graph
\mathcal{G}
, its spectral gap for a
SRW
is lower bounded by
\frac{\kappa(\mathcal{G})^{2}}{2d_{\max}}
where
d_{\max}
is the maximal degree of vertices in
\mathcal{G}
.

For a proof of the discrete-time version of Lemma
2.8
one can see Lemma 3.3.7 in
[
25
]
.

For future reference we record the following result about transition probabilities on graphs.
It is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.8
.

Lemma 2.9
.

For any
\kappa_{0}
and
\bar{d}>0
, there is a constant
C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}
such that the following is true.
For any graph
\mathcal{G}
such that the maximum degree
d_{\max}\leq\bar{d}
and
\kappa(\mathcal{G})>\kappa_{0}
,
we have

\forall\,t\leq|V|,\qquad\frac{\max_{x}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{x}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s}+\max_{x}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s<C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}.

2.2.3
Kac’s formula and an exponential approximation for Markov chains

In this paper we will frequently use the following Kac’s formula. Consider the case where
V
is finite. Let
A,B\subset V
. Let
Q(A,B):=\sum_{x\in A,y\in B}\pi(x)r_{x,y}
. The ‘exit-measure’
\nu_{A}
is defined as

\nu_{A}(x):=\frac{\sum_{y\in A}\pi(y)r_{y,x}}{Q(A,A^{c})}=\lim_{t\to 0}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X_{t}=x\mid X_{0}\in A,X_{t}\notin A).

(2.8)

Note that when
A
is a singleton, this definition coincides with the one given in Definition
1.2
.

Lemma 2.10
.

For all
t\geq 0
and
A\subsetneq V
,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T_{A}\in(t,t+\mathrm{d}t))=Q(A,A^{c})\mathbb{P}_{\nu_{A}}(T_{A}>t)\mathrm{d}t,

(2.9)

where
T_{A}:=\inf\{s\geq 0:X_{s}\in A\}
is the hitting time of
A
.
Integrating this equation over
t>0
we get

\pi(A^{c})=Q(A,A^{c})\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{A}}(T_{A}).

(2.10)

This lemma can be found in many places, e.g.,
(
2.9
) is
[
2
, Equation (2.23)]
and (
2.10
) is
[
2
, Equation (2.24)]
.
6
6

6

Note there is a typo in their definition of
Q(A,A^{c})
between (2.23) and (2.24) in
[
2
]
:
q_{ij}
should be replaced by
\pi_{i}q_{ij}
there.

A powerful tool for controlling the cumulative distribution function as well as the probability density function of
T_{A}
with initial condition
\pi
is
the following classical exponential approximation result, due to Aldous and Brown
[
1
]
. It will also be used extensively throughout this paper. We state it as a lemma here.

Lemma 2.11
(Exponential approximation for hitting times
[
1
]
).

For an irreducible
reversible Markov chain on a finite state space
V
with stationary distribution
\pi
and

A\subset V
, if we denote the hitting time of
A
by
T_{A}
and its density function w.r.t. the stationary chain by
f_{T_{A}}
, then

\left|\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T_{A}>t)-\exp\left(-\frac{t}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}},

(2.11)

and

\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}+t}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\right)\leq f_{T_{A}}(t)\leq\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{2t}\right).

(2.12)

As a consequence of (
2.11
), for
t\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}
, we have

\left|\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T_{A}\leq t)-\frac{t}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\right|\leq\left(\frac{t}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}\right)^{2}+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T_{A}}.

(2.13)

Equations (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) are due to Theorem 1 and Lemma 13 in
[
1
]
, whereas (
2.13
) follows from (
2.11
) and the inequality that
1-x\leq e^{-x}\leq 1-x+x^{2}
for
0\leq x\leq 1
.

We note that, very recently Berestycki, Hermon and Teyssier give an elementary proof of a stronger version of Lemma
2.11
. See
[
6
, Chapter 4]
.

2.2.4
Relations between Markov chain parameters

We will need the following lemma, giving relations between
t_{\mathrm{meet}},t_{\mathrm{rel}}
and
r_{\max}:=\max_{x\in V}r(x)
for a finite Markov chain.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
\left|V\right|=n\geq 2
and symmetric jump rates
\mathbf{r}
, we have

t_{\mathrm{rel}}\geq 1/(2r_{\max}),

(2.14)

\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4},

(2.15)

p_{s}(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{2}(p_{s}(x,x)+p_{s}(y,y)),\,\forall x,y\in V.

(2.16)

2.2.5
Bounds for meeting probabilities

For future reference, we record the following lemma which controls the probability for two walks to collide within some time interval.

Lemma 2.13
.

Assume that
\mathbf{r}
is symmetric. For any
x,y\in V
with
x\neq y
and any
0<T<t
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{x,y}(T<\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}<t)\leq 2\exp(-T/t_{\mathrm{rel}})\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}+\frac{8t}{n}(T^{-1}\vee r_{\max}).

Here recall that
\mathbb{P}_{x,y}
denotes two independent continuous-time random walks starting from
x
and
y
respectively.

2.2.6
Meeting time and integral of transition probability for transitive chains

In the setting of transitive chains, the quantity
\int_{0}^{t}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s
satisfies, for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{rel}}
,

n\int_{0}^{t}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\mbox{Tr}(p_{2s})\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp(-2\lambda_{i}s)\mathrm{d}s\in\left[\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}+t,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}+t\right],

(2.17)

where
n=|V|
, and
\lambda_{1}=0<\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n}\leq 2r_{\mathrm{max}}
are the eigenvalues of
-Q
.
Moreover,

n\int_{0}^{t}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\leq t+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{1}{2\lambda_{i}}

holds for all
t\geq 0
.
The eigentime identity (see, e.g.,
[
21
, Lemma 12.17]
) asserts that for all
x
,

\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}=\sum_{y}\pi(y)\mathbb{E}_{x}T_{y},

where
T_{y}
is the hitting time of
y
.
For transitive chains we have
\mathbb{E}_{x}T_{y}=2\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}
for all
x,y
(see, e.g.,
[
2
, Section 14.2]
). Hence

\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}=2t_{\mathrm{meet}}.

(2.18)

Using (
2.17
) and (
2.18
)
we see that for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{rel}}
,

\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{2n}\leq\int_{0}^{t}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mbox{Tr}(p_{2s})\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}+t}{n}.

(2.19)

2.2.7
Uniformly transient Markov chains

We state the following proposition which we will use in the proof of Theorem
1.11
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
be a sequence of uniformly transient transitive Markov chains with
r_{n}(x)=1
for all
n
and
x\in V_{n}
, and
|V_{n}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Then we have

t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=o(t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n}))\mbox{ and }t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=\Theta(\left|V_{n}\right|),

(2.20)

as
n\to\infty
.

3
General bounds on
P_{t}

In this section, we present an upper and a lower bound for
P_{t}
. For many families of graphs, including all transitive graphs, the two bounds differ only by some absolute constant.
As discussed in the introduction, while these bounds are interesting in their own rights, the upper bound will play a key role in the proofs of our main results.

Recall that
p_{t}(x,y)
is the time
t
transition probability from
x
to
y
for
(V,\mathbf{r})
.

Theorem 3.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C_{0}
such that for every Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
,

\frac{m_{t}}{C_{0}t}\leq P_{t}(x)\leq\frac{C_{0}M_{t}}{t},\qquad\forall x\in V,\;t>0,

(3.1)

where

M_{t}=\sup_{x\in V}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\quad m_{t}=\inf_{x\in V}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s.

(3.2)

Corollary 3.2
.

There exists an absolute constant
C_{0}
such that for every transitive Markov chain,

\frac{1}{C_{0}}\frac{\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s}{t}\leq P_{t}\leq C_{0}\frac{\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s}{t},\qquad\forall t>0.

(3.3)

Remark 3.3
.

We comment that the ratio
\frac{\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s}{t}
has two neat probabilistic interpretations:

•

It is the probability that a random walk is at the origin at time
T\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,t]
.

•

For a transitive chain, it can be shown that up to a universal constant (independent of the chain and
t
) its inverse is proportional to the expected number of vertices visited by time
t
.

We assume that
\sum_{y}r_{x,y}=1
. Using a standard coupling between the continuous-time Markov chain and the corresponding discrete-time Markov chain, it is easy to show that
\sum_{i=0}^{t}P^{i}(o,o)\asymp\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s
, where
P^{t}
is the transition probability for the discrete time Markov chain; and that the expected number of vertices visited by time
t
for the discrete- and continuous-time chains are also comparable up to a universal constant. We omit the details.

Hence we may work in discrete time.
Let
J_{i}
be the indicator that the vertex visited at time
i
is visited at least
16\sum_{s=0}^{t}P^{s}(o,o)
times during
\{i,i+1,\ldots,t\}
. The probability of this event is at most
1/16
by Markov’s inequality. Hence with probability at least
3/4
there are at most
(t+1)/4
times such that the event
J_{i}
occurs. On this event the number of vertices visited by time
t
is at least
\frac{t+1}{64\sum_{i=0}^{t}P^{i}(o,o)}
deterministically.

We now prove the upper bound on
\mathbb{E}|R_{t}|
, where
R_{t}
is the set of states visited by the chain by time
t
. Let
N_{x}(s)
be the amount of time spent at state
x
by time
s
. Then

\mathbb{P}(x\in R_{t})=\mathbb{P}(|N_{x}(t)|\geq 1)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}|N_{x}(2t)|}{\mathbb{E}(|N_{x}(2t)|\mid|N_{x}(t)|\geq 1)}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}|N_{x}(2t)|}{\sum_{s=0}^{t}P^{s}(o,o)}.

Summing over
x
yields
\mathbb{E}|R_{t}|\leq 2t/\sum_{s=0}^{t}P^{s}(o,o)
as desired.
∎

We note that the assertion of Corollary
3.2
is valid for many other graphs. In some cases, the constant
C_{0}
has to depend on the graph or on the family of considered graphs. For instance, with some effort one can prove this is the case for a roughly transitive graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
(meaning that for some
K,K^{\prime}>0
, for all
x,y\in V
, the rooted graphs
(V,E,x)
and
(V,E,y)
are
(K,K^{\prime})
-quasi isometric
7
7

7

Two rooted graphs
\mathcal{G}=(V,E,o)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},o^{\prime})
are said to be
(K,K^{\prime})
- quasi isometric if there exists a map
\phi
from
V
to
V^{\prime}
s.t.
\phi(o)=o^{\prime}
and
K^{-1}\mbox{dist}(x,y)-K^{\prime}\leq\mbox{dist}(\phi(x),\phi(y))\leq K\mbox{dist}(x,y)+K^{\prime}
for all
x,y\in V
.
) of bounded degree.

We define a modified Poisson model in the following way. We fix a time
T>0
. Recall the definition of
M_{t}
in Equation (
3.2
). Define
\lambda=\log(T/M_{T})+2
. At the beginning, we put
\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)
particles independently on each site. We assign to each particle
x
a uniform random variable
U_{x}\sim\text{Unif}[0,1]
independently. We partition the particles into disjoint sets
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{i},\ldots
by defining
A_{i}=\{x:U_{x}\in(2^{-i},2^{-i+1}]\}
. Define
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
to be such that
2^{m-1}<\lambda/4\leq 2^{m}
. Define
t_{0}=t_{1}=\cdots=t_{m}=0
. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, define
t_{i+m}=2^{i+2}M_{T}
and
S_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}t_{j}
. For the convenience of notations, we set
S_{0}=0
. For
i\geq m+1
, we define Round
i
to be the time period
(S_{i-1},S_{i}]
. And we let Round 1 to Round
m
all happen at time
0
. In the modified Poisson model, each particle performs an independent SRW as in the original model but we only allow a specific type of coalescence in each round. Namely, in Round
i
, for particles
x\in A_{j}
and
y\in A_{i}
with
j<i
, if they meet, the particle
x
is removed. In other words the particle
x
‘coalesces into’
y
. No other type of coalescence is allowed in Round
i
.

As above, we use
P_{t}(x)
to denote the density of surviving particles at site
x
at time
t
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(x\mbox{ is occupied at time }t)
in the CRW model. And we define
\hat{P}_{t}(x)
to be the expected number of surviving particles at site
x
at time
t
for the modified Poisson model. We claim that

\displaystyle P_{t}(x)\leq\hat{P}_{t}(x)+e^{-\lambda}.

To prove it, we define a second modified Poisson model to be the same as the modified Poisson model except we allow all coalescences at any time. Then the second modified Poisson model is the same as putting
\mathrm{Ber}(1-e^{-\lambda})
particles per site independently at the beginning (
t=0
) and run CRW. Let
R_{t}
denote the set of surviving particles at time
t
in the modified Poisson model and let
R_{t}^{\prime}
be the second modified Poisson model. Then we can couple the two model so that
R_{t}^{\prime}\subset R_{t}
for any
t
almost surely. See Proposition 3.2 in
[
23
]
for a similar treatment. This readily implies the inequality because for the original CRW model. Indeed, we can color each particle red independently with probability
e^{-\lambda}
. If we do not allow red particles to coalesce, then the density of particles is the same as that for the second modified Poisson model plus
e^{-\lambda}
, which is bounded by that for the modified Poisson model plus
e^{-\lambda}
.

Define
\hat{P}_{t}(v,j)
to be the expected number of particles from
A_{j}
at site
v
at time
t
. Then for any
i>0
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\text{number of particles at }v\text{ from }\cup_{j=i+m}^{\infty}A_{j}\text{ at time }S_{i+m})+\sum_{j=1}^{i+m-1}\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v,j)

(3.4)

\displaystyle=\lambda 2^{-(i+m-1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{i+m-1}\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v,j),

(3.5)

since during the time period
[0,S_{i+m}]
, particles from
\cup_{j=i+m}^{\infty}A_{j}
are not removed, and at time
S_{i+m}
the number of particles at
v
from
\cup_{j=i+m}^{\infty}A_{j}
follows Poisson
(\lambda 2^{-(i+m-1)})
.

For
j\leq m
, with
m
defined as above, we consider the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
v\in V
, the number of particles at site
v
from
A_{j}
that survives at time
0
has the following distribution: with probability
\exp(-\lambda 2^{-j}+\lambda 2^{-m})
it follows Poisson
(\lambda 2^{-j})
and with probability
1-\exp(-\lambda 2^{-j}+\lambda 2^{-m})
it is
0
.

The event that
x\in A_{j}
survives at time
0
is equivalent to that no particle from
\cup_{\ell=j+1}^{m}A_{\ell}
starts at the same position as
x
. By a straightforward computation, we have the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

For
i>0
, let
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of the trajectories of the particles in
\cup_{j=1}^{i}A_{j}
up to time
S_{i}
. For
\ell>m
such that
S_{\ell}\leq T
and
j<\ell
, for any particle
x
from
A_{j}
that survives to time
S_{\ell-1}
, we have that almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(x\text{ survives to time }S_{\ell}|\mathcal{F}_{\ell-1})\leq\frac{1}{4}.

The lemma will be proven later. With this lemma, we have for any
i>0
with
S_{i+m}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v,j)\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda 2^{-j}\exp\left(-\lambda 2^{-j}+\lambda 2^{-m}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp(-\log(2)j-\lambda 2^{-j}+\lambda 2^{-m})

\displaystyle(\text{using }\lambda>2^{m+1})\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp(-\log(2)j-2^{m-j+1}+2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i}\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp(\log(2)(j-2m))\leq 2\lambda 2^{-m}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i},

as well as

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m+1}^{i+m-1}\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v,j)\leq\sum_{j=m+1}^{i+m-1}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{i+m-j}\lambda 2^{-j}\leq\lambda 2^{-m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}.

Combine the two estimates with (
3.5
), we have that

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v)\leq 5\lambda 2^{-m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\leq 20\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}.

Note that the same bound holds for
i=0
trivially. Now, take
i=\lceil\log_{2}(\frac{T}{M_{T}}+8)\rceil-4
. Then
S_{i+m}<T\leq S_{i+m+1}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{T}(v)\leq\hat{P}_{S_{i+m}}(v)\leq 20\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\leq\frac{160M_{T}}{T}.

So

\displaystyle P_{T}(v)\leq\frac{160M_{T}}{T}+e^{-\lambda}\leq\frac{CM_{T}}{T}.

This implies the upper bound (since
v
is any vertex in
V
and
T
is any positive real number). Now we prove Lemma
3.5
.

Consider a particle
x\in A_{j}
. For
\ell>m
such that
S_{\ell}\leq T
and
j<\ell
, we want to study the probability that
x
survives during the time interval
[S_{\ell-1},S_{\ell}]
. We let
x
continue to perform a random walk with rate
\mathbf{r}
independently after colliding with particles in
A_{\ell}
if this happens and define
\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)
to be the (Lebesgue measure of the) time units that particles from
A_{\ell}
spent together with
x
during
[S_{\ell-1},S_{\ell}]
(to put it differently, we pretend that the particle
x
picks its trajectory in advance, and define
\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)
w.r.t. this trajectory, without terminating it when
x
should be removed). As
x
plays no further rule in the model after a collision, the event
\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0
