is well defined in the probability space we work with. We have the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)}{\mathbb{E}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)|\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(S_{\ell}-S_{\ell-1})\lambda 2^{-\ell}}{\mathbb{E}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)|\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)}.

Here the last equality follows from the observation that on each site, the number of particles from
A_{\ell}
follows Poisson
(\lambda 2^{-\ell})
distribution independently. See Fact 2.1 in
[
18
]
for more explanations. Note that the denominator

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)|\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)=\int_{S_{\ell-1}}^{S_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}(N_{\ell}(x)|\text{ the first collision is at time }s)\bar{f}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

where
\bar{f}(s)
is the probability density of the first collision time during
[S_{\ell-1},S_{\ell}]
given such a collision happens.
We use the following easy fact to control the expectation in the r.h.s..

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
X
be a random variable whose distribution is given by
\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)
conditioned on being positive, then
X
is stochastically dominated by
1+\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)
.

To see this, consider the number of points in
[0,1]
for a rate
\lambda
Poisson process. Given that the first point is at some
x\in[0,1]
, the number of additional points has a Poisson
(\lambda(1-x))
distribution, and is stochastically dominated by Poisson
(\lambda)
. See Section 3 of
[
18
]
for a similar treatment.
∎Fix the path that
x
takes during
[S_{\ell-1},s]
(for some
S_{\ell-1}\leq s\leq S_{\ell}
). Let
W_{v,\ell}(s)
be the set of particles from
A_{\ell}
at
v
at time
s
. Consider the conditional law of
(|W_{v,\ell}|)_{v\in V}
given that no particle from
A_{\ell}
visits the space-time coordinates
\{(x(t),t):t\in[S_{\ell-1},s]\}
. Here
x(\cdot)
is the trajectory of particle
x
. Then
|W_{v,\ell}|
for each
v\in V
is Poisson distributed with parameter less than or equal to
2^{-\ell}\lambda
, and all the
|W_{v,\ell}|
are independent. This can be obtained by a Poisson thinning argument and Fact 2.1 in
[
18
]
. Now, by Lemma
3.6
, given that the first collision occurs at time
s
, we have that the joint law of
(|W_{v,\ell}(s)|)_{v\in V}
is stochastically dominated by
(Y_{v})_{v\in V}
, where each
Y_{v}
is independent with
\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda 2^{-\ell})+\mathbbm{1}[v=x(s)]
distribution. (To see this, consider the following two cases. The first is that
x
jumps to
x(s)
right on time
s
, then we apply Lemma
3.6
. The second case is that
x
comes to
x(s)
before time
s
and stays there until at least
s
, then the number of particle from
A_{\ell}
at
x(s)
is
1
, which is also stochastically dominated by
Y
.) Define
\bar{f}(s,y)
to be the joint probability density of the first collision time and place, given that the first collision happens during
[S_{\ell-1},S_{\ell}]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)|\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int_{S_{\ell-1}}^{S_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}(N_{\ell}(x)|\text{ the first collision is at time }s\text{ and at }y)\bar{f}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int_{S_{\ell-1}}^{S_{\ell}}\left(t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}+\sum_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell}-(s-S_{\ell-1})}p_{t}(y,z)p_{t}(y,z)\mathrm{d}t\right)\bar{f}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int_{S_{\ell-1}}^{S_{\ell}}\left(t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}+\int_{0}^{t_{\ell}-(s-S_{\ell-1})}p_{2t}(y,y)\mathrm{d}t\right)\bar{f}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\int_{S_{\ell-1}}^{S_{\ell}}\left(t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}+\sup_{z\in V}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell}}p_{2t}(z,z)\mathrm{d}t\right)\bar{f}(s,y)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}+\frac{M_{2t_{\ell}}}{2},

where we used the fact that
p_{t}(y,z)=p_{t}(z,y)
to get the fourth line from the third line. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)}{\mathbb{E}(\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)|\overline{N}_{\ell}(x)>0)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}}{t_{\ell}\lambda 2^{-\ell}+M_{2t_{\ell}}/2}

\displaystyle\text{(using }\lambda\geq 2^{m+1},t_{\ell}\leq T\text{ and }t_{\ell}=2^{\ell+2-m}M_{T}\text{)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
3.5
and hence the proof of the upper bound in (
3.1
).
∎

We consider a CRW model, where at the beginning (
t=0
), at each site independently we have one particle with probability
\frac{m_{t}}{8t}
. For each
t>0
and site
x
,

\displaystyle P_{t}(x)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(x\text{ is occupied at time }t)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(x\text{ is occupied at time }t\text{ by a particle that hasn't collided with anyone})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\text{the particle from }x\text{ at time }0\text{ survives without colliding by time }t),

where the last equality comes from the reversibility of
(V,\mathbf{r})
. Then the above equals

\displaystyle\frac{m_{t}}{8t}\mathbb{P}(\text{the particle from }x\text{ has no collision by time }t|\text{there is a particle at $x$ at time }0).

To bound this conditional probability from below, we let the particles not coalesce with one another upon collisions, i.e., they continue to perform random walks independently. By a straightforward coupling argument, this cannot increase the probability that the particle from
x
has not collided with another particle by time
t
. Let
\tilde{N}_{t}
be the time units that other particles spend together with the particle from
x
during
[0,t]
, given that there is a particle at
x
at time
0
. Then we just need to lower bound
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{N}_{t}=0)
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tilde{N}_{t}>0)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{N}_{2t}}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{N}_{2t}|\tilde{N}_{t}>0)}.

The numerator satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\tilde{N}_{2t}\leq\frac{m_{t}}{8t}2t\leq\frac{m_{2t}}{4}.

The denominator satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\tilde{N}_{2t}|\tilde{N}_{t}>0)\geq\int_{0}^{2t-s}p_{2u}(y,y)\mathrm{d}u\geq\int_{0}^{t}p_{2u}(y,y)\mathrm{d}u\geq\frac{m_{2t}}{2},

where we assume the first collision is at time
s\in[0,t]
at vertex
y
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tilde{N}_{t}>0)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus we have

\displaystyle P_{t}(x)=\mathbb{P}(x\text{ is occupied at time }t)\geq\frac{m_{t}}{16t},

which gives us the lower bound.
∎

4
A general framework: moments, reversibility, and branching structures

In this section we set up a framework to analyze the decay of the density of particles.

4.1
The decay of density and moments of
N_{t}

Recall in Lemma
2.1

we established that
P_{t}=\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{-1}
, where
N_{t}
is the number of particles that have coalesced with the particle starting from a uniform location
\mathcal{U}
up to time
t
. We can rewrite this formula as

P_{t}=\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{-1}=(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}\right)^{-1}\right).

If we could determine
each term in the above product
up to smaller order terms then we would
have a good control of
P_{t}
.
Our general strategy is similar to that in
[
7
]
. A crucial difficulty we encounter is that we do not have Sawyer’s Theorem (which is a crucial component of the analysis in
[
7
]
) at our disposal, which for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
approximates all moments of
N_{t}
. We will
estimate the
k
-th moment
\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}
for any
k
and use the method of moments to show that the distribution of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}
is close to the Gamma
(2,2)
distribution. We will need to take care of the behavior of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}
near
0
when computing the limit of
\mathbb{E}((N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1})
. We will show that under some conditions,
\mathbb{E}\left((N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1}\right)
will be close to 2.

For the time being, we concentrate on controlling
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})
.
Let the state space
V
be finite, and be denoted as
V=[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
It follows from the definition of
N_{t}
and the graphical representation of the model, which is implicitly used in the second equality that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})&=\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{x\in[n]}\mathbbm{1}[\mbox{the particles starting at }x\mbox{ and }\mathcal{U}\mbox{ have coalesced by time }t]\right)^{k}\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k+1}\in V}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}[X_{i}(0)=x_{i},\forall 1\leq i\leq k+1]\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})\leq t]\right)\\
&=n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes k+1}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})\leq t),\end{split}

(4.1)

where
\pi^{\otimes k+1}
means that initially the
k+1
particles are independently uniformly distributed.
Observe that to estimate the r.h.s. for small values of
t
it does not suffice to estimate
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes k+1}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})\leq t)
up to an
o(1)
additive error. This is the reason we cannot rely on the elegant techniques developed by Oliveira in
[
24
]
, which allow one to estimate this probability up to some
o(1)
additive error, when
t_{\mathrm{mix}}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
. The
o(1)
error is much larger than the probability in question, unless
t_{\mathrm{meet}}/t
is not too large.

In particular, for
k=1
we have

\mathbb{E}(N_{t})=n\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes 2}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{1},X_{2})\leq t).

(4.2)

To control this probability, we use the exponential approximation result, i.e., equation (
2.13
), and
consider the product chain of two independent random walks.
Let
A
be the diagonal set
\{(x,x):x\in V\}
.
The relaxation time of the product chain is the same as the original chain. The coalescence time for a pair of independent walks has the same distribution as the hitting time of
A
of the product chain.
It follows from equation (
2.13
) that for
t\leq t_{\mathrm{meet}}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}N_{t}-\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|\leq n\left(\left(\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{2}+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right).

(4.3)

Note that the right hand side is
o\left(\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)
when
t\vee t_{\mathrm{rel}}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
, so it is also
o(\mathbb{E}N_{t})
.

To obtain estimates on
\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}
for
k\geq 2
, or equivalently
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(C_{1}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})\leq t)
, we use reversibility of random walks.
Specifically, if
X(s),0\leq s\leq t
is a random walk evolving according to rates
\mathbf{r}
and starting from a uniform initial location, then the reversed path
\gamma(s):=X(t-s),0\leq s\leq t
is also a random walk path that starts from a uniform initial location and has jump rates
\mathbf{r}
(because
\mathbf{r}
is symmetric).
After reversal of time, the collision events become ‘branching’ events, i.e., if particle
i
jumps to particle
j
at time
t
, then after reversal of time this corresponds to that particle
j
gives birth to particle
i
. We will define branching structures in the next section to study this.

4.2
Reversal of time and branching structures

In this section, we define branching structures to study the probability density of the coalescing time of
k+1
particles. We set

I_{k}=[i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}],

where
i_{0},\ldots,i_{k}
is a sequence of integers satisfying
i_{0}=0
, and
0\leq i_{\ell}\leq\ell-1
,
\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
We use
\Phi_{k}
to denote the collection of all such
I_{k}
.
We write

\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}:=\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}):0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}<t\}
,
and let
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}
be the
k
dimensional Lebesgue measure on it.
Take
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
.
For each
0\leq\ell\leq k
, we define
\gamma_{\ell}
to be a random walk path from time
t_{\ell}
to
t
. We do this inductively. First,
\gamma_{0}:[0,t]\to V
is defined to be a continuous-time Markov chain evolving according to
(V,\mathbf{r})
, whose starting location
\gamma_{0}(0)
is uniformly distributed.
Then for
\ell\geq 1
, let
A_{\ell}:=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t_{\ell})
.
Conditioned on
A_{\ell}
, we let

B_{\ell}
be a random vertex sampled from the distribution
\nu_{A_{\ell}}
. Here we recall the exit measure
\nu_{v}

defined by

\nu_{v}(u):=\frac{r_{v,u}}{r(v)},

where
r(v):=\sum_{x}r_{v,x}
is the total transition rate at
v
. We define
\gamma_{\ell}:[t_{\ell},t]\to V
to be a random walk starting at
B_{\ell}
.
Using this branching structure we can define

h(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k}):=\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}r(A_{i})\mathbbm{1}[\forall\,0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq k,\;\forall\,t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t],\;\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime})]\right),

(4.4)

which, intuitively, describes the probability that no coalescence happens for all branches, weighted by the transition rates.

We may think of the above as follows. At time
t_{j}
a particle is born at a random position
\sim\nu_{A_{j}}
(a location adjacent to the current position of the particle
i_{j}
). This particle can be thought of as an offspring of the particle
i_{j}
.
The offspring particle is then required to avoid all other particles by time
t
. Upon reversal of time, the times at which particles are created correspond to collision times.
For simplicity of notations, we shall usually omit the dependence on
t
, and just write
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=h(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k})
.

We have the following lemma relating
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})
and the branching structure.

Lemma 4.1
.

For
k\geq 1
, let
X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}
be coalescing random walks. Then we have

\displaystyle n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t,\forall 0\leq i<j\leq k,

\displaystyle\,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))

\displaystyle=(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}
means that the initial locations
X_{0}(0),\ldots,X_{k}(0)
are random, and are independently uniformly distributed.

We say a
X
‘jumps onto’ another particle
Y
to create a collision if for some
t>0
,
X_{t-}\neq X_{t},Y_{t-}=Y_{t},X_{t}=Y_{t}
. We set the following convention that
after the coalescence the new particle keeps the label of
Y
.
Now let us
consider all possible ways that particles jump onto other particles to create the
k
collisions. For
1\leq m\leq k
, we assume that
X_{j_{m}}
jumps to
X_{\ell_{m}}
for the
m
-th collision. Then clearly
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}
have to satisfy the conditions that
all
j_{m}
are mutually distinct numbers chosen from
\{0,\ldots,k\}
and each
\ell_{m}
belongs to the set
\{0,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\}
. Conversely, given any sequence of numbers
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k})
satisfying the above conditions, we have that for any
1\leq m\leq k
, with positive probability, the particle with label
j_{m}
jumps onto the particle with label
\ell_{m}
for the
m
-th collision.
We use
\hat{\Phi}_{k}
to denote the collection of all such vectors, and we call each
\hat{I}_{k}\in\hat{\Phi}_{k}
a ‘collision pattern’.
We use
L(a,b,s)
to denote the event that the particle with label
a
jumps onto the particle with label
b
at time
s
. Then for any
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
and
\hat{I}_{k}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k})
, we set

\hat{L}((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})=\cap_{m=1}^{k}L(j_{m},\ell_{m},s_{m}).

Using
f((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})
to denote the probability density of the event
L((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t,\forall\,0\leq i<j\leq k,

\displaystyle\,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\sum_{\hat{I}_{k}\in\hat{\Phi}_{k}}f((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}.

By the invariance of
f((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})
w.r.t. the permutation of indices of the particles, we only need to deal with
\hat{I}_{k}
where
j_{m}=k+1-m
, for any
1\leq m\leq k
. For such
\hat{I}_{k}
we can associate an
I_{k}
with it
by setting
i_{m}=\ell_{k+1-m}
for each
1\leq m\leq k
, and
i_{0}=0
. One can check that
(i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
is indeed an element of the set
\Phi_{k}
.
Setting
t_{m}=t-s_{k+1-m}
, we have
L(j_{m},\ell_{m},s_{m})=L(k+1-m,i_{k+1-m},t-t_{k+1-m})
, so

\hat{L}((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}),\hat{I}_{k})=\cap_{m=1}^{k}L(m,i_{m},t-t_{m}),

which we also denote by
L((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})
. We also use
f((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})
to denote the probability density of the event
L((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})
.
Thus

\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t,\forall\,0\leq i<j\leq k,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))\\
=(k+1)!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}f((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}.

Thus to prove Lemma
4.1
it suffices to show that

f((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})=n^{-k}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}).

Define the event

F(m,i_{m},B_{m},A_{m},t-t_{m})=\{X_{m}(t-t_{m})=B_{m},X_{i_{m}}(t-t_{m})=A_{m}\},

and set

\begin{split}F_{k}=F_{k}(\{A_{m},B_{m},i_{m},t-t_{m}\}_{m=1}^{k})=\cap_{m=1}^{k}F(m,i_{m},B_{m},A_{m},t-t_{m}).\end{split}

(4.5)

We write
f_{k}(\{A_{m},B_{m}\}_{m=1}^{k})
as a shorthand for the probability density of
F_{k}
. Then we can write

f((t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}),I_{k})
as the sum
over all possible collision locations:

\sum_{\{A_{m},B_{m}\}_{m=1}^{k}}f_{k}\left(\{A_{m},B_{m}\}_{m=1}^{k}\right).

Since the jump rate from any
B_{m}
to
A_{m}
is
r_{B_{m},{A_{m}}}
, and

\mathbb{P}(X_{m}(t-t_{m})=B_{m})=1/n
(as
X_{m}(t-t_{m})
is uniformly random), by reversal of time, we see the above sum is equal to

n^{-k}\sum_{A_{m},B_{m},\forall 1\leq m\leq k}\left(\prod_{m=1}^{k}r_{B_{m},A_{m}}\right)\mathbb{P}(\hat{\gamma}_{i_{m}}(t_{m})=A_{m},\forall 1\leq m\leq k,\textrm{all }\hat{\gamma}_{m}\textrm{'s don't collide}),

(4.6)

where

\hat{\gamma}_{m}:[t_{m},t]\to V
is the reversal of

X_{m}(s)
for
s\in[0,t-t_{m}]
conditioned on

X_{m}(t-t_{m})=B_{m}
.
Note that
\hat{\gamma}_{m}
also performs a continuous-time random walk by reversibility, and
X_{m}
performs an independent random walk until a collision happens. We have the indicator
\mathbbm{1}[\textrm{all }\hat{\gamma}_{m}\textrm{'s don't collide}]
in our equation because collisions cannot happen outside
\{t-t_{m},1\leq m\leq k\}
.
Moreover, by writing

r_{B_{m},A_{m}}
as

r(A_{m})\cdot(r_{B_{m},A_{m}})/r(A_{m})
we see
that (
4.6
) is exactly equal to
n^{-k}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
. This completes the proof of Lemma

4.1
. ∎

We have the following corollary of Lemma
4.1
, which is about two or more particles starting from the same place.
Let
\Xi
be any partition of
\{0,1,\ldots,k-1,k\}
, represented as a collection of subsets of
\{0,1,\ldots,k-1,k\}
.
For
0\leq i<j\leq k
we write
i\leftrightarrow j
if
i
and
j
belong to the same set of
\Xi
and
i\nleftrightarrow j
otherwise.

Corollary 4.2
.

For
k\geq 1
and
\left|\Xi\right|\geq 2
we have

n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t;\forall i\leftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)=X_{j}(0);\forall i\nleftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))\\
=\left|\Xi\right|!\sum_{I_{\left|\Xi\right|-1}\in\Phi_{\left|\Xi\right|-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\left|\Xi\right|-1}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{\left|\Xi\right|-1})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}.

Using Lemma
4.1
we have

\begin{split}&n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t;\forall i\leftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)=X_{j}(0);\forall i\nleftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))\\
=&n^{\left|\Xi\right|-1}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(\left|\Xi\right|)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{\left|\Xi\right|-1})\leq t;\forall 0\leq i<j\leq\left|\Xi\right|-1,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))\\
=&\left|\Xi\right|!\sum_{I_{\left|\Xi\right|-1}\in\Phi_{\left|\Xi\right|-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\left|\Xi\right|-1}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{\left|\Xi\right|-1})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}.\end{split}

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎Lemma
4.1
is the special case of Corollary
4.2
,
where
i\nleftrightarrow j
for any
0\leq i<j\leq k
.
For the case where
i\leftrightarrow j
for any
0\leq i<j\leq k
, i.e.,
\left|\Xi\right|=1
, we have

\begin{split}&n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t;\forall i\leftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)=X_{j}(0))\\
=&n^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\forall\,\,0\leq i<j\leq k,X_{i}(0)=X_{j}(0))=1.\end{split}

By summing over all possible partitions
\Xi
and using Corollary
4.2
,
we get the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

For
k\geq 1
, we have

\begin{split}0&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t)-n^{-k}(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k-1}(k+1)^{\ell+1}n^{-k}(\ell+1)!\sum_{I_{\ell}\in\Phi_{\ell}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{\ell})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}+n^{-k}.\end{split}

(4.7)

Note that we have the decomposition

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t)\\
=&\sum_{\Xi}\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{\otimes(k+1)}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t,\forall i\leftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)=X_{j}(0);\forall i\nleftrightarrow j,X_{i}(0)\neq X_{j}(0))\end{split}

(4.8)

where the sum of
\Xi
is over all partitions of the set
\{0,1,\ldots,k-1,k\}
.
Equation (
4.7
) now follows by applying Corollary
4.2
, and using the trivial upper bound that the number of partition
\Xi
of the set
\{0,1,\ldots,k-1,k\}
s.t.
\left|\Xi\right|=\ell+1
is at most
(k+1)^{\ell+1}
.
∎We record the following lemma which will be used in Section
6
.
We recall the notation for maximal and minimal jump rate:

r_{\max}=\max_{x\in V}r(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad r_{\min}=\min_{x\in V}r(x).

Throughout, we denote their ratio by

R:=r_{\max}/r_{\min}.

Recall that
\gamma_{\ell}:[t_{\ell},t]\to V
is a random walk starting at
B_{\ell}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
\ell
, and any
s\in[t_{\ell},t]
,
x\in V
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(s)=x)\leq\frac{R^{\ell}}{n}.

We use the fact that for any initial measure
\mu_{0}
and any symmetric Markov transition kernel,
\sup_{x}\mu_{s}(x)
is not non-increasing in
s
. Indeed,

\begin{split}\sup_{x}\mu_{s}(x)&\leq\sup_{x}\sum_{y}\mu_{0}(y)p_{s}(y,x)\\
&\leq\sup_{x}\sum_{y}(\sup_{z}\mu_{0}(z))p_{s}(x,y)=\sup_{z}\mu_{0}(z).\end{split}

(4.9)

We now prove Lemma
4.4
inductively. The case of
\ell=0
follows from the fact that
\gamma_{0}(t_{0})
is sampled from the uniform distribution. Suppose that this lemma holds for
\ell\leq m-1
. Then for
\ell=m
we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{m}(t_{m})=x)&=\sum_{y}\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{i_{m}}(t)=y)\frac{r_{y,x}}{r(y)}\\
\leq&\frac{R^{m-1}}{n}\sum_{y}\frac{r_{y,x}}{r_{\min}}=\frac{R^{m-1}}{n}\frac{r(x)}{r_{\min}}\leq\frac{R^{m}}{n}\end{split}

(4.10)

This proves the induction step and hence completes the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
∎

4.3
Intermediate quantities

We now estimate the integral of
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
. We let
\mathcal{U}\sim\pi
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{U}}
. We also let
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
be two random walks starting from
\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
respectively.

We define some intermediate quantities. Let

g(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k}):=\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}r(A_{\ell})\mathbbm{1}[\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})]\right).

(4.11)

It describes the probability that no collision happens between a newly born branch and its parent until the next branching time. We also define

q(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k}):=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}(r(\mathcal{U})\mathbbm{1}[\forall t^{\prime}\in[0,t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}],W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})]).

(4.12)

Then
q(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k})
describes the probability that no collision happens between a newly born branch and its parent until the next branching time, assuming that the place of branching is uniformly distributed.
Similar to
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
, for simplicity of notations, we often omit the dependence on
t
, and just write
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=g(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k})
and
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=q(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k})
.

Our general approach for estimating the integral of
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
is to control each of the following.

1.

n^{-k}(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\left(h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}
,

2.

n^{-k}(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\left(g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}
,

3.

\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}-\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}
.

Remark 4.5
.

The first error term describes the change in the coalescence probability
if we ignore collisions that are
not
between a new branch and its parent. We will control this error in Lemma
5.4
(for general Markov chains)
and Lemma
7.3
(for transitive Markov chains).
Going back to the original CRW system (i.e., undoing the reverse of time), showing that this error term is small is equivalent to saying that conditioned on that two walkers collide in a certain time interval with length
\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}
, with high probability we don’t see any other collisions in this interval.

Remark 4.6
.

As will be seen in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, the contribution to the integral of
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
that comes from
\boldsymbol{t}
outside the
\Delta
-good set (see (
5.1
) to (
5.3
) for the definition) is negligible under some conditions.
This implies that the typical spacing between successive collisions is much larger than
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
, even if we assume that
\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\leq t
.

5
Estimates for general Markov chains

In this section we provide estimates for general finite Markov chains.

To control the integral of
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and the integral of
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
, we consider
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
such that the difference

t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}
is not too small for each
1\leq\ell\leq k
(here we set
t_{k+1}=t
). Specifically, let

\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t},t):=\min\{t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}:1\leq\ell\leq k\},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}.

(5.1)

For simplicity of notations, we also usually write
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})=\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t},t)
since
t
is mostly fixed.
We define

H(t):=\sup_{t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2<s<2t}\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{s}p_{s^{\prime}}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{s}p_{s^{\prime}}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}}.

(5.2)

Given
\Delta>2
, let

G(t,\Delta):=t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log(H(t))+t_{\mathrm{rel}}\Delta.

(5.3)

We call
\boldsymbol{t}
‘
\Delta
-good’ if
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>G(t,\Delta)
. We now give an implication of being ‘
\Delta
-good’.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
is
\Delta
-good, we have
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\geq 1/r_{\max}
,
and

\max_{1\leq r\leq k}\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}\leq e^{-\Delta}.

Let
\boldsymbol{t}
be
\Delta
-good. For
\Delta>2
, we have

t_{\ell}-t_{\ell-1}>2t_{\mathrm{rel}}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq k
, which implies that

\max_{1\leq r\leq k}\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}\leq H(t).

It follows that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq r\leq k}\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{r}-t_{r-1})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(\log H(t)+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)H(t)\leq e^{-\Delta}.

Also, as
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\geq 1/2r_{\max}
(see (
2.14
) in Lemma
2.12
), we have
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\geq 1/r_{\max}
.
∎

To control
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
,
for
k\geq 2
we define the following error parameters:

\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t}):=\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|,\qquad\text{and}

(5.4)

e_{k}(t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}.

(5.5)

Now we are ready to state our main result of this section. This is a framework to be used in the following sections.
The main thing we establish here is that, given bounds on some error terms, we could estimate the density up to a multiplicative
1+o(1)
factor.
Recall that

R=\frac{r_{\max}}{r_{\min}}=\frac{\max_{x\in V}r(x)}{\min_{x\in V}r(x)}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
K^{\prime}>0
, there exist some
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\delta>0
, such that the following holds.
Consider an arbitrary Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
|V|=n
.
Take

\Delta:=K\log\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}+1\right)+\log R+\frac{1}{\delta}.

(5.6)

Define

\begin{split}E_{1}&:=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}\right)^{K}\frac{G(t,\Delta)}{t},\qquad E_{2}:=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}\right)^{K}R\frac{r_{\max}t}{n},\\
E_{3}&:=\sum_{k=2}^{K}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{tn}\right)^{k}e_{k}(t),\qquad\qquad E_{4}:=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}\right)^{K}\frac{1}{r_{\max}t}.\end{split}

(5.7)

If the following two conditions hold

\displaystyle E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\delta,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in V,\,0\leq s\leq t}P_{s}(x)\frac{ns}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime},

(5.9)

then we have

\left|P_{t}-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right|\leq\varepsilon\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}.

Remark 5.3
.

Since
G(t,\Delta)
is increasing in
\Delta
and hence decreasing in
\delta
, Proposition
5.2

still holds if we let
\Delta
be

\Delta:=K\log\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}+1\right)+\log R+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}

(5.10)

for some
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
.
We will use this variant in the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

5.1
Estimating
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})

Now we bound the difference
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
for
\Delta
-good
\boldsymbol{t}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
\left|V\right|=n
, and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}
, and
\Delta
-good
\boldsymbol{t}
, we have

0\leq g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq r_{\max}^{k}\frac{k(k+1)}{2}(10R+1)\left(2e^{-\Delta}+\frac{8tr_{\max}}{n}\right).

(5.11)

By definition

\begin{split}0\leq&g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\\
\leq&r_{\max}^{k}\mathbb{P}(\{\forall 1\leq\ell\leq k\text{ and }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}]\text{, we have }\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\}\\
&\cap\{\exists 0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq k\text{ and }t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t]\text{, such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\})\\
\leq&r_{\max}^{k}\sum_{0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq k}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[\{\forall 1\leq\ell\leq k\text{ and }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\text{ we have }\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\}\\
&\cap\{\exists t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t],\text{ such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\}]\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\times\left(\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}=i_{\ell_{2}}]+\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}\neq i_{\ell_{2}}\text{ and }\forall t^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}],\text{ we have }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})]\right)\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).\end{split}

(5.12)

For the expectation inside the summation in the last expression, depending on whether the collision happens before or after
t_{\ell_{2}+1}
, we upper bound it by

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}+1},t]\})\\
+&\mathbb{P}(\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]\})\\
:=&P_{1}+P_{2}.\end{split}

(5.13)

Here we have used the observation that if
\gamma_{\ell_{1}}
collides with
\gamma_{\ell_{2}}
within the time period

[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]
then it must be true that
\ell_{1}\neq i_{\ell_{2}}
, since otherwise the indicator

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}[\{\forall 1\leq\ell\leq k\text{ and all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],

\displaystyle\text{ we have that }\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\cap\{\exists t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t]\text{ such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\}]

would be
0
.

For the first term, since
\gamma_{\ell_{1}}
and
\gamma_{\ell_{2}}
are independent random walks, by Lemma
2.13
, we have

\begin{split}P_{1}&\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}}}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2(t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}})}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}+\frac{8t}{n}((t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}})^{-1}\vee r_{\max})\\
&\leq 2e^{-\Delta}+\frac{8tr_{\max}}{n},\end{split}

where the second inequality follows from the assumption that
\boldsymbol{t}
is
\Delta
-good and Lemma
5.1
.

For the second term,
we modify the proof of Lemma
2.13
to control it.
Let

N_{1}
denote the amount of time
\gamma_{\ell_{1}}
and
\gamma_{\ell_{2}}
spend together during
[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}}+(t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}-1})]
.
Then it suffices to upper bound

\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{1}}{\mathbb{E}(N_{1}\mid\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime}),\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]\})}.

Note that in
[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}]
,
\gamma_{\ell_{1}}
and
\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}
perform independent random walks; in
[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]
,
\gamma_{\ell_{1}}
and
\gamma_{\ell_{2}}
perform independent random walks
and
\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t_{\ell_{2}})
is sampled from
\nu_{\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t_{\ell_{2}})}
.
For the denominator, we can lower bound it by

\min_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{(t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1})\wedge r_{\max}^{-1}}{8},

using arguments similar to those in the proof of Lemma
2.13
.
Since
\boldsymbol{t}
is
\Delta
-good, by Lemma
5.1
, we have
t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}\geq\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\geq 1/r_{\max}
, so the denominator is bounded from below by

1/(8r_{\max})
. For the numerator, it is bounded from above by

\max_{x,y}\int_{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}^{t_{\ell_{2}+1}+t_{\ell_{2}}-2t_{\ell_{2}-1}}\sum_{w}p_{s}(x,w)\hat{p}_{s}(y,w)\mathrm{d}s,

where
\hat{p}_{s}
is the transition probability of a random walk with rate
\mathbf{r}
that makes a jump at time
t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}
. More precisely,

\hat{p}_{s}(y,z):=\begin{cases}p_{s}(y,z),&\text{if $s<t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}$,}\\
\sum_{a,b}p_{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}(y,a)\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}p_{s-(t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1})}(b,z),&\text{if $s>t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}$}.\end{cases}

(5.14)

To bound
\hat{p}_{s}(y,z)
, we will need to look at the term
\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}
. We have the following claim.

Claim 1
.

For all
a,b\in V
,

\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}\leq 10Rp_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b).

Recall that
R=r_{\max}/r_{\min}
.
Note
the event that the random walk transits from
a
to
b

in time
1/r_{\max}
includes the event that it makes a jump from
a
to
b
before time
1/r_{\max}

and then stays at
b
until (at least)
1/r_{\max}
. Thus we have

p_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b)\geq\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{r(a)}{r_{\max}}\right)\right)\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}\exp\left(-\frac{r(b)}{r_{\max}}\right).

Using the inequality
1-e^{-x}\geq x/2
for
0\leq x\leq 1
we see

p_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b)\geq\frac{1}{2e}\frac{r(a)}{r_{\max}}\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}=\frac{1}{2e}\frac{r_{a,b}}{r_{\max}}.

Rearranging this inequality we get

\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}\leq 2e\frac{r_{\max}}{r(a)}p_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b)\leq 10\frac{r_{\max}}{r_{\min}}p_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b)=10Rp_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b),

as desired.
∎Now, given the upper bound of
\frac{r_{a,b}}{r(a)}
, we have that for
s>t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}
,

\hat{p}_{s}(y,z)\leq 10R\sum_{a,b}p_{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}(y,a)p_{1/r_{\max}}(a,b)p_{s-(t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1})}(b,z)=10Rp_{s+1/r_{\max}}(x,y).

(5.15)

Hence we can upper bound the numerator
\mathbb{E}N_{1}
by

10R\max_{x,y}\int_{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}^{t_{\ell_{2}+1}+t_{\ell_{2}}-2t_{\ell_{2}-1}}\sum_{w}p_{s}(x,w)p_{s+1/r_{\max}}(y,w)\mathrm{d}s\\
\leq 10R\max_{x,y}\int_{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}^{t_{\ell_{2}+1}+t_{\ell_{2}}-2t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s+1/r_{\max}}(z,w)\mathrm{d}s.

(5.16)

Using Lemma
2.5
and equation (
2.16
) to replace
\max_{x,y}
with
\max_{x}
, and writing
a(\ell_{2}):=\left\lceil\frac{t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}\right\rceil
, we further upper bound this by

10R\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{a(\ell_{2})}\exp\left(-\frac{i(t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1})}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\right)\max_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t_{\ell_{2}+1}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}{n}\right)\\
\leq 10R\left(\frac{\exp(-\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}{1-\exp(-\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}\max_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right).

(5.17)

Thus we can bound
P_{2}
(defined in (
5.13
)) by

10R\left(\frac{\exp(-\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}{1-\exp(-\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{t_{\ell_{2}}-t_{\ell_{2}-1}}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}+\frac{8tr_{\max}}{n}\right).

Using the fact that
\boldsymbol{t}
is
\Delta
-good, and Lemma
5.1
, we can bound this by

10R\left(2e^{-\Delta}+\frac{8tr_{\max}}{n}\right)
.
Finally, the conclusion follows by summing over all
0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq k
.
∎

5.2
Estimating
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})

Lemma 5.5
.

For any Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
\left|V\right|=n
and
t_{\mathrm{meet}}>3t
, any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}
, and any
\Delta
-good
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
, we have that

\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|\leq\left(\frac{3tk}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\frac{2^{k-1}t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{G(t,\Delta)}\right)\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}.

(5.18)

Recall that we let
\mathcal{U}\sim\pi
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{U}}
, and that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
are two random walks starting from
\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
respectively.
We first show that for all
\hat{t}>0
,

\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}+\hat{t}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\left(r(\mathcal{U})\mathbbm{1}[W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime}),\forall t^{\prime}\in[0,\hat{t}]]\right)\leq\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{2\hat{t}}\right).

(5.19)

By Lemma
4.1
(in the case
k=1
) and (
2.9
), we have

h(\mathbf{s},I_{1})=\frac{n}{2}f(\hat{t}),

when
\mathbf{s}:=(0,\hat{t},2\hat{t})
.
Here
f
is the density function of the meeting time of two independent random walks starting from
\pi^{\otimes 2}
.
By the definition of the branching structure,

\mathbb{E}\left(r(\mathcal{U})\mathbbm{1}[W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,\hat{t}]]\right)=h(\boldsymbol{s},I_{1})=\frac{n}{2}f(\hat{t}).

(5.20)

Consider the product chain of two independent walks on
(V,\mathbf{r})
. It has the same relaxation time as the original chain. Using (
2.12
) for the diagonal set in the product chain, we get

\frac{1}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})+\hat{t}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)\leq f(\hat{t})\leq\frac{1}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})}{2\hat{t}}\right),

(5.21)

where we have used the definition
t_{\mathrm{meet}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\pi}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}})
.
Thus equation (
5.19
) follows from (
5.20
) and (
5.21
).

By taking
\hat{t}=\hat{t}_{\ell}=t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}
, for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, we have

\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}+t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)\leq q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{2(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})}\right),

and this implies that

\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\left(1-\frac{3t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\leq q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{2\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})}\right)^{k}.

(5.22)

Since
3t<t_{\mathrm{meet}}
, we have

q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\geq-\frac{3tk}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}.

(5.23)

Since
\boldsymbol{t}
is
\Delta
-good, we have
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>G(t,\Delta)
. Recall that we only consider
\Delta>2
, so we have
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\leq\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})
. It follows that

q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\leq\frac{2^{k-1}t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{G(t,\Delta)}\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}.

(5.24)

Thus our conclusion follows from (
5.23
) and (
5.24
).
∎

5.3
From moments to coalescing probability

We start by assembling the above results to estimate
\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and large enough
t
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any Markov chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
\left|V\right|=n
, and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
t<t_{\mathrm{meet}}/3
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}-(k+1)!\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|\\
\leq C_{k}\left((1+r_{\max}t)^{k-1}+e_{k}(t)+\left(\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{G(t,\Delta)}\right)\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right.\\
\left.+r_{\max}^{k}t^{k}R\left(e^{-\Delta}+\frac{tr_{\max}}{n}\right)+G(t,\Delta)t^{k-1}\left(r_{\max}^{k}+\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right)\right),

(5.25)

where
G(t,\Delta)
is defined in (
5.3
), and
C_{k}
is some constant depending on
k
.

By (
4.1
) and Lemma
4.3
,
we have

\begin{split}&\left|\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})-(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})d\boldsymbol{t}\right|\\
\leq&\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k-1}(k+1)^{\ell+1}(\ell+1)!\sum_{I_{\ell}\in\Phi_{\ell}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{\ell})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}+1\\
\leq&\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k-1}(k+1)^{\ell+1}(\ell+1)!(r_{\max}t)^{\ell}+1.\end{split}

(5.26)

We also have

\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}-\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|=\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\left(h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\right|\\
\leq\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\boldsymbol{t}\text{ is }\Delta\text{-good}}\left(h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\right|+k!\cdot kG(t,\Delta)t^{k-1}\left(r_{\max}^{k}+\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right),

(5.27)

where in the last inequality we use the definition of
\Delta
-good (to argue that
\int_{\boldsymbol{t}:\,\boldsymbol{t}\text{ is not }\Delta\text{-good}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\leq kG(t,\Delta)t^{k-1}
), as well as the bound
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq r_{\max}^{k}
.
By Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
, and the definition of
e_{k}(t)
, the first term in the second line of equation (
5.27
) can be bounded above by

k!e_{k}(t)+\left(\frac{3tk}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\frac{2^{k-1}t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{G(t,\Delta)}\right)\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}+r_{\max}^{k}t^{k}\frac{k(k+1)}{2}(10R+1)\left(2e^{-\Delta}+\frac{8tr_{\max}}{n}\right).

By putting everything together the conclusion follows.
∎

The value of
K
is determined later.
Throughout this proof we will use
C_{K}
to denote a constant depending only on
K
, and its value may change from line to line.

Our main task is to bound
\mathbb{E}N_{t}
as well as
\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}/(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{k}
.
By (
4.3
) and the fact that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}/n\geq 1/4
(by Lemma
2.12
), we have that

\left|\mathbb{E}N_{t}-\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|\leq\left(\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t}\right)\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq\left(\frac{4r_{\max}t}{n}+\frac{G(t,\Delta)}{t}\right)\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq C_{K}(E_{1}+E_{2})\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}.

To estimate
(\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k})t_{\mathrm{meet}}^{k}/(nt)^{k}
, we control the (relative) error terms in Lemma
5.6
one by one:

\begin{split}&(r_{\max}t)^{k-1}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right)^{k}=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}\right)^{k}\frac{1}{r_{\max}t}\leq C_{K}E_{4},\qquad\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right)^{k}\leq C_{K}E_{4}^{k},\\
&\sum_{k=2}^{K}e_{k}(t)\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right)^{k}\leq E_{3},\qquad\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{G(t,\Delta)}\leq C_{K}(E_{2}+\delta),\\
&r_{\max}^{k}R\left(e^{-\Delta}+\frac{tr_{\max}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\right)^{k}\leq C_{K}(\delta+E_{2}),\\
&G(t,\Delta)t^{k-1}\left(r_{\max}^{k}+\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right)\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right)^{k}\leq C_{K}E_{1}.\end{split}

It follows that under the first condition of Proposition

5.2
, we have

\begin{split}\left|\mathbb{E}N_{t}-\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|&\leq C_{K}\delta\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}},\\
\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}}{(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{k}}-\frac{(k+1)!}{2^{k}}\right|&\leq C_{K}\delta,\quad\mbox{for }1\leq k\leq K.\end{split}

(5.28)

From this, we see that by picking a sufficiently small
\delta
, we can make
\mathbb{E}N_{t}t_{\mathrm{meet}}/(nt)
arbitrarily close to
1
and

\mathbb{E}N_{t}^{k}/(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{k}
arbitrarily close to
(k+1)!/2^{k}
for
1\leq k\leq K
.
Consider a random variable
\Gamma
under the Gamma distribution with
the probability density function
\mathbb{P}(\Gamma=x)=4xe^{-2x}\mathbbm{1}[x\geq 0]
.
Its
k
-th moment is
(k+1)!/2^{k}
, and it also satisfies the Carleman’s moment condition

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{E}\Gamma^{2m})^{-1/2m}=\sum_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{\left((k+1)!/2^{k}\right)^{1/k}}=\infty.

Hence the Gamma distribution is the unique distribution with the prescribed moments
(k+1)!/2^{k}
.
We claim that
for any bounded piecewise continuous function
\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
and any
\eta>0
, there exist
K_{0},\delta_{0}
depending on
\psi
and
\eta
, s.t. for any
K\geq K_{0},\delta<\delta_{0}
, if Condition
1
(
5.8
) is satisfied for such
(K,\delta)
, then we have

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\psi\left(\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}\right)\right)-\mathbb{E}(\psi(\Gamma))\right|<\eta.

(5.29)

Indeed, assuming this is not true, we can find a sequence of (non-negative) random variables
\{\Gamma^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
, whose
k
-th moment converges to
(k+1)!/2^{k}
but
|\mathbb{E}(\psi(\Gamma^{(n)}))-\mathbb{E}(\psi(\Gamma))|
does not decay to zero.
The moment condition also means that
\{\Gamma^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
is tight, so by taking a subsequence we can assume that
\left|\Gamma\right|^{(n)}
converges in distribution, and the limit must have its
k
-th moment equal to
(k+1)!/2^{k}
(since
\left|\Gamma^{(n)}\right|^{k}
is uniformly integrable by the convergence of all moments) and therefore
must be
\Gamma
by the Carleman’s moment condition.
We conclude that when
\psi
is bounded and continuous, we have
|\mathbb{E}(\psi(\Gamma^{(n)}))-\mathbb{E}(\psi(\Gamma))|\to 0
; and this extends to any bounded piecewise continuous
\psi
since the probability density function of
\Gamma
is bounded.

For later reference, we remark that the arguments leading to (
5.29
) also imply that for any fixed
0<a<1
, the
k
-th moment of
N_{at}/\mathbb{E}(N_{at})
is close to that of
\Gamma
(for the same values of
k
for which this holds for
a=1
). Hence, equation (
5.29
) also holds for
N_{at}/\mathbb{E}(N_{at})
(with possibly different
K_{0},\delta_{0}
).

Let
\hat{\varepsilon}
be some small number which satisfies

\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[\Gamma<\hat{\varepsilon}])\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{5}

(5.30)

and is to be determined.
Using (
5.29
)
we can pick
K
large enough and
\delta
small enough (depending on
\varepsilon
and
\hat{\varepsilon}
), so that

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}{N_{t}};\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}\geq\hat{\varepsilon}\right)-\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{-1};\Gamma\geq\hat{\varepsilon})\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{5}.

(5.31)

It remains to estimate
\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}N_{t}/N_{t};N_{t}/\mathbb{E}N_{t}<\hat{\varepsilon}\right)
. For this we adapt an argument from
[
7
, Lemma 2]
.
By Corollary
2.2
we see that

\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}{N_{t}};\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}<\hat{\varepsilon}\right)=\mathbb{E}N_{t}\mathbb{P}(0<n_{t}<\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t}),

(5.32)

where
n_{t}
is the size of the opinion cluster of a uniformly random vertex in the voter model, as defined at the end of Section
2.1
. Write
\eta=\sqrt{\hat{\varepsilon}}
, then the probability on the right hand side of (
5.32
) can be bounded from above by

\mathbb{P}\left(n_{(1-\eta)t}\geq\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(n_{t}\geq\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(n_{(1-\eta)t}<\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t},n_{t}>0\right)=:\frac{J_{1}-J_{2}+J_{3}}{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}.

By choosing
\hat{\varepsilon}<1/8
(so that
(1-\hat{\varepsilon})^{-1}<(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{2}
), taking
\delta
small and using (
5.28
), we have

\begin{split}&(1-\hat{\varepsilon})\frac{nt}{{(1-\eta)t_{\mathrm{meet}}}}\leq\mathbb{E}N_{(1-\eta)t}\leq(1+\hat{\varepsilon})\frac{nt}{{(1-\eta)t_{\mathrm{meet}}}},\\
&(1-\hat{\varepsilon})\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq\mathbb{E}N_{t}\leq(1+\hat{\varepsilon})\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{3}\mathbb{E}N_{(1-\eta)t}.\end{split}

(5.33)

It follows that

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}N_{t}\mathbb{E}(N_{(1-\eta)t}^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[N_{(1-\eta)t}>\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t}])

\displaystyle\leq(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{4}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{(1-\eta)t}}{N_{(1-\eta)t}};\frac{N_{(1-\eta)t}}{\mathbb{E}N_{(1-\eta)t}}>\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}}{(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{4}}\right).

Using the definition of
J_{2}
together with Corollary
2.2
we get that

J_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}{N_{t}};\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}>\hat{\varepsilon}\right).

Using this, as well as equation (
5.29
), we see
that if we choose
K
large enough and
\delta
small enough, we have

J_{1}-J_{2}\leq(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{5}\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma^{-1}\mathbbm{1}\left[\Gamma>\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}}{(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{4}}\right]\right)-(1+\hat{\varepsilon})^{-1}\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{-1}\mathbbm{1}[\Gamma>\hat{\varepsilon}]),

(5.34)

which can be made smaller than
\varepsilon/5
by taking
\hat{\varepsilon}
sufficiently small.

Finally, using the second condition (
5.9
) together with (
5.33
) (to bound
\mathbb{E}N_{t}
), we see that

\begin{split}J_{3}&\leq\mathbb{E}N_{t}\sum_{A\subset V:1\leq\left|A\right|\leq\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}\mathbb{P}(\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{(1-\eta)t}=A)\mathbb{P}(n_{t}>0|\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{(1-\eta)t}=A)\\
&\leq\left(\mathbb{E}N_{t}\mathbb{P}(n_{(1-\eta)t}>0)\right)\left(\hat{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}N_{t}\sup_{x\in V}P_{\eta t}(x)\right)\\
&\leq\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}((1+\hat{\varepsilon})K^{\prime})^{2}}{\eta(1-\eta)}=\frac{\sqrt{\hat{\varepsilon}}((1+\hat{\varepsilon})K^{\prime})^{2}}{1-\sqrt{\hat{\varepsilon}}},\end{split}

(5.35)

which can be made smaller than
\varepsilon/5
by taking
\hat{\varepsilon}
small enough. Here, we have used a union bound and the duality between the voter model and CRW in the second inequality of (
5.35
):

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(n_{t}>0|\zeta^{\mathcal{U}}_{(1-\eta)t}=A)=\mathbb{P}\left(\cup_{x\in A}\{\zeta^{x}_{\eta t}\neq\emptyset\}\right)&\leq\sum_{x\in A}\mathbb{P}\left(\{\zeta^{x}_{\eta t}\neq\emptyset\}\right)\\
&=\sum_{x\in A}P_{\eta t}(x)\leq\left|A\right|\sup_{x\in V}P_{\eta t}(x).\end{split}

Combining equations (
5.30
) to (
5.35
) we get

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}{N_{t}}\right)-\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{-1})\right|\leq 4\varepsilon/5.

Hence by using (
5.33
) to control
(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1}
we get

\left|P_{t}-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right|=\left|(\mathbb{E}N_{t})^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}N_{t}}{N_{t}}\right)-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right|\leq\left(\frac{10\hat{\varepsilon}}{1-\hat{\varepsilon}}(1+\frac{4\varepsilon}{5})+\frac{4\varepsilon}{5}\right)\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt},

which can be made smaller than
(\varepsilon t_{\mathrm{meet}})/(nt)
by choosing
\hat{\varepsilon}<\varepsilon/100
.

In conclusion, for the given
\varepsilon>0
, we first pick
\hat{\varepsilon}
small enough s.t. (
5.30
) holds and the right hand side of (
5.34
) is smaller than
\varepsilon/5
. Then we choose a large enough
K
and a small enough
\delta
s.t. (
5.31
), (
5.33
), (
5.34
) and (
5.35
) hold. This completes the proof of Proposition
5.2
.
∎

6
Random graphs: the configuration model and Galton-Watson trees

In this section, we use the framework given by Proposition
5.2
to study the decay of density for the configuration model and unimodular Galton-Watson trees. Throughout this section, we let
D
be a degree distribution that is at least
3
and upper bounded (i.e., the degree distribution has finite support).
For CRW, the jump rate is taken as
r_{x,y}=\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]
.

To prove Theorem
1.6
, we will apply Proposition
5.2
, checking that the conditions are satisfied. The most efforts will be devoted to controlling the term
E_{3}
defined in equation (
5.7
), i.e., showing that the behaviors of the particles after each branching are similar to being independent.
In other words, we show that the coalescing process of
k+1
particles, starting from i.i.d. stationary locations, evolves similarly to the case in which after each collision the positions of the remaining particles are re-sampled to be i.i.d. stationary.
We also give estimates for
\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}
, showing that it converges to
\alpha(D)^{-1}
in probability as
n\to\infty
, with
\alpha(D)
defined in equation (
1.5
).

One important feature of the configuration model that helps in our analysis is that it is an expander graph with high probability. Recall that in equation (
2.7
) we defined
\kappa(\mathcal{G})
to be the vertex expansion constant of the graph
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,

\kappa(\mathcal{G}):=\min_{0<|S|\leq\frac{|V|}{2}}\frac{|\partial S|}{|S|},

where
\partial S:=\{v\in V\setminus S:\exists u\in S,u\sim v\}
is the out-boundary of
S
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a constant
\kappa_{0}
, such that the following is true. Take
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
, conditioned on that it is connected.
Then we have
\mathbb{P}(\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})\geq\kappa_{0})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
. Consequently,
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\leq 2\bar{d}/\kappa_{0}^{2})=1
, where
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is the relaxation time of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\bar{d}
is the supremum of the support of
D
.

The first part of this result can be found in
[
14
, Theorem 6.3.2]
.
The second part follows from the first part together with Lemma
2.8
(Cheeger’s inequality).

6.1
Truncate random walks within a ball

We define some intermediate quantities. For a vertex
v
and a distance
\rho\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, define
N_{\rho}(v)=N_{\rho}(v,\mathcal{G}_{n})
to be the subgraph of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
induced on
\{u\in V_{n}:d(u,v)\leq\rho\}
(the ball of radius
\rho
centered at
v
), where
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is the graph distance in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Recall the construction of the random paths
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k+1}
, given
\boldsymbol{t}
and a collision pattern
I_{k}
. In particular, we let
A_{\ell}=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t_{\ell})
and
B_{\ell}
be sampled from
\nu_{A_{\ell}}
, for each
1\leq\ell\leq k
. Let
\tau_{\ell}
be the first time after
t_{\ell}
when
\gamma_{\ell}
or
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}
exits
N_{\rho}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell}))
(whichever occurs first). We consider the following quantity

\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k},\rho):=\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}r(A_{\ell})\mathbbm{1}[\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}]]\right),

(6.1)

which describes the weighted probability that no coalescence happens between a newly born branch and its parent until one of them exits
N_{\rho}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell}))
or until the next branching time (whichever happens first).

Take
\mathcal{U}\sim\pi
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{U}}
, and two independent walks
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
starting from
\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
, respectively. We define
\tau_{\mathcal{U}}
to be the first time that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
or
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
exits
N_{\rho}(\mathcal{U})
(whichever occurs first), and set

\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},t,I_{k},\rho):=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left(r(\mathcal{U})\mathbbm{1}[W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,\tau_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})]\right).

(6.2)

The quantity
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
describes the weighted probability that no coalescence happens between a newly born branch and its parent until one of them exits
N_{\rho}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell}))
or the next branching time, assuming that the place of branching is uniformly distributed. For the sake of ease of notation, below we suppress the dependence of
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
on
\rho
and
t
in our notations.

We show that
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
well approximate
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
(defined in (
4.11
) and (
4.12
)) provided
1\ll t\ll n
and
\rho\gg 1
, for any fixed graph with lower bounded vertex expansion constant and upper bounded degree.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
For any graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
with
\kappa(\mathcal{G})>\kappa_{0}
and maximum degree bounded by
\bar{d}
, we have that for all
\rho>100
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}
and any time sequence
\boldsymbol{t}
,

0\leq\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq 8\bar{d}^{k}\left(kC_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}})+\frac{t\bar{d}}{n}\right),

(6.3)

0\leq\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq 8k\bar{d}^{k}\left(kC_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}})+\frac{t\bar{d}}{n}\right),

(6.4)

where
C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}
is the constant from Lemma
2.9
, and
n=|V|
.

Comparing the definitions of
\hat{g}
and
g
and those of
\hat{q}
and
q
one readily sees that

\begin{split}0\leq&\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\\
=&\mathbb{E}\biggl(\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}r(A_{\ell})\mathbbm{1}[\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}]]\right)\\
&\times\mathbbm{1}[\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some }1\leq\ell\leq k\text{ and some }t^{\prime\prime}\in[\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1},t_{\ell+1}]]\biggl)\\
\leq&\bar{d}^{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\left(\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime\prime}\in[\tau_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}]\}\right),\end{split}

(6.5)

where we used
\max_{\ell}r(A_{\ell})\leq\bar{d}
. Further, we have that

\begin{split}0\leq&\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq\bar{d}^{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}(\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,\tau_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]\}\\
&\cap\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime\prime})=W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime\prime}\in[\tau_{\mathcal{U}},t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]\}).\end{split}

(6.6)

For any
0<T<\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
, we have,

\mathbb{P}\left(\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime}\in[\tau_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\}\right)\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell}+T]\}\cap\{\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})=\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell}+T,t_{\ell+1}\}]\right)\\
+\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}<t_{\ell}+T),

(6.7)

and similarly,

\mathbb{P}\left(\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,\tau_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]\}\cap\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})=W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime}\in[\tau_{\mathcal{U}},t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]\}\right)\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,T]\}\cap\{W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})=W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for some }t^{\prime}\in[T,t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]\}\right)\\
+\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\mathcal{U}}<T).

(6.8)

By Lemma
2.13
, the first terms in the r.h.s. of the above two equations can be bounded by

2\exp(-T/t_{\mathrm{rel}})\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}+\frac{8(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})}{n}(T^{-1}\vee\bar{d})\\
\leq 2C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-T/t_{\mathrm{rel}})+\frac{8(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})}{n}(T^{-1}\vee\bar{d}),

(6.9)

where the inequality and the constant
C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}
come from Lemma
2.9
.

We now bound each of
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}<t_{\ell}+T)
and
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\mathcal{U}}<T)
.
Note that in order for the event
\{\tau_{\ell}<t_{\ell}+T\}
(resp.
\{\tau_{\mathcal{U}}<T\}
) to occur we must have that either
\gamma_{\ell}
or
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}
(resp.
W_{\mathcal{U}}
or
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
) needs to jump at least
\rho
times during
[t_{\ell},t_{\ell}+T]
(resp.
[0,T]
). Since the jump rate is bounded by
\bar{d}
,
the law of the number of jumps that a particle performs in a time interval of length
T
is stochastically dominated by the
\hbox{Poisson}(T\bar{d})
distribution.
Hence we have that

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}<t_{\ell}+T),\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\mathcal{U}}<T)\leq 2\sum_{i=\rho}^{\infty}\frac{(T\bar{d})^{i}e^{-T\bar{d}}}{i!}.

Now we take
T=\sqrt{\rho}/\bar{d}
.
Then we have

\begin{split}0\leq&\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}),\;\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\\
\leq&k\bar{d}^{k}\left(2C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/(\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}}))+\frac{8t\bar{d}}{n}+\frac{4\rho^{\rho/2}e^{-\sqrt{\rho}}}{\rho!}\right),\end{split}

(6.10)

which can be further upper bounded by

\begin{split}&k\bar{d}^{k}\left(2C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/(\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}}))+\frac{8t\bar{d}}{n}+\frac{4\rho^{\rho/2}e^{-\sqrt{\rho}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\rho^{\rho+1/2}e^{-\rho}}\right)\\
\leq&k\bar{d}^{k}\left(2C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/(\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}}))+\frac{8t\bar{d}}{n}+4\exp(-\rho)\right).\end{split}

(6.11)

This implies our conclusion since

\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\leq\frac{2\bar{d}\sqrt{\rho}}{\bar{d}}\leq\rho

by equation (
2.14
) and the assumption
\rho>100
.
∎

6.2
Almost independence among branching: estimate
e_{k}(t)

In the previous subsection, we compared
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
to
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
to
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
. In this subsection, we will proceed to compare
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
. Recall the definition of
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), respectively.
Now if we write

M_{\ell}:=r(A_{\ell})\mathbbm{1}[\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}]],

(6.12)

then,

\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}M_{\ell}\right).

(6.13)

Define

Q_{\ell}:=\mathbb{E}\left(r(\mathcal{U})\mathbbm{1}[W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[0,\tau_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})]]\right),

(6.14)

then

\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}Q_{\ell}.

(6.15)

We next define a few properties of the graph
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 6.3
.

For any
\delta>0
, a graph
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
\delta
-homogeneous
if for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we have either
|\{v\in V:\deg(v)=i\}|\geq\delta n
or
|\{v\in V:\deg(v)=i\}|=0
.
In addition, we say that
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is
feasible
(for
\mathcal{G}
) if
|\{v\in V:\deg(v)=i\}|>0
. For notational convenience, we also consider
0
as feasible.

The construction of
\CM_{n}(D)
implies the following lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

For any degree distribution
D
that is upper bounded by some constant, there exists
\delta_{D}>0
,
such that if we take
\mathcal{G}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
, then
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}\textrm{ is }\delta_{D}\textrm{-homogeneous})=1
.

Definition 6.5
.

For
\rho\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we call a graph
\mathcal{G}

\rho
-locally tree like
if
|\{v\in V:N_{\rho}(v)\text{ is a tree}\}|\geq n-\sqrt{n}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
\rho\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
\mathcal{G}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}\text{ is }\rho\textrm{-locally tree like})=1.

This is a direct consequence of the local weak convergence of the configuration model to unimodular Galton-Watson tree, as stated in Lemma
B.4
. (A quantitative estimate can be derived via Markov’s inequality, by noting that the expected number of vertices
v
such that
N_{\rho}(v)
is not a tree is upper bounded by some constant depending only on
\bar{d}
and
\rho
.)
∎

In order to bound the difference between
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
, we will use induction. We will show inductively that, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and any sequence of feasible numbers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}
, we have

\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1}=m_{\ell-1})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\mbox{ and }\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|

are small while
\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}(M_{j}=m_{j})\right)
is not too small. Here
\mathcal{D}(\cdot)
denotes the law of the random variable, and
\pi
is the stationary distribution of the random walk (in our case, it is the uniform distribution over
V_{n}
).

We now state the induction step as the following two lemmas.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
,
\delta>c_{1}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Assume
t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}\geq 1
. Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is
\delta
-homogeneous with maximum degree bounded by
\bar{d}
. Assume further that for any sequence of feasible numbers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}
(not necessarily mutually distinct), we have

\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1}=m_{\ell-1})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq c_{1}.

(6.16)

Also assume that

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}Q_{j}\right|\leq c_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}Q_{j},

(6.17)

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq c_{3}.

(6.18)

Then for
m_{\ell}
feasible, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|\leq(c_{1}(1+c_{2})\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}+c_{2})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j},

(6.19)

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq(\delta-c_{1})c_{3}\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}.

(6.20)

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
c_{4}>0
and
1\leq\ell\leq K-1
, and
C,\rho>0
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
\rho
-locally tree like graph with
n
vertices, maximum degree bounded by
\bar{d}
. Take any sequence of feasible integers
m_{1},\cdots,m_{\ell}
, suppose we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq c_{4}.

(6.21)

Then when
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho>0
, we have

\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell+1}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\bar{d}^{\ell}\exp(-C)\left(\frac{1}{c_{4}-\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{4}},

(6.22)

if
c_{4}>\varepsilon
.
Here
\varepsilon
is defined as

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,n):=\frac{3(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\sqrt{n}}.

(6.23)

Deferring the proofs of these two lemmas to the next subsection, we now use them to do the induction.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
\delta_{1}
-homogeneous and
\rho
-locally tree like graph
\mathcal{G}
and for
C>0
and
1\leq k\leq K
, if

1.

\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\geq 1
,

2.

\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho>0
,

3.

\varepsilon\leq\min\{\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}})^{2k},\frac{1}{64k^{2}\bar{d}^{4\rho+4}}\}
for
\varepsilon
defined in Lemma
6.8
,

4.

\exp(C)\geq\frac{\bar{d}^{k}}{\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,n)^{2}}
,

then

|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})-\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})|\leq 8k\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\sqrt{\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,n)}\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k}),

when
n
is large enough.

We note that the constants in the lemma are far from being optimal. We take those for the convenience of computation as will be shown below. They are complicated at first sight, but they naturally arise in our computations.

The proof is done inductively using Lemmas
6.7
and
6.8
. We will show that for
1\leq\ell\leq k
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1}=m_{\ell-1})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon},

(6.24)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|\leq 8\ell\sqrt{\varepsilon}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j},

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}(M_{j}=m_{j})\right)\geq\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell}.

(6.26)

When
\ell=1
, we have
\mathcal{D}(A_{1})=\pi
,
\mathbb{E}M_{1}=Q_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}(M_{1}=m_{1})\geq\delta_{1}\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}
, so equations (
6.24
)
-
(
6.26
) hold. Now assume that equations (
6.24
)
-
(
6.26
) hold for
\ell-1
. The conditions
\Delta_{\min}(t)\geq 1
and
\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,n)\leq\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}})^{2k}
and equations (
6.24
) and (
6.25
) ensure that the assumptions in Lemma
6.7
hold. Therefore, by Lemma
6.7
and the assumption that
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{64k^{2}\bar{d}^{4\rho+4}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq((1+8(\ell-1)\sqrt{\varepsilon}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2})(2\sqrt{\varepsilon})\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}+8(\ell-1)\sqrt{\varepsilon}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}

\displaystyle\leq 8\ell\sqrt{\varepsilon}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}.

By Lemma
6.7
, equation (
6.24
), equation (
6.26
) and the assumption that
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}})^{2k}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}(M_{j}=m_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\geq(\delta_{1}-2\sqrt{\varepsilon})\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell-1}\geq\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell}.

By Lemma
6.8
, equation (
6.26
) and the assumptions that
\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,\delta_{2})\leq\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}})^{2k}
and
\exp(C)\geq\frac{\bar{d}^{k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1}=m_{\ell-1})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{d}^{k}\exp(-C)\left(\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell-1}-\varepsilon\right)^{-1}+\varepsilon\left(\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell-1}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{2}\left(\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{\ell-1}-\varepsilon\right)^{-1}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Therefore, equations (
6.24
)
-
(
6.26
) hold for
1\leq\ell\leq k
. In particular, when
\ell=k
, we have
\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})=\mathbb{E}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}M_{j})
and
\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})=\prod_{j=1}^{k}Q_{j}
, and

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{k}Q_{j}\right|\leq 8k\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\sqrt{\varepsilon(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t}),\rho,C,n)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}Q_{j}.

(6.27)

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎Recall that we have defined previously the error terms in Proposition
5.2
:

\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t}):=\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|,\quad e_{k}(t):=\int_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}<t}\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t},

and
E_{3}=\sum_{k=2}^{K}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{tn}\right)^{k}e_{k}(t)
.
We next control
E_{3}
, by choosing appropriate intermediate parameters and applying Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.9
.

Proposition 6.10
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with
K\geq 2
,
0<\delta_{1}\leq 1
, and
\delta,\kappa_{0},C_{\alpha}>0
, there exists an appropriate choice of
\rho=\rho(\delta,\kappa_{0},C_{\alpha})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, such that whenever
\mathcal{G}

has maximal degree upper bounded by
\bar{d}
,
is
\delta_{1}
-homogeneous and
\rho
-locally tree like,
\kappa(\mathcal{G})>\kappa_{0}
and
\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\leq C_{\alpha}
, then

E_{3}(t)\leq\delta,

(6.28)

for any
t
large enough and
n
such that
n/t
is large enough.

Take appropriate
\rho>100
such that

\displaystyle 16\bar{d}^{K}\left(KC_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}})\right)\leq 16\bar{d}^{K}C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\rho}\frac{\kappa_{0}^{2}}{2\bar{d}^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}.

(6.29)

For a chosen
\rho
, take
\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}
large enough such that

\displaystyle\frac{3(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{2K},\frac{1}{64K^{2}\bar{d}^{4\rho+4}},\left(\frac{1}{3K^{2}C_{\alpha}^{K}\bar{d}^{2\rho+K+2}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

Now, fix
\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}
and pick
C
large enough such that

\displaystyle\exp(C)\geq\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\left(\frac{3(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}\right)^{2}}.

This implies that when
n
is large, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\Delta_{0},\rho,C,n)=\frac{3(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\delta_{1}}{2\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}\right)^{2K},\frac{1}{64K^{2}\bar{d}^{4\rho+4}},\left(\frac{1}{3K^{2}C_{\alpha}^{K}\bar{d}^{2\rho+K+2}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(6.30)

\displaystyle\exp(C)\geq\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\varepsilon(\Delta_{0},\rho,C,n)^{2}}=\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\left(\frac{3(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{n}\right)^{2}},

(6.31)

\displaystyle 8K\bar{d}^{2\rho+K+2}\sqrt{\frac{3(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{0}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{n}}=8K\bar{d}^{2\rho+K+2}\sqrt{\varepsilon(\Delta_{0},\rho,C,n)}\leq\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}.

(6.32)

Now we estimate
\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})
for
2\leq k\leq K
.

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})

\displaystyle=\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|+\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|+\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|.

For the first and the third term, by Lemma
6.2
and equation (
6.29
),

\displaystyle\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|+\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 16\bar{d}^{k}k\left(C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\exp(-\sqrt{\rho}/\bar{d}t_{\mathrm{rel}})+\frac{t\bar{d}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+16k\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}.

For the second term, by Lemma
6.9
and the fact that
Q_{\ell}\leq\bar{d}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|\leq 8k\bar{d}^{2\rho+K+2}\sqrt{\frac{3(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{n}}.

When
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\geq\Delta_{0}
, this is further bounded by
\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}
using equation (
6.32
). It follows that

\displaystyle e_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})=\int_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}<t}\hat{e}_{t}(\boldsymbol{t})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\left(\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|+\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|+\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}

\displaystyle\leq t^{k}\left(\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+16\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t},\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta_{0}}\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t},\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\leq\Delta_{0}}\sup_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|\hat{q}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}

\displaystyle\leq t^{k}\left(\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+16k\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}\right)+t^{k}\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+\Delta_{0}t^{k-1}\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}\hat{e}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t})

\displaystyle\leq t^{k}\left(\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+16k\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}\right)+t^{k}\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+2\Delta_{0}t^{k-1}\bar{d}^{k}.

This implies that

\displaystyle E_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{K}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{tn}\right)^{k}e_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=2}^{K}\frac{C_{\alpha}^{k}}{t^{k}}t^{k}\left(\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+16k\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}+\frac{\delta}{3KC_{\alpha}^{K}}+2\Delta_{0}\frac{1}{t}\bar{d}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta}{3}+16K^{2}C_{\alpha}^{k}\bar{d}^{k+1}\frac{t}{n}+2KC_{\alpha}^{k}\Delta_{0}\bar{d}^{k}\frac{1}{t}.

When
t
and
n/t
are large enough, we have that

E_{3}(t)\leq\delta
,
thus our conclusion follows.
∎

6.3
Proofs of induction steps

In this subsection we prove Lemmas
6.7
and
6.8
.

Let
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}
be feasible. Denote
