B_{i}:=\cap_{j=1}^{i}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}
. Observe that
M_{\ell}
is conditionally independent of
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1})
given
A_{\ell}
(indeed,
M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1}
do not involve
\gamma_{\ell}
, and they involve
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}
only at intervals contained in
[0,t_{\ell}]
). Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(B_{\ell}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(B_{\ell-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}\mathbb{P}\left(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v,B_{\ell-1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\ell}=v\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(B_{\ell-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}\mathbb{P}\left(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v\right)\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\ell}=v\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(B_{\ell-1}\right)

which is at least

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}\mathbb{P}(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v)\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\ell}=v\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)c_{3},

using assumption (
6.18
). Note that
M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}
for some feasible
m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
if and only if one of the following two cases happen. Either
m_{\ell}>0
,
\deg(A_{\ell})=m_{\ell}
and the indicator from the definition of
M_{\ell}
occurs or
m_{\ell}=0
and indicator from the definition of
M_{\ell}
doesn’t occur. Now if
m_{\ell}>0
, when
A_{\ell}=v
and
\deg(v)=m_{\ell}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v)=\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}])\geq\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}.

(6.33)

This can be seen by considering two arbitrary disjoint paths connecting
A_{\ell}
and
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})^{c}
and
\nu_{A_{\ell}}
and
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})^{c}
respectively, as possible trajectories for the two walks until they exit
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})
. This implies that when
m_{\ell}>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}\mathbb{P}\left(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v\right)\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\ell}=v\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)c_{3}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V,\deg v=m_{\ell}}\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\ell}=v\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}c_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\deg(A_{\ell})=m_{\ell}\mid B_{\ell-1}\right)\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}c_{3}.

As we are in the
\delta
-homogeneous graph
\mathcal{G}
, the number of vertices with feasible degree
m_{\ell}
(when
m_{\ell}>0
) is lower bounded by
\delta n
. Using our assumption (
6.16
), when
m_{\ell}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq(\delta-c_{1})c_{3}\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}.

Now, when
m_{\ell}=0
and
t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}(M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell}=v)=1-\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}\wedge t_{\ell+1}])\geq\frac{1}{8\bar{d}}>\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}.

(6.34)

Indeed, the probability that
\gamma_{\ell}(t)
stays at
A_{\ell}
during
[t_{\ell},t_{\ell}+\frac{\log 2}{\bar{d}}]
and that
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t)
jumps to
A_{\ell}
by time
t_{\ell}+\frac{\log 2}{\bar{d}}
is at least
\frac{1}{8\bar{d}}
. Therefore when
m_{\ell}=0
, we also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq\frac{1}{8\bar{d}}c_{3}>(\delta-c_{1})c_{3}\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2\rho+1}}.

Since
r(\mathcal{U})\geq 1
, the above equations (
6.33
) and (
6.34
) imply
Q_{\ell}\geq\bar{d}^{-(2\rho+1)}
.
Now we consider

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}(M_{\ell}\mid M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(M_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell})\mid M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right).

By (
6.16
) and as
0\leq M_{\ell}\leq r_{\max}\leq\bar{d}
,

|\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(M_{\ell}\mid A_{\ell})\mid M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell-1})-Q_{\ell}|\leq c_{1}\bar{d}\leq c_{1}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}Q_{\ell}.

(6.35)

Therefore,

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)Q_{\ell}\right|\leq c_{1}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)Q_{\ell}.

(6.36)

Using assumption (
6.17
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}M_{j}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)Q_{\ell}\right|+\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)Q_{\ell}-\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}M_{j}\right)Q_{\ell}+c_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\left((1+c_{2})c_{1}\bar{d}^{2\rho+2}+c_{2}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Q_{j}.

Thus our conclusion follows.
∎

To prove Lemma
6.8
, we present an intermediate result that controls the exit time
\tau_{\ell}
.
Recall that
\varepsilon=\frac{3(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\sqrt{n}}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
c_{4}>0
and
1\leq\ell\leq K-1
. For any
\rho
-locally tree like graph
\mathcal{G}
with maximum degree bounded by
\bar{d}
and any sequence of feasible integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}
, if we assume that

\mathbb{P}\left(\cap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{M_{j}=m_{j}\}\right)\geq c_{4},

(6.37)

then

\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})\leq\frac{1}{c_{4}}\varepsilon,

(6.38)

when
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho>0
(the
C
here is the same as the one in the definition of
\varepsilon
in (
6.23
)).

Intuitively, for most vertices
v
, its neighborhood
N_{\rho}(v)
is a tree. In this case, a random walk starting from
v
has a drift towards the leaves, so the time of exit cannot be too large. In the following, we write down the idea rigorously. Notice that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{\mathbb{P}(M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{c_{4}}.

To control the numerator, we consider the probability

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}-t_{\ell}>s).

For
s\in[0,t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}]
,
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(s+t_{\ell})
is a random walk starting at
A_{\ell}
. We denote the graph distance of
\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(s+t_{\ell})
to
A_{\ell}
by
d_{1}(s)
. Define another random process
d_{2}(s)
to be a (time-homogeneous) drifted continuous-time random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
starting from
0
, where the transition rate from
x
to
x+1
is
2
and from
x
to
x-1
is
1
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and all other rates are zero. If
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})
is a tree with minimal degree at least 3, then
d_{1}(s)
stochastically dominates
d_{2}(s)
. If we define
\tau^{\prime}
to be the first time that
d_{2}(s)
gets larger than
\rho
, then given
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})
is a tree,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}-t_{\ell}>s)\leq\mathbb{P}(\tau^{\prime}>s).

To bound the right hand side, we compute the second moment. Define
d_{3}(s):=d_{2}(s)-s
, then
d_{3}(s)
is a continuous-time martingale, with
\mathbb{E}d_{3}(s)=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(d_{3}(s))=3s
. Then, by Chernoff bound, given
N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})
is a tree,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\ell}-t_{\ell}>s)\leq\mathbb{P}(\tau^{\prime}>s)\leq\mathbb{P}(d_{2}(s)\leq\rho)=\mathbb{P}(d_{3}(s)\leq\rho-s)\leq\frac{3s}{(s-\rho)^{2}}.

Substituting
t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}
for
s
in the above inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})\text{ is a tree })+\mathbb{P}(N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})\text{ is not a tree})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\mathbb{P}(N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})\text{ is not a tree}).

By Lemma
4.4
, we have that for any
v\in V
,
\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell}=v)\leq\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{n}
. So
\mathbb{P}(N_{\rho}(A_{\ell})\text{ is not a tree })\leq\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{\sqrt{n}}
. By definition of
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})
, we know that
t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}\geq\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})
. This implies that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{4}}\left(\frac{3(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{4}}\left(\frac{3(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})}{(\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho)^{2}}+\frac{\bar{d}^{K}}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

and our conclusion follows.
∎

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell+1}=v\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell+1}=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell+1}=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell}).

By Lemma
6.11
,
\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}<Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{4}}
. Hence

\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})\geq c_{4}-\varepsilon.

For any
v\in V
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell+1}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell+1}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})=v)/\mathbb{P}(t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{(c_{4}-\varepsilon)n}.

In the last step we used Lemma
4.4
with
R\leq\bar{d}
.
When
t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}
, given
\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell+1}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})
,
A_{\ell+1}
is independent of
M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}
. Therefore, by (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v}\left(\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell+1}=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp(-2C)\sum_{v}\left(\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell+1}-Ct_{\mathrm{rel}})=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2},

which is smaller than

\exp(-2C)\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{c_{4}-\varepsilon}-1\right)^{2}.

Therefore,

\sum_{v}\left|\mathbb{P}(A_{\ell+1}=v\mid t_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}\geq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}},M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})-\frac{1}{n}\right|\leq\exp(-C)\left(\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{c_{4}-\varepsilon}\right).

(6.39)

And finally using Lemma
6.11
again, we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}(A_{\ell+1}\mid M_{1}=m_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}=m_{\ell})-\pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-C)\left(\frac{\bar{d}^{\ell}}{c_{4}-\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{4}}.

Thus our conclusion follows.
∎

6.4
Limit of
\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}
in the configuration model

In this section we will prove the convergence of

t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n
to
(2\alpha(D))^{-1}
(defined in equation
(
1.5
)) in the configuration model.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
be sampled from the configuration model
\CM_{n}(D)
and condition on that
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is connected. Then

t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})/n
converges in probability to
(2\alpha(D))^{-1}
as
n
goes to
\infty
.

Remark 6.13
.

Recall that
\UGT(D)
denotes the unimodular Galton-Watson tree and
o
is the root of the tree. Also recall
\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}
is the (random) meeting time of two independent random walks. Define

\alpha(D,k)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{o,\nu_{o}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}>t|D_{o}=k),

(6.40)

where the probability is taken over both the law of random walks and the law of the Galton-Watson tree.
Then
for all
k
such that
\mathbb{P}(D=k)>0
, we have
\alpha(D,k)>0
. Indeed, since we assume the minimal degree is 3, the random walk on
\UGT(D)
with unit jump rate along each edge is transient.

We apply formula (
2.10
) to the product chain of two independent random walks and let
A
be the diagonal set
A:=\{(x,x):x\in V_{n}\}
.
Since
r_{x,y}=\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]
, we have

Q(A,A^{c})=\sum_{x}\frac{1}{n^{2}}2\sum_{y}\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]=\frac{2\sum_{x}D_{x}}{n^{2}}.

For any pair
(x,y)\in V_{n}\times V_{n}-A
,
the exit measure
\nu_{A}
puts mass

\frac{(r_{x,y}+r_{y,x})/n^{2}}{(2\sum_{z}r(z))/n^{2}}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]}{\sum_{z}D_{z}}

on
(x,y)
. We get

Q(A,A^{c})\mathbb{E}_{\nu(A)}(T_{A})=\frac{2\sum_{z}D_{z}}{n^{2}}\sum_{x\neq y}\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x\sim y]}{\sum_{z^{\prime}}D_{z^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{x},W_{y}))=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{x}D_{x}\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\nu_{x}}(\mathcal{C}(W_{x},W_{y})),

(6.41)

where
\mathcal{C}
is the coalescence time and
W_{x},W_{y}
are two independent random walks starting from
x
and
y
, respectively, and
y
is sampled from
\nu_{x}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{U}
is defined to
be a uniform random vertex and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
a uniform random neighbor of
\mathcal{U}
.
The last expression in equation (
6.41
) can be rewritten as

\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k}k\mathbb{P}(D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k).

Since we are considering the product chain, we have
\pi^{\otimes 2}(A)=\sum_{x\in V}1/n^{2}=1/n
and
\pi^{\otimes 2}(A^{c})=1-1/n
. Hence using (
2.10
) we have

\sum_{k}k\mathbb{P}(D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)=\frac{n}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right).

(6.42)

To compute
t_{\mathrm{meet}}
we make the following claim.

Claim 2
.

The following two statements hold.

1.

The (quenched) conditional probability (i.e., we fix the random graph)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\log n\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k,\mathcal{G}_{n})

converges to
\alpha(D,k)>0
in probability as
n\to\infty
.

2.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and any
k
such that
\mathbb{P}(D=k)>0
, the following event

\begin{split}\{&\left|\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k,\mathcal{G}_{n})-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\log n\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k,\mathcal{G}_{n})t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\right|\\
\leq&\varepsilon t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})+16\log n\}\end{split}

has probability tending to 1 as
n\to\infty
.

It now follows from Claim
2
and equation (
6.42
) that

t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n
converges to
\frac{1}{2\alpha(D)}
in probability
since the definition of
\alpha(D)
implies

\alpha(D)=\sum_{k}k\mathbb{P}(D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\alpha(D,k).

It remains to prove the claim. For notational convenience we omit
\mathcal{G}_{n}
from the conditioning.
To prove the first part of claim, we fix some
\rho>0
and replace the quantity
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)

by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>\tau_{\rho}\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)
, where
\tau_{\rho}
is the first time that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
or
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
exits
N_{\rho}(\mathcal{U})
. For fixed
\rho
, by Lemma
B.4
, we see that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>\tau_{\rho}\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)
converges to its corresponding version on
\UGT(D)
conditioned on the root having degree
k
. We call this probability
\alpha(D,k,\rho)
. One can show that
\alpha(D,k,\rho)
converges to
\alpha(D,k)
as
\rho\to\infty
, using the transience of the simple random walk on a rooted tree whose minimal degree is
\geq 3
. On the other hand, we can repeat the argument in Section
6.1
to show the difference
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>\tau_{\mathcal{U}}\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n\mid D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)
goes to 0 in probability by first sending
n
to
\infty
and then
\rho
to
\infty
. Thus the first part of the claim follows.

To prove the second part of Claim
2
, we condition on
D_{\mathcal{U}}=k
as well as
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
don’t collide before time
4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n
. Define the random time
\tau
to be the first time that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
coalesced with
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
in a modified CRW model where the meeting of
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
in the time interval
[4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]
is ignored. Clearly we can couple
\tau
and
\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})
together s.t.
\tau\geq\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})
almost surely and on the event
\{\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n\}
we have
\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})=\tau
. Note that in the modified model, conditioned on that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
doesn’t collide with
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
before time
4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n
, the
\ell_{\infty,\pi^{\otimes 2}}
distance between the joint distribution of
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
at time
8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n
and
\pi^{\otimes 2}
is smaller than
1/n^{2}
by Corollary
2.6
applied to the product chain. This implies

\left|\mathbb{E}(\tau\mid\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-t_{\mathrm{meet}}\right|\leq t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n^{2}+8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n.

(6.43)

Using the coupling between
\tau
and
\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})
, we see

\begin{split}&\left|\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mid\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-t_{\mathrm{meet}}\right|\\
\leq&\left|\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mid\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-\mathbb{E}(\tau\mid\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\mathcal{U}}>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\right|\\
+&\left|\mathbb{E}(\tau\mid\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-t_{\mathrm{meet}}\right|\\
\leq&\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\\
+&\mathbb{E}(\tau\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)+t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n^{2}+8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n\\
\leq&\mathbb{E}(\tau\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}))>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{U}=k)+16t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n+t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n^{2}.\end{split}

(6.44)

To control the term
\mathbb{E}(\tau\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\mathcal{U}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)
, we consider another modified CRW model where we ignore collisions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
in the time interval
[4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,12t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]
. Denote the collision time in this new model by
\tau^{*}
. It follows that
\tau\leq\tau^{*}
almost surely. Similar to (
6.43
), we have

\left|\mathbb{E}(\tau^{*}\mid 4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n<\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)-t_{\mathrm{meet}}\right|\leq t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n^{2}+12t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n.

It follows that

\mathbb{E}(\tau\mathbbm{1}[\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n]|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})>4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\\
\leq\left(12t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n+(1+1/n^{2})t_{\mathrm{meet}}\right)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\geq 4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)

(6.45)

The first part of Claim
2
implies that the quenched probability

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\geq 4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)

is bounded from below with high probability. This together with Lemma

2.13

implies that with high probability we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\leq 8t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n|\mathcal{C}(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})\geq 4t_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n,D_{\mathcal{U}}=k)\leq Ct_{\mathrm{rel}}\log n\left(n^{-4}+n^{-1}\right),

(6.46)

where
C
is some constant depending on
\bar{d}
.
Finally, we recall that
t_{\mathrm{rel}}
is bounded from above by some constant with high probability (Lemma
6.1
). Hence the second part of Claim
2
follows from (
6.44
), (
6.45
) and (
6.46
). This completes the proof of Claim
2
and hence also Lemma
6.12
.
∎

6.5
Proof of Theorem
1.6
and
1.8

We now prove Theorem
1.6
, using Proposition
5.2
and the bounds on the error terms we obtained in previous sections.

Suppose the degree distribution
D
is upper bounded by
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Let
\kappa_{0}
be defined as in Lemma
6.1
, and
\delta_{D}
be defined as in Lemma
6.4
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, let
\delta^{\prime}
be some number to be determined.
Take
\rho=\rho(\delta_{D},\kappa_{0},(2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})
as given by Proposition
6.10
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we call a graph
\mathcal{G}

good
, if

1.

|t_{\mathrm{meet}}/n-(2\alpha(D))^{-1}|\leq\delta^{\prime}
.

2.

\kappa(\mathcal{G})\geq\kappa_{0}
.

3.

\mathcal{G}
is
\delta_{D}
-homogeneous.

4.

\mathcal{G}
is
\rho
-locally tree like.

Note that when
\mathcal{G}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
, by
taking
n
large enough, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}\textrm{ is good})>1-\varepsilon
, by Theorem
3.1
, Lemmas
6.4
,
6.6
, and
6.12
. Now we take a good graph
\mathcal{G}
on
n
vertices.
By Lemma
2.9
, we have

\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{s}p_{s^{\prime}}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{s}p_{s^{\prime}}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}}+\max_{z}\int_{0}^{s}p_{s^{\prime}}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\leq C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}

(6.47)

for any
s\leq n
.

Now we verify the conditions in Proposition
5.2
.
We first check the second condition.
Since we assume
\lim_{n\to\infty}n/t_{n}=\infty
, we have
t_{n}\leq n/2
for
n
large enough.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is a good graph, by Theorem
3.1
,

\sup_{x\in V,0\leq s\leq t_{n}}P_{s}(x)\frac{ns}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq C_{0}\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\sup_{x\in V}\int_{0}^{t_{n}}p_{t^{\prime}}(x,x)\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\leq 4C_{0}C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\alpha(D),

if
\delta^{\prime}\leq 1/(4\alpha(D))
. Here
C_{0}
is the constant given in Theorem
3.1
. Hence if we take
K^{\prime}
to be
4C_{0}C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}}\alpha(D)
, the second condition
of Proposition
5.2

is satisfied. Given
\varepsilon
and
K^{\prime}
, we let
K
and
\delta
be given as in the statement of Proposition
5.2
(see also Remark
5.3
). We then set
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{\delta,1/\alpha(D)\}/4
and set the value of
\Delta
to be

\Delta:=K\log\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}r_{\max}}{n}+1\right)+\log R+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}},

where
R=r_{\max}/r_{\min}
.
We now check the first condition.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is a good graph and
R\leq\bar{d}
, we have

\Delta\leq K\log\left(((2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})\bar{d}+1\right)+\log(\bar{d})+(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}.

It follows from the definitions of
E_{1},E_{2}
and
E_{4}
as well as (
6.47
) that

E_{1}\leq\frac{1}{t_{n}}\frac{2\bar{d}}{\kappa_{D}^{2}}\left(((2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})\bar{d}\right)^{K}(2\left(\log(C_{\kappa_{0},\bar{d}})+K\log\left(((2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})\bar{d}+1\right)+\log(\bar{d})+(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\right),

E_{2}\leq\frac{t_{n}}{n}\left(((2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})\bar{d}\right)^{K}\bar{d}^{2},

and

E_{4}\leq\frac{1}{t_{n}}\left(((2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime})\bar{d}\right)^{K}.

For
E_{3}
, we apply Proposition
6.10
with
\delta_{1}=\delta_{D}
and
C_{\alpha}=(2\alpha(D))^{-1}+\delta^{\prime}
. Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}n/t_{n}=\infty
, we have
E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4}<\delta
for all
n
large enough.

Hence by Proposition
5.2
we have

|t_{n}P_{t_{n}}-\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}|<\varepsilon
, for any
n
large enough whenever
\mathcal{G}
is a good graph. Thus we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\{|t_{n}P_{t_{n}}-\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}|<\varepsilon\}\right)\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_{n}\mbox{ is good})\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Our conclusion follows since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary.
∎

We deduce Theorem
1.8
from Theorem
1.6
and the local weak convergence of the configuration model.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
and condition on
\mathcal{G}_{n}
being connected. By Lemma
B.4
and Proposition
B.5
we see
P_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{n})

converges to
\mathbb{E}_{\UGT(D)}P_{t}(o)
, where the subscript indicates the expectation is taken over the law of
\UGT(D)
.

Take any increasing and diverging sequence of times
t_{1},t_{2},\cdots
. By repeating each entry many times, we can construct another sequence of times
t_{1}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime},\cdots
containing the previous sequence
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots
as well as a sequence
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
; and for each large enough
n
,
|t_{n}^{\prime}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})-t_{n}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{UST}(D)}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(o)|<\varepsilon_{n}
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{n}
,
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}n/t_{n}^{\prime}=\infty
.
By Theorem
1.6
,
t_{n}^{\prime}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
converges to
\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}
in probability as
n\to\infty
.
Thus
t_{n}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\UGT(D)}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(o)
converges to
\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}
, as
n\to\infty
. Since
t_{n}
is a subsequence of
t_{n}^{\prime}
, we have that
t_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\UGT(D)}P_{t_{n}}(o)
converges to
\frac{1}{\alpha(D)}
as well.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

We have to repeat each entry many times because only in this way can we guarantee

|t_{n}^{\prime}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}_{n})-t_{n}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{UST}(D)}P_{t_{n}^{\prime}}(o)|
is small with high probability, since now we can treat
t_{n}^{\prime}
as almost being a constant and let
n
be as large as needed.

7
Mean field behavior for transitive Markov chains

In this section we will prove Theorems
1.9
,
1.11
and
1.12
. Recall that for transitive chains we always assume
r(x)=\sum_{y}r_{x,y}=1
. We also have
R=r_{\max}/r_{\min}=1
. We will need to refine the estimate of the integral of
h(t,\phi_{k})
.

Recall that we let
\mathcal{U}\sim\pi
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{U}}
, and that
W_{\mathcal{U}}
and
W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
are two random walks starting from
\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
respectively.
The key observation is that due to transitivity, the conditional probability

\mathbb{P}(W_{\mathcal{U}}(s)\neq W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(s),0\leq s\leq t\mid\mathcal{U})

(7.1)

is a constant independent of
\mathcal{U}
. Hereafter we denote this quantity by
q(t)
, which is also equal to
\frac{n}{2}f(t)
where
n
is the size of the chain and
f(t)
is the probability density function of the meeting time of two independent random walks starting from
\pi^{\otimes 2}

(see equation (
5.20
)).
Recall the definition of branching structures in Section
4.2
and the notation
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
where
0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t
. For convenience we let
t_{0}=0
and
t_{k+1}=t
.
From the definition of
q(t)
we get

\mathbb{P}\left(\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],\gamma_{k}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{k}}(t^{\prime})|\gamma_{\ell}(t_{k}),1\leq\ell\leq k-1\right)=q(t_{k+1}-t_{k}).

(7.2)

Hence
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
equals to

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\mathbbm{1}[\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})]\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}],\gamma_{k}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{k}}(t^{\prime})|\gamma_{\ell}(t_{k}),1\leq\ell\leq k-1\right)\right)\\
=&q(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\mathbbm{1}[\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})]\right)\\
=&q(t_{k+1}-t_{k})q(t_{k}-t_{k-1})\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-2}\mathbbm{1}[\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})]\right)\\
=&\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})=q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}).\end{split}

(7.3)

Recall the definition of
\alpha_{t}(x)
and
\alpha_{t}
in (
1.3
).
In the transitive setup we have

\alpha_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}\geq t)
since
r(x)=1,\forall x
.
We first give a result that relates
t_{\mathrm{meet}}
to
\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}\geq t)
, which is reminiscent of Lemma
6.12
in Section
6.4
. As a corollary, the two statements in Theorem
1.9
are equivalent.

Proposition 7.1
.

For any sequence of transitive Markov chains
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
satisfying

\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=0,

and for any sequence
t_{n}
satisfying
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\alpha_{t_{n}}}{n}=1.

Proposition
7.1
follows directly from equation (
2.9
) and
equation (
2.12
). Indeed, as we mentioned in the beginning of Section
7
, by transitivity, for all
x\in V
,

\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}>t)=\frac{n}{2}f(t).

Let
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}^{2})
be the relaxation time for the product chain of two independent copies of
(V,\mathbf{r})
. The definition of relaxation time implies that
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}^{2})=t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})
. Using (
2.12
) we get

\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})+t}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}>t)\leq\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r})}{2t}\right).

(7.4)

where we have used the definition
t_{\mathrm{meet}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\pi}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}})
. For a sequence of Markov chains
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
such that
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})+t_{n}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{2t_{n}}\right)=1.

Combining this with equation (
7.4
) we get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{2\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\tau_{\mathrm{meet}}\geq t_{n})t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{n}=1,

as desired.
∎

7.1
General transitive Markov chains

Let
(V_{n},\mathbf{r}_{n})
be a sequence of transitive chains
such that

t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/n
is bounded from above and
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
.
Let
t_{n}
be any sequence of times satisfying

t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
. We will show that the conditions in Proposition
5.2
hold for any
\varepsilon>0
when
n
is sufficiently large.

Recall the definition of
H(t)
and
G(t,\Delta)
in equation (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) respectively. Since the graph is transitive,
H(t)=1
and
G(t,\Delta)=t_{\mathrm{rel}}\Delta
. Also recall that
\boldsymbol{t}
is
\Delta
-good if
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>t_{\mathrm{rel}}\Delta
. Note by fact (
2.14
) we have
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\geq 1/2
(since
r_{\max}=1
) so

t_{n}\gg 1
.
Theorem
3.1
and equation (
2.19
)
together imply that there exists a universal constant
K^{\prime}
such that

\sup_{x\in V_{n}}P_{s}(x)\frac{ns}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\leq K^{\prime}\mbox{ for all }0\leq s\leq t_{n}.

Thus the second condition of Proposition
5.2
holds. To verify the first condition, we fix any
\varepsilon>0
, let
K>0

and
\delta
be given by the statement of Proposition
5.2
. By transitivity we have
e_{k}(t)=0
for all
k
and
t
, which gives
E_{3}=0
.
Since
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/n
is
bounded above,
\Delta
(defined in equation (
5.6
)) is also a bounded number as
n\to\infty
.
Hence for
n

large enough we have

E_{1}=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{n}\right)^{K}\Delta\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{t_{n}}\leq\frac{\delta}{3},

and

E_{4}=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{n}\right)^{K}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\leq\frac{\delta}{3},

since
t_{n}\gg t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
,
t_{n}\gg 1
and
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/n
stays bounded as
n\to\infty
. For
E_{2}
, we have

E_{2}=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{n}\right)^{K}\frac{t_{n}}{n}\leq\frac{\delta}{3},

since
t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
.
Thus for
n
large we have

E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4}\leq\frac{\delta}{3}+\frac{\delta}{3}+0+\frac{\delta}{3}\leq\delta.

Thus both conditions of Proposition
5.2
hold and consequently, for any
\varepsilon>0
, when
n
is large enough we have

\left|P_{t_{n}}-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{nt_{n}}\right|\leq\varepsilon\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{nt_{n}}.

(7.5)

This implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|\frac{nt_{n}}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}P_{t_{n}}-1\right|=0.

By Proposition
7.1
, we also get

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|t_{n}\alpha_{t_{n}}P_{t_{n}}-1\right|=0.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

7.2
Uniformly transient transitive Markov chains

For uniformly transient Markov chains we have
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
and
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\asymp n
(we include a proof of this fact in Proposition
2.14
). If
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
is bounded when
n
goes to infinity, then Theorem
1.9
already implies Theorem
1.11
. Thus we only need to consider the case when
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
goes to infinity. As in the proof of Theorem
1.9
we will establish a proposition giving conditions under which mean-field behavior occurs for transitive chains.
To this end we
first upper bound
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
(see Lemma
7.3
below). Then we control the integral of
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
using estimates for
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
.

In this section the ‘good’ set is simply defined to be
\{\boldsymbol{t}:\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta\}
(while previously we used
\{\boldsymbol{t}:\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>G(t,\Delta)=t_{\mathrm{rel}}\Delta\}
as our
\Delta
-good set). Recall the definition of the branching structure in Section
4.2
.
We now state a lemma that controls the meeting probability, from which we will deduce Lemma
7.3
.

Lemma 7.2
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
such that the following two statements hold for all transitive chains
(V,\mathbf{r})
with
\left|V\right|=n
.
For any
1<\Delta<t_{\mathrm{rel}}/4
and
t>\Delta
we have

\begin{split}\max_{x,y\in V}\mathbb{P}_{x,y}\left(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\Delta,t]\text{ such that }W_{x}(t^{\prime})=W_{y}(t^{\prime})\right)\leq C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right),\end{split}

(7.6)

where
W_{x},W_{y}
are two independent random walks starting from
x
and
y
and
z
is any element of
V
.
Moreover, suppose
\ell_{1}<i_{\ell_{2}}
, then we have

\begin{split}&\max_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}+1}]\text{ such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime})|\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t_{\ell_{2}-1})=x,\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t_{\ell_{2}-1})=y)\\
\leq&C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right).\end{split}

(7.7)

We start with the first statement. Note that
r(x)=1
for all
x
. Define
N(x,y,t)
to be the Lebesgue measure of the set

\{s\in[\Delta,t+1]:W_{x}(s)=W_{y}(s)\}.

Since
r(x)=1
for all
x
, we have that

\mathbb{E}(N(x,y,t)|W_{x}(s)=W_{y}(s)\mbox{ for some }s\in[\Delta,t])\geq\exp(-2).

This implies that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{x,y}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\Delta,t]\text{ such that }W_{x}(t^{\prime})=W_{y}(t^{\prime}))\\
\leq&\frac{\mathbb{E}(N(x,y,t))}{\mathbb{E}(N(x,y,t)|W_{x}(s)=W_{y}(s)\mbox{ for some }s\in[\Delta,t])}\\
\leq&\exp(2)\mathbb{E}(N(x,y,t))=\exp(2)\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}\left(\sum_{z^{\prime}}p_{s}(x,z^{\prime})p_{s}(y,z^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}s\\
=&\exp(2)\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}\left(\sum_{z^{\prime}}p_{s}(x,z^{\prime})p_{s}(z^{\prime},y)\right)\mathrm{d}s=\exp(2)\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}p_{2s}(x,y)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

It follows that

\begin{split}&\max_{x,y\in V}\mathbb{P}_{x,y}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\Delta,t]\text{ such that }W_{x}(t^{\prime})=W_{y}(t^{\prime}))\\
=&C\max_{x,y\in V}\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}p_{2s}(x,y)\mathrm{d}s\leq C\left(\max_{x,y\in V}\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}\left(p_{2s}(x,y)-\frac{1}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+1}{n}\right)\\
\leq&C\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lceil 2(t+1)/(t_{\mathrm{rel}}-2\Delta)\rceil}\max_{x,y\in V}\int_{\Delta+(\ell-1)(t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2-\Delta)}^{\Delta+\ell(t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2-\Delta)}\left(p_{2s}(x,y)-\frac{1}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+1}{n}\right).\end{split}

(7.8)

For transitive graphs,
p_{s}(x,x)
is independent of
x
. Thus using equation (
2.5
) and
t_{\mathrm{rel}}>4\Delta
, we get

\begin{split}\int_{\Delta+(\ell-1)(t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2-\Delta)}^{\Delta+\ell(t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2-\Delta)}\left(p_{2s}(x,y)-\frac{1}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq&\exp\left(-(\ell-1)\frac{t_{\mathrm{rel}}-2\Delta}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\int_{\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\\
\leq&\exp\left(-\frac{\ell-1}{2}\right)\int_{\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(7.9)

Summing
\ell
from 1 to infinity we have

\max_{x,y\in V}\mathbb{P}_{x,y}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\Delta,t]\text{ such that }W_{x}(t^{\prime})=W_{y}(t^{\prime}))\leq C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right),

which proves the first assertion of Lemma
7.2
(note that we assume
t>1
so that
t+1\leq 2t
). For the second assertion,
using equation (
5.15
) and the definition of the branching structure, the first line in equation (
7.7
) is bounded from above by

C\int_{\Delta}^{t+1}\sum_{z^{\prime}}p_{s}(x,z^{\prime})\hat{p}_{s}(y,z^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s

where we
recall that
\hat{p}_{\cdot}
is a transition probability related to
p_{\cdot}
defined in equation (
5.14
). Using equation (
5.15
) we have

C\int_{\Delta}^{t}\sum_{z^{\prime}}p_{s}(x,z^{\prime})\hat{p}_{s}(y,z^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s\leq C^{\prime}\int_{\Delta}^{t}\sum_{z^{\prime}}p_{s}(x,z^{\prime})p_{s+1}(y,z^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s=C^{\prime}\int_{2\Delta}^{t+1}p_{2s+1}(x,y)\mathrm{d}s,

(7.10)

which can be bounded by

C^{\prime\prime}\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right),

by repeating the proof of first assertion of Lemma
7.2
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.2
.
∎

Using this lemma we can give a refined upper bound for
g(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},\phi_{k})
for transitive chains.

Lemma 7.3
.

Consider coalescing random walks on a transitive Markov chain. There exists some constant
C_{k}
depending on
k
s.t.
for any
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}
and
\Delta>1
that satisfy
\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>t_{\mathrm{rel}}>4\Delta
we have

0\leq g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq C_{k}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{k}\prod_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i-1,i}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})\right)\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right).

(7.11)

Using equation (
5.12
) we get

\begin{split}0\leq&g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\\
\leq&\sum_{0\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq\ell_{3}\leq k}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[\{\forall 1\leq\ell\leq k\text{ and }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}]\text{ we have }\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\}\\
&\cap\{\exists t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{3}},t_{\ell_{3}+1}]\text{ such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\}]\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\times\left(\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}=i_{\ell_{2}}]+\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}\neq i_{\ell_{2}}\text{ and }\forall t^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}]\text{ we have }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})]\right)\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).\end{split}

(7.12)

Using equation (
7.1
) and
proceed in a similar fashion to (
7.3
), we see the summand on the right hand side is equal to (recall the equation of
q(t)
in (
7.2
))

\begin{split}&\prod_{\ell=\ell_{3}+1}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[\{\forall 1\leq\ell\leq\ell_{3}\mbox{ and }t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\mbox{ we have }\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\}\\
&\cap\{\exists t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{3}},t_{\ell_{3}+1}]\mbox{ such that }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\}]\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\times\left(\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}=i_{\ell_{2}}]+\mathbbm{1}[\ell_{1}\neq i_{\ell_{2}}\mbox{ and }\forall t^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{2}-1},t_{\ell_{2}}],\mbox{ we have }\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})]\right)\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).\end{split}

(7.13)

Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we consider two cases
\ell_{3}=\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{3}>\ell_{2}
separately. In the first case we must have
\ell_{1}\neq i_{\ell_{2}}
. Using the second statement in Lemma
7.2
we have the following bound for the conditional probability

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{3}-1},t_{\ell_{3}}],\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell_{2}}}(t^{\prime}),\exists t^{\prime\prime}\in[t_{\ell_{3}},t_{\ell_{3}+1}],\gamma_{\ell_{1}}(t^{\prime\prime})=\gamma_{\ell_{2}}(t^{\prime\prime})\right.\\
&\left.|\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime\prime}),0\leq\ell\leq\ell_{3}-1,t_{\ell}\leq t^{\prime\prime}\leq t_{\ell_{3}-1}\right)\leq C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right).\end{split}

Therefore, we can upper bound the quantity in (
7.13
) by

\begin{split}&C\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbbm{1}[\forall 1\leq\ell\leq\ell_{3}-1,t^{\prime}\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}],\gamma_{\ell}(t^{\prime})\neq\gamma_{i_{\ell}}(t^{\prime})\right)\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right)\prod_{\ell=\ell_{3}+1}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})\\
=&C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right)\prod_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{3}-2}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})\prod_{\ell=\ell_{3}+1}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}).\end{split}

In the second case
\ell_{3}>\ell_{2}
, we can also get an upper bound of

C\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right)\prod_{\ell=1,\ell\neq\ell_{3},\ell_{3}-1}^{k}q(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})

for a possibly different
C
.
Hence Lemma
7.3
follows by summing over all possible
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
.
∎

Now we estimate the integral of
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
. Fix any
\Delta\in(1,t_{\mathrm{rel}}/4)
. We split the domain of the integral into
\{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta\}
and

\{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\leq\Delta\}
.
On the set
\{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\leq\Delta\}
we simply use the bound
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\leq 1
since
r(x)=1
for all
x
. For the set

\{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta\}
, Lemma
7.3
implies that

0\leq\int_{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta}(g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\leq C_{k}\left(\int_{0}^{t}q(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)^{k-2}t^{2}\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right).

(7.14)

By definition of
q(t)
, for any
\Delta\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}
, we have

\begin{split}0\leq q(t_{1})-q(t_{2})&\leq\mathbb{P}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[t_{1},t_{2}],W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})=W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime}))\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\min\{t_{1},t_{\mathrm{rel}}/4\},t_{2}],W_{\mathcal{U}}(t^{\prime})=W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}(t^{\prime}))\\
&\leq C\left(\int_{\min\{2t_{1},t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2\}}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t_{2}}{n}\right),\end{split}

(7.15)

where
(W_{\mathcal{U}},W_{\tilde{\mathcal{U}}})
is a pair of independent random walks starting from
(\mathcal{U},\nu_{\mathcal{U}})
and we have used equation (
7.6
) in the last inequality
(with the
\Delta
there replaced by
\min\{2t_{1},t_{\mathrm{rel}}/2\}
).
Now using (
5.20
) and (
5.21
),

\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1-\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)\leq q(\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}})\leq\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}\right).

(7.16)

Combining equations (
7.15
) and (
7.16
), we see that for all
\Delta\leq s\leq t
,

\left|q(s)-\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|\leq C\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right).

(7.17)

Using (
7.17
) together with facts
q(s)\leq 1
for all
s

and
n/(2t_{\mathrm{meet}})\leq 2
(by equation (
2.15
)), we get

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{0}^{t}q(s)\mathrm{d}s-\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|\leq\int_{0}^{\Delta}q(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\Delta n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}+\left|\int_{\Delta}^{t}q(s)\mathrm{d}s-\frac{n(t-\Delta)}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right|\\
\leq&3\Delta+Ct\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right).\end{split}

(7.18)

It follows from (
7.14
) – (
7.18
) that

\begin{split}0\leq&\int_{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta}(g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\leq Ct^{2}\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right)\\
&\times\left(\Delta+t\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right)\right)^{k-2}.\end{split}

(7.19)

It remains to control the integral of
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})(=q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}))
.
Using equation (
7.17
) together with the facts that
q(t,I_{k})\leq 1
and
n/(2t_{\mathrm{meet}})\leq 2
we have

\begin{split}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|=&\left|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}f(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|\\
\leq&C_{k}\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right).\end{split}

(7.20)

We can now ensemble above estimates to prove the following proposition, which is an analogue of Proposition
5.2
for transitive chains.

Proposition 7.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
K^{\prime}>0
, there exists some
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\delta>0
, such that the following is true for all
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
.
Take any transitive chain
(V,\mathbf{r})
, with
n=|V|
. Take

\Delta=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}+1\right)^{K}+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}.

(7.21)

Define

\begin{split}E_{1}&=\frac{\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\right)^{K},\quad E_{2}=\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\right)^{K}\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s,\\
E_{3}&=\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\right)^{K},\quad E_{4}=\frac{\Delta}{t}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{n}\right)^{K}.\end{split}

(7.22)

If the following three conditions hold

1.

\Delta<t_{\mathrm{rel}}/4
,

2.

E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4}\leq\delta
,

3.

P_{s}\frac{ns}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\leq K^{\prime}
for
0\leq s\leq t
,

then we have

\left|P_{t}-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}\right|\leq\varepsilon\frac{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}{nt}.

(7.23)

Using (
7.19
), (
7.20
) and the fact
n/t_{\mathrm{meet}}\leq 4
we have
(recall
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}=\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}):0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}<t\}
)

\begin{split}&\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}-\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|\\
\leq&\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}-\left(\frac{nt}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|+\left|\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}(h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k}))\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\right|\\
\leq&\int_{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta}\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}+\int_{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})\leq\Delta}\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\\
&+k!\left(\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)^{k}\Delta t^{k-1}+\int_{\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta}\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\left|h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}\\
\leq&C_{k}\left[t^{k}\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right)+\Delta t^{k-1}\right.\\
&\left.+\left(\Delta+t\left(\frac{n}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}+\frac{(2+\Delta)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}}\right)+\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t+\Delta t_{\mathrm{rel}}}{n}\right)\right)^{k-2}\right.\\
&\times\left.t^{2}\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}\right)\right].\end{split}

(7.24)

Using this one can get a bound for
\left|\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})-(k+1)!(\frac{nt}{t_{\mathrm{meet}}})^{k}\right|
(similar to the proof of Lemma
5.6
). The proof of Proposition
7.4
follows from the same argument as the proof of Proposition
5.2
via Lemma
5.6
. We omit the details here.
∎

As before we will show that the conditions in Theorem
1.11
imply conditions of Proposition
7.4
for any fixed
\varepsilon
and large
n
. Let
\delta
be given by Proposition
7.4
. Recall that we only need to deal with the case where
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. The third condition in Proposition
7.4
is satisfied, as we showed in proof of Theorem
1.9
. The first condition also holds for large
n
since
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/n
remains upper bounded (by uniform transience) and
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\to\infty
. For the second condition, we note

•

E_{1}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}
for large
n
since
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
;

•

E_{2}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}
for small
\delta^{\prime}
and large
n
by the definition of uniform transience;

•

E_{3}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}
for large
n
since we have
t_{n}\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
and
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\ll t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})
(by uniform transience);

•

E_{4}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}
for large
n
since
t_{n}
goes to
\infty
.

We also need to use the fact that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})/n
stays bounded to control
E_{2},E_{3}
and
E_{4}
.
Hence all conditions of Proposition
7.4
are satisfied for any
\varepsilon>0
by first picking a sufficiently small
\delta^{\prime}
and then making
n
large enough.
Thus we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{nt_{n}}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\left|P_{t_{n}}-\frac{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}{nt_{n}}\right|=0,

as desired. To prove the second statement of Theorem
1.11
, it suffices to show

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|\alpha_{t_{n}}-\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\right|=0.

For a fixed
\Delta
, using equation (
7.17
) we have (recall
\alpha_{t_{n}}=q(t_{n})
by the definition of
q(t)
)

\begin{split}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left|q(t_{n})-\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\right|\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\right).\end{split}

(7.25)

Since the l.h.s. of equation (
7.25
) is independent of
\Delta
, we can send
\Delta
to infinity to get

\begin{split}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left|q(t_{n})-\frac{n}{2t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}\right|\leq\limsup_{\Delta\to\infty}C\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{2\Delta}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\right)=0,\end{split}

by the definition of uniform transience. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.11
.
∎

7.3
Infinite transient transitive unimodular graphs

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.12
.
We first recall
[
15
, Lemma 2.1]
, which shows that Lemma
2.1
holds true for unimodular graph as well, provided that we define
N_{t}
be the number of walkers that coalesced with the walker starting from
o
, the root of the unimdular graph, in the sense of the graphical representation of CRW. The strategy to control
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1})
is similar to the framework for finite graphs. As in finite graphs, we
write

\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1})=\mathbb{E}(N_{t})^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}{N_{t}}\right).

We estimate
\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1})
in two steps. We first show the convergence of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}(N_{t})
to a Gamma distribution with density function
4xe^{-2x}\mathbbm{1}[x\geq 0]
using the method of moments. We will also determine the leading order of
\mathbb{E}(N_{t})
in this step. We then show the contribution to
\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(N_{t})/N_{t})
when
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}(N_{t})
is near
0
is small. Once these two steps are completed Theorem
1.12
follows immediately.

We let
\mathbf{X}(t):=(X_{0}(t),\ldots,X_{k}(t))
where
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{k-1},X_{k}
be
k+1
coalescing random walks. Then we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})&=\sum_{o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}\in V}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\leq t\mid\mathbf{X}(0)=(o,o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}))\\
&=\sum_{o^{\prime},o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}\in V}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\leq t,X_{0}(t)=o^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{X}(0)=(o,o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}))\\
&=\sum_{o^{\prime},o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}\in V}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\leq t,X_{0}(t)=o\mid\mathbf{X}(0)=(o^{\prime},o_{1},\ldots,o_{k})),\end{split}

(7.26)

where in the last equality we have applied mass transport principle to the diagonally invariant function
F(x,y)
defined by

F(x,y):=\sum_{o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}\in V}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\leq t,X_{0}(t)=y\mid\mathbf{X}(0)=(x,o_{1},\ldots,o_{k})).

Here
F(x,y)
being ‘diagonally invariant’ means
F(x,y)=F(\phi(x),\phi(y))
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
and
\phi
in the set of automorphism group of
\mathcal{G}
. See
[
22
, Chapter 8]

for more details about mass transport principle.
By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.1
we get

\begin{split}&\sum_{o^{\prime},o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}\in\mathcal{G}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{k})\leq t,X_{0}(t)=o\mid\mathbf{X}(0)=(o^{\prime},o_{1},\ldots,o_{k}))\\
&=(k+1)!\sum_{I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<,t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}.\end{split}

(7.27)

Here
h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
is define using equation (
4.4
) with
\gamma_{0}(0)=o
. Similarly we define
g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
and
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
using (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), respectively.
As in the proof of Theorem
1.11
we let the ‘good’ set be
\{\boldsymbol{t}:\Delta_{\min}(\boldsymbol{t})>\Delta\}
. On the good set, using the method of the proof of Lemma
5.4
, the difference
\left|g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\right|
can be upper bounded by

C_{k}\int_{2\Delta}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s
.
Let
X(s),s\geq 0
and
Y(s),s\geq 0
be two independent random walks.
We have the bound

\left|q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})-\mathbb{P}_{o,\nu_{o}}(X(s)\neq Y(s),\forall s\geq 0)^{k}\right|\leq k\mathbb{P}_{x,\nu_{x}}(\exists s\geq\Delta,X(s)=Y(s))\leq Ck\int_{2\Delta}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s

on the good set. It follows that (note that
q(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})=g(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})
for transitive chains)

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{<.t}}h(\boldsymbol{t},I_{k})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}-\mathbb{P}_{o,\nu_{o}}(X(s)\neq Y(s),\forall s\geq 0)^{k}t^{k}/k!\right|\leq C_{k}\left(\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{\infty}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s\right)t^{k}+\Delta t^{k-1}\right).

Summing over all possible
I_{k}\in\Phi_{k}
and using
Lemma
4.3

we get, for
\Delta,t\geq 1
and
n
large enough,

\left|\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})-\mathbb{P}_{o,\nu_{o}}(X(s)\neq Y(s),\forall s\geq 0)^{k}t^{k}(k+1)!\right|\leq C_{k}\left(\left(\int_{2\Delta}^{\infty}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s\right)t^{k}+\Delta t^{k-1}\right).

(7.28)

The transience of
\mathcal{G}
implies

\int_{2\Delta}^{\infty}p_{s}(o,o)\mathrm{d}s
converges to 0 as
\Delta
goes to infinity.
For any fixed
K
and
\varepsilon
, when
t
is large enough, using (
7.28
) we get

\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{k})}{(\mathbb{E}(N_{t}))^{k}}-(k+1)!/2^{k}\right|\leq\varepsilon,

(7.29)

for
1\leq k\leq K
. This implies the convergence of
N_{t}/\mathbb{E}(N_{t})
to the Gamma distribution with density
4xe^{-2x}\mathbbm{1}[x\geq 0]
. It remains to show

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}{N_{t}};\frac{N_{t}}{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}\leq\delta\right)=0.

This can be proven directly by using
[
7
, Lemma 2]
, where we set the
f_{t}
there to be
t
. Then
f_{t}P_{t}
is uniformly bounded over
t
by Theorem
3.1
and thus the condition of that lemma is satisfied.
Combining the above two steps we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}tP_{t}(\mathcal{G})=\lim_{t\to\infty}t\mathbb{E}(N_{t}^{-1})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{2t}{\mathbb{E}(N_{t})}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{o,\nu_{o}}(X(s)\neq Y(s),\forall s\geq 0)}.

This completes the proof of Theorem

1.12
.

A
Classical results on Markov chains

We provide proofs to some classical results on Markov chains from Section
2.2
.

A.1
The Poincaré inequality

Note that now the stationary distribution
\pi
is uniform.
Equation (
2.4
) is a direct consequence of equation (
2.3
) by noting that
\sum_{x}\left(\mu_{t}(x)-\frac{1}{|V|}\right)^{2}=|V|\Var(p_{t}\mu)
. To prove equation (
2.5
),
we first note that by Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and symmetry of
p_{t/2}
, we have

p_{t}(x,y)^{2}=\left(\sum_{z}p_{t/2}(x,z)p_{t/2}(z,y)\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{z}p_{t/2}(x,z)^{2}\sum_{z}p_{t/2}(y,z)^{2}=p_{t}(x,x)p_{t}(y,y),

and hence
\max_{x,y}p_{t}(x,y)=\max_{x}p_{t}(x,x)
for all
t\geq 0
.
Also, if
\mu
is the Dirac measure at state
x
, then by the symmetry of
p_{t/2}
,

p_{t}(x,x)=\sum_{y}p_{t/2}(x,y)p_{t/2}(y,x)=\sum_{y}\mu_{t/2}(y)^{2}=\left(\sum_{y}\left(\mu_{t/2}(y)-\frac{1}{|V|}\right)\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{|V|}.

Thus (
2.5
) follows from (
2.4
). ∎

A.2
Mixing time and relaxation time

Assume
n=\left|V\right|
.
Since
p_{s}(x,x)\geq\exp(-\bar{d}s)
, we see for
t\leq 1/\bar{d}

\min_{x}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\geq t\exp(-1),

while

\max_{x}\int_{0}^{t}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\leq t.

Hence it suffices to prove that for some constant
C
depending on
\kappa_{0}
and
\bar{d}
, we have

\max_{x}\int_{0}^{n}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\leq C.

for all
x\in V
.
Using equation (
2.5
), we see that

p_{s}(x,x)-\frac{1}{n}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right).

It follows that

\int_{0}^{n}\left(p_{s}(x,x)-\frac{1}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{s}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq t_{\mathrm{rel}}.

Hence we have

\int_{0}^{n}p_{s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s\leq 1+t_{\mathrm{rel}}\leq 1+\frac{2\bar{d}}{\kappa_{0}^{2}},

as desired.
∎

A.3
Relations between Markov chain parameters

To prove equation (
2.14
), we see by definition
t_{\mathrm{rel}}=1/\lambda
where
-\lambda
is the largest non-zero eigenvalue of the
Q
-matrix associated with the transition rate
\mathbf{r}
(i.e.,
Q_{ij}=r_{i,j}
for all
i\neq j
and
Q_{i,i}=-r(i)
for all
i
).
One can show the matrix
Q+2r_{\max}I
is positive definite (
I
is the identity matrix), implying
-\lambda\geq-2r_{\max}
. Hence
t_{\mathrm{rel}}\geq 1/(2r_{\max})
.
On the other hand, equation (
2.15
) follows directly from
Lemma 2 in
[
1
]
applied to the product chain of two independent Markov chains by considering the diagonal set.
Finally equation (
2.16
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality

\begin{split}p_{s}(x,y)&=\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(x,z)p_{s/2}(z,y)\leq\sqrt{\left(\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(x,z)^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(z,y)^{2}\right)}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(x,z)^{2}\right)+\left(\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(z,y)^{2}\right)\right)\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(x,z)^{2}=\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(x,z)p_{s/2}(z,x)=p_{s}(x,x),

\displaystyle\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(z,y)^{2}=\sum_{z}p_{s/2}(y,z)p_{s/2}(z,y)=p_{s}(y,y),

owing to the fact that
\mathbf{r}
is symmetric (so
p
is also symmetric).
∎

A.4
Bounds for meeting probability

Denote by
q(x,y)
the probability in question.
Denote by
N_{1}(t,t+T,x,y)
the time two independent random walks that starts from
x
and
y
spend together between time
t
and
t+T
and by
N_{2}(t,z)
the time two random walks both starting from
z
spend together between time 0 and time
t
.
It follows that

q(x,y)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(N_{1}(t,t+T,x,y))}{\mathbb{E}(N_{1}(T,t+T,x,y)\mid\mbox{a collision happens between }T\mbox{ and }t)}.

(A.1)

Note that the denominator is bounded below by

\min_{z}\mathbb{E}(N_{2}(z,t))\geq\min_{z}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{w}p_{s}(z,w)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\min_{z}\int_{0}^{T}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s.

The numerator is bounded above by

\int_{t}^{t+T}\sum_{w}p_{s}(x,w)p_{s}(y,w)\mathrm{d}s\leq\max_{z,w}\int_{t}^{t+T}p_{2s}(z,w)\mathrm{d}s.

The inequality (
2.5
) implies

\max_{z,w}\int_{0}^{t+T}p_{2s}(z,w)\mathrm{d}s\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil t/T\rceil}\exp\left(-\frac{iT}{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}\right)\right)\max_{x}\int_{0}^{T}p_{2s}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{n}.

We claim that

\min_{z}\int_{0}^{T}p_{2s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{1}{8}\left(T\wedge\frac{1}{r_{\max}}\right).

(A.2)

To see that this is true, note
\min_{z}p_{2s}(z,z)\geq\exp(-2sr_{\max})
. If
T<1/r_{\max}
then
\min_{z}p_{2s}(z,z)
will be be at least
e^{-2}
for all
0\leq s\leq T
and hence
the integral will be at least
T/8
.

It follows that
q(x,y)
is bounded above by

\frac{\exp(-T/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}{1-\exp(-T/t_{\mathrm{rel}})}\frac{\max_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}{\min_{z}\int_{0}^{2T}p_{s}(z,z)\mathrm{d}s}+\frac{8t}{n}\left(\frac{1}{T}\vee r_{\max}\right).

Since
q(x,y)
is trivially bounded above by
1
we arrive at the form presented in the statement of Lemma
2.13
.
∎

A.5
Uniformly transient Markov chains

Without loss of generality we may assume
\left|V_{n}\right|=n
.
We first prove
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=o(t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n}))
by contradiction. Suppose
t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\geq cn
for some constant
c>0
and all
n
.
Using the spectral decomposition (see, e.g.,
[
21
, Section 12.1]
) and the fact that the stationary measure is the uniform measure in our case, we see that

p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)\geq 1/n

for any
t\geq 0
.
Using this
and the fact
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\geq n/4
(by (
2.15
)), we see that for any fixed
s
and
n>s/c
,

\max_{x,y\in V_{n}}\int_{s\wedge t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,y)\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{s}^{cn}\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{cn-s}{n}.

(A.3)

Sending
n\to\infty
and then
s\to\infty
, we get

\lim_{s\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\max_{x,y\in V_{n}}\int_{s\wedge t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})}p_{t}^{(n)}(x,y)\mathrm{d}t\geq\lim_{s\to\infty}c>0,

which contradicts the definition of uniform transience.
To prove
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=\Theta(n)
it suffices to show
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=O(n)
since the other direction is true by (
2.15
).
Again by using the spectral decomposition we see for all
x
and all
t,s\geq 0

0\leq p^{(n)}_{t+s}(x,x)-\pi(x)\leq\exp(-s/t_{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbf{r}_{n}))(p_{t}^{(n)}(x,x)-\pi(x)).

This implies for any
j\geq 0
,

0\leq\int_{(j+1)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}^{(j+2)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t\leq e^{-1}\int_{jt_{\mathrm{rel}}}^{(j+1)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t.

It follows that

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\int_{jt_{\mathrm{rel}}}^{(j+1)t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t\\
\leq&\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-j}\right)\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{e}{e-1}\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

(A.4)

The first line of (
A.4
) is equal to

\pi(x)\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(T_{x})
by
[
21
, Proposition 10.26]
.
On the other hand the
condition of uniform transience implies the existence of a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{x\in V_{n}}\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{rel}}}(p^{(n)}_{t}(x,x)-\pi(x))\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

(A.5)

It follows from (
A.4
), (
A.5
) and the inequality
t_{\mathrm{hit}}\leq 2\max_{x\in V_{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(T_{x})
that

t_{\mathrm{hit}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})\leq\frac{2Ce}{e-1}n

since
\pi(x)=1/n
in our case. Since
t_{\mathrm{meet}}=t_{\mathrm{hit}}/2
for transitive chains, we conclude that
t_{\mathrm{meet}}(\mathbf{r}_{n})=O(n)
, as desired.
∎

B
Local weak convergence of random graphs

To explain the notion of local weak convergence we need a few definitions.

Definition B.1
.

For any graph
\mathcal{G}
,
v\in V
, and
\rho\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, let
N_{\rho}(v,\mathcal{G})
be the subgraph induced by
\{u:d(u,v)\leq\rho\}
, where
d
is the graph distance.

Definition B.2
.

We consider the space
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
of all rooted graphs.
We make it a metric space: for any
(\mathcal{G}_{1},x_{1}),(\mathcal{G}_{2},x_{2})\in\mathfrak{G}_{*}
,
their distance is defined to be

d_{*}((\mathcal{G}_{1},x_{1}),(\mathcal{G}_{2},x_{2})):=(1+\rho_{*})^{-1}

where
\rho^{*}
is the maximum integer such that

N_{\rho^{*}}(x_{1},\mathcal{G}_{1})
is isomorphic to
N_{\rho^{*}}(x_{2},\mathcal{G}_{2})
.

Definition B.3
.

For a sequence of finite (possibly random) graphs
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, make each of them a random element in the space
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
, by choosing a root
\mathcal{U}_{n}
uniformly among all vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
.
The
local weak limit
of
(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
is defined as the weak limit of these random elements
(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
in
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
, under the topology given by the metric
d_{*}
. Equivalently, we say
(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
converges to
(\mathcal{G},o)
in the local weak sense if
for any function
\psi:\mathfrak{G}_{*}\to\mathbb{R}
that is continuous under the metric
d_{*}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(\psi(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n}))\mbox{ converges to }\mathbb{E}(\psi(\mathcal{G},o))\mbox{ in probability},

where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(\psi(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n}))
indicates the expectation is only taken w.r.t. the randomness of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
and
\mathbb{E}(\psi(\mathcal{G},o))
is the expectation of
\psi(\mathcal{G},o)
taken w.r.t. the law of
(\mathcal{G},o)
in
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
.

Let
\UGT(D)
be the unidomular Galton-Watson tree as introduced before Theorem
1.6
.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}\sim\CM_{n}(D)
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be a uniformly chosen vertex of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{G}\sim\UGT(D)
and
o
be the root of
\mathcal{G}
. Then
(\mathcal{G},o)
is the local weak limit of

(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})

A proof of this fact can be found in, e.g.,
[
32
, Theorem 2.11]
.
The following proposition gives a connection between CRW on finite and infinite graphs.

Proposition B.5
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
is a sequence of random rooted graphs with uniformly bounded degree
that converges in the local weak sense to
(\mathcal{G},o)
. Consider CRW on
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\mathcal{G}
with unit edge rate.
Then for any fixed finite time
t
,

P_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
converges in distribution to
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{G}}(P_{t}(o))
,
where
P_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is the density of CRW in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{G}}(P_{t}(o))
is the expectation of
P_{t}(o)
under the law of
(\mathcal{G},o)
.

Using the duality between the voter model and CRW, it is equivalent to proving the convergence of the survival probability of the voter model.
For
o\in\mathcal{G}
, we let
\psi(\mathcal{G},o)
be the probability that the initial opinion of
o
survives to time
t
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(\zeta^{o}_{t}\neq\eset)
and similarly define
\psi(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
. Then
P_{t}(\mathcal{G}_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(\psi(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n}))
and
P_{t}(o)=\psi(\mathcal{G},o)
. We also define
\psi_{\rho}(\mathcal{G},o)
to be

\mathbb{P}(\zeta^{o}_{t}\neq\eset,\zeta^{o}_{s}\cap N_{\rho}(o)^{c}=\eset,\forall 0\leq s\leq t)
and similarly define

\psi_{\rho}(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})
. Then
\psi_{\rho}
is a continuous function in the space
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
under the metric
d_{*}
.
Due to that the degree of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
is uniformly bounded, the quantity
\left|\psi_{\rho}(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})-\psi(\mathcal{G}_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})\right|
converges to
0
as
\rho\to\infty
, uniformly in
n
. Similarly
\left|\psi_{\rho}(\mathcal{G},o)-\psi(\mathcal{G},o)\right|
also converges to
0
. Proposition
B.5
now follows from applying the definition of local weak convergence to the function
\psi_{\rho}
and then sending
\rho\to\infty
.
∎
